# Daily Summary: 2025-06-12

### Learning to Reason Across Parallel Samples for LLM Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09014v1)
- **Authors**: Jianing Qi, Xi Ye, Hao Tang, Zhigang Zhu, Eunsol Choi
- **Abstract**: Scaling test-time compute brings substantial performance gains for large language models (LLMs). By sampling multiple answers and heuristically aggregate their answers (e.g., either through majority voting or using verifiers to rank the answers), one can achieve consistent performance gains in math domains. In this paper, we propose a new way to leverage such multiple sample set. We train a compact LLM, called Sample Set Aggregator (SSA), that takes a concatenated sequence of multiple samples and output the final answer, optimizing it for the answer accuracy with reinforcement learning. Experiments on multiple reasoning datasets show that SSA outperforms other test-time scaling methods such as reward model-based re-ranking. Our approach also shows a promising generalization ability, across sample set sizes, base model families and scales, and tasks. By separating LLMs to generate answers and LLMs to analyze and aggregate sampled answers, our approach can work with the outputs from premier black box models easily and efficiently.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Learning to Reason Across Parallel Samples for LLM Reasoning":

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel approach to test-time scaling for large language model (LLM) reasoning called the Sample Set Aggregator (SSA). Unlike existing parallel scaling methods that aggregate multiple independently generated answers via majority voting or verifier-based reranking, SSA trains a compact LLM to directly consume a concatenated sequence of multiple sampled answers and output the final answer.  SSA is optimized via reinforcement learning (RL) to maximize answer accuracy.  The authors demonstrate that SSA outperforms other test-time scaling methods, including reward model-based reranking, across various reasoning datasets, model families, and scales. A key benefit is that SSA separates the answer generation from the analysis, making it easy to use with black-box LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its hybrid approach, combining parallel sampling with a sequential aggregation mechanism trained with RL. This contrasts with purely parallel methods that treat samples independently and purely sequential methods that iteratively refine a single solution. The idea of training a lightweight model to "reason over" multiple generations, effectively learning to interpret the output distribution of a larger, potentially black-box, model is also novel.

*   **Significance:** The paper offers several potentially significant benefits. Firstly, the SSA approach achieves strong performance gains with a relatively small model, suggesting a more efficient way to leverage test-time compute compared to simply scaling up the base LLM. Secondly, its ability to work with black-box LLMs makes it highly practical. Separating the inference and analysis with another small and trainable model make it scalable, efficient and adaptive, allowing users to use a cheap trainable model for many different black-box models to aggregate and improve. Thirdly, the demonstrated generalization across datasets, models, and tasks hints at the robustness and wider applicability of the SSA.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper presents a clear and well-motivated approach to test-time scaling.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate consistent and substantial improvements over strong baselines across diverse reasoning tasks and model scales.
    *   The modularity of SSA, allowing it to work with black-box LLMs, is a significant advantage.
    *   The ablation studies and analysis provide valuable insights into the approach, such as the surprising result that shorter "thinking" chains do not degrade performance.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper demonstrates impressive results, the gains on truthfulQA were limited, suggesting SSA's approach may not be suited for all types of tasks and may require adaption.
    *   The current implementation limits scalability of *k* to low number due to the context size constraints. The impact and practicality of the SSA approach with significantly large *k* remains to be seen.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more detailed analysis of *why* SSA works.  The authors speculate on its ability to interpret the output distribution, but further investigation could strengthen this claim.
    *   A comparison of end-to-end (training and inference) wall-clock time with other parallel scaling approaches, demonstrating the actual speedups obtained, is missing (only inference is given).
    *   More insights on hyperparameters of RL could improve the study.
    *   The number of ablations (e.g., varying the architectures) is limited.
    *   It is not completely clear that the results for MMLU-Pro and ARC are statistically significant since they're close to the majority baseline.

*   **Potential Influence:** The SSA approach could influence future research on test-time scaling and LLM reasoning by encouraging the exploration of hybrid approaches that combine parallel sampling with learned aggregation mechanisms. Its modular design has potential in the industry since can easily be adapted with different models and data.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:** The paper presents a novel and promising approach to test-time scaling for LLMs with demonstrable performance gains. The practicality of working with black-box LLMs and the potential for efficiency improvements make it a significant contribution. However, the limitations on context length/large *k* values, lack of complete understanding on *why* it works, some not thoroughly studied generalization experiments and missing comparison of wall clock time prevent it from achieving a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### SPEED-RL: Faster Training of Reasoning Models via Online Curriculum Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09016v1)
- **Authors**: Ruiqi Zhang, Daman Arora, Song Mei, Andrea Zanette
- **Abstract**: Training large language models with reinforcement learning (RL) against verifiable rewards significantly enhances their reasoning abilities, yet remains computationally expensive due to inefficient uniform prompt sampling. We introduce Selective Prompting with Efficient Estimation of Difficulty (SPEED), an adaptive online RL curriculum that selectively chooses training examples of intermediate difficulty to maximize learning efficiency. Theoretically, we establish that intermediate-difficulty prompts improve the gradient estimator's signal-to-noise ratio, accelerating convergence. Empirically, our efficient implementation leads to 2x to 6x faster training without degrading accuracy, requires no manual tuning, and integrates seamlessly into standard RL algorithms.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SPEED-RL, a novel online curriculum learning approach to accelerate the training of large language models (LLMs) for reasoning tasks using reinforcement learning (RL). SPEED-RL adaptively selects training prompts based on real-time estimates of their difficulty, aiming to maximize learning efficiency. The key idea is to prioritize prompts of intermediate difficulty, theoretically justified by showing that prompts with pass rates near 0% or 100% provide limited learning signals due to a low signal-to-noise ratio (SNR). The method employs a two-phase inference strategy to reduce computational overhead: a screening phase quickly estimates prompt difficulty followed by a continuation phase that generates more responses for qualified prompts. The authors demonstrate significant wall-clock speedups (2x to 6x) compared to standard RL algorithms on mathematical reasoning benchmarks, without sacrificing accuracy and requiring no manual tuning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several aspects:

*   **Theoretical justification for online curriculum learning:** While the concept of curriculum learning isn't new, the paper provides a rigorous theoretical connection between prompt difficulty (pass rate) and the SNR of gradient estimators in RL, justifying the selection of intermediate-difficulty prompts. This goes beyond merely stating an intuition and provides a formal basis.
*   **Efficient implementation for LLM reasoning:**  Prior attempts at online curriculum learning in this domain involved full inference to determine pass rates, which is computationally expensive. The authors introduce a practical two-phase inference strategy coupled with a pre-fetching mechanism that significantly reduces the overhead, making the approach viable. This addresses a key bottleneck in applying curriculum learning to LLMs.
*   **Seamless integration with existing RL algorithms:** The method is designed to be algorithm-agnostic, integrating readily with commonly used RL algorithms such as RLOO, GRPO and PPO, and many tasks with binary-verifiable rewards, making it easily adoptable by the community.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a significant challenge in training LLMs for reasoning tasks: the high computational cost of RL fine-tuning. By demonstrating substantial speedups without compromising accuracy, SPEED-RL has the potential to make RL-based reasoning training more accessible and practical. The algorithm agnostic nature increases the potential influence on the community.

**Strengths:**

*   **Strong theoretical grounding:** The SNR analysis provides a solid foundation for the proposed method.
*   **Practical implementation:** The two-phase inference strategy and pre-fetching mechanism are crucial for realizing the benefits of curriculum learning in the LLM domain, where inference costs dominate.
*   **Comprehensive experimental evaluation:** The experiments cover multiple datasets, models, and RL algorithms, providing strong evidence for the effectiveness of SPEED-RL.
*   **Algorithm is easy to adopt** Designed to be seamlessly integrated to existing RL algorithms.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Exploration of Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** While the paper mentions the effect of `Ninit`, further investigation into the sensitivity of performance to hyperparameters could be valuable.
*   **Ablation Studies:** It would be interesting to have ablation studies evaluating the impact of individual components (e.g., the pre-fetching mechanism) to quantify their contributions to the overall speedup.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has a good chance of influencing the field by:

*   **Encouraging further research on online curriculum learning for LLMs:** The work demonstrates the potential of adaptive sampling strategies in the LLM domain and sets a direction for other researchers to follow.
*   **Providing a practical and efficient tool for RL fine-tuning:** SPEED-RL can be directly used by practitioners to accelerate the training of LLMs for reasoning tasks.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

The rigorousness of the mathematical derivations adds significant weight to the claims, as does the comprehensive nature of the experimental work presented. The detailed analysis of SNR and its correlation with empirical performance lends further credence to the findings. The implementation details are also reasonably well articulated, which could benefit other researchers trying to replicate this approach.

**Score: 8**

*   The paper presents a novel and theoretically well-founded approach to online curriculum learning for accelerating RL fine-tuning of LLMs for reasoning. The practical implementation aspects are also well considered and evaluated empirically. While not a complete paradigm shift (as curriculum learning has been explored before), it presents a significant and valuable improvement in the efficiency of RL fine-tuning, specifically tailored to LLMs for reasoning. The comprehensive experimental validation, strong theoretical foundation, and open source codebase all point toward positive real world impact and adoption within the community. It addresses a critical problem, and is likely to lead to further work. The weaknesses cited are also potential directions for further exploration.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Diffuse and Disperse: Image Generation with Representation Regularization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09027v1)
- **Authors**: Runqian Wang, Kaiming He
- **Abstract**: The development of diffusion-based generative models over the past decade has largely proceeded independently of progress in representation learning. These diffusion models typically rely on regression-based objectives and generally lack explicit regularization. In this work, we propose \textit{Dispersive Loss}, a simple plug-and-play regularizer that effectively improves diffusion-based generative models. Our loss function encourages internal representations to disperse in the hidden space, analogous to contrastive self-supervised learning, with the key distinction that it requires no positive sample pairs and therefore does not interfere with the sampling process used for regression. Compared to the recent method of representation alignment (REPA), our approach is self-contained and minimalist, requiring no pre-training, no additional parameters, and no external data. We evaluate Dispersive Loss on the ImageNet dataset across a range of models and report consistent improvements over widely used and strong baselines. We hope our work will help bridge the gap between generative modeling and representation learning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "Diffuse and Disperse" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Dispersive Loss," a simple, plug-and-play regularization technique designed to improve diffusion-based generative models.  It encourages internal representations of the model to spread out in the hidden space, similar to the repulsive effect in contrastive self-supervised learning. Unlike contrastive learning, Dispersive Loss does *not* require positive sample pairs or data augmentation, making it easily integrable into existing diffusion pipelines.  The method is self-contained, minimalist (no pre-training, no extra parameters, no external data), and shows consistent performance improvements on ImageNet using models like DiT and SiT. The authors also demonstrate effectiveness in one-step generative models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies in the minimalist adaptation of contrastive learning principles to diffusion models.  Existing efforts like REPA use external pre-trained models and representation alignment. Dispersive Loss distinguishes itself by working purely within the diffusion model's own representation space and avoiding the need for carefully constructed positive pairs.  The idea of creating a "contrastive loss without positive pairs" in the *representation* space is novel, even if the underlying individual loss functions (e.g., InfoNCE) are not new in the field of representation learning itself. The key innovation is adapting and applying these well-established loss functions in a novel setting (diffusion models). However, it's important to note that negative-free contrastive learning has been studied in self-supervised learning before, and the paper acknowledges this. The novelty is applying this principle to *generative* models.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work lies in:
    *   **Bridging the Gap:** It takes a step toward connecting representation learning and generative modeling, two largely independent areas.
    *   **Simplicity and Accessibility:**  The "plug-and-play" nature of Dispersive Loss makes it highly accessible and easy to adopt.  Its minimalist design allows it to be used without extensive modifications to existing pipelines.
    *   **Performance Improvements:**  The consistent performance gains demonstrated across different models and model sizes suggest a general applicability.
    *   **Computational Efficiency:** The small overhead makes it more likely to be adopted in real-world applications.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents a wide range of experiments on different models (SiT, DiT, MeanFlow) and scales, demonstrating the effectiveness of Dispersive Loss.
    *   **Ablation Studies:**  The ablation studies provide insights into the effects of different loss variants, the optimal layer for regularization, and the impact of the regularization strength and temperature.
    *   **Minimalist Design:**  The simplicity of the method is a major strength, increasing its potential for widespread adoption.
    *   **Clarity:** The paper is generally well-written and easy to follow.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While consistently positive, the improvements are often incremental. Some readers may view these as minor optimizations rather than a breakthrough. The absolute improvements are not extremely large, although the relative improvements, especially at lower epochs, are encouraging.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:**  The paper provides an intuitive explanation of why Dispersive Loss works, but lacks a rigorous theoretical analysis.
    *   **Dependence on Hyperparameter Tuning:** While the method is presented as plug-and-play, there is a temperature and weight parameter that needs tuning. Some experiments show robustness to these parameters, but a truly generalizable solution would ideally be parameter-free.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence the field by:
    *   **Encouraging Further Research:**  It may inspire other researchers to explore representation learning techniques in generative modeling.
    *   **Improving Generative Models:**  Dispersive Loss itself may become a widely used regularization technique, leading to improved generative models.
    *   **Inspiring New Regularization Methods:** Other researchers may be motivated to develop new, more sophisticated representation learning techniques to improve generative models.

**Justification of Score:**

A score of 7 is justified.  The paper presents a novel and effective regularization technique for diffusion models that is easy to implement and improves performance. While the individual components are not entirely novel and the improvements are not dramatic, the overall contribution is significant due to its simplicity, generalizability, and empirical support. The method avoids dependence on external models, making it practical and widely applicable. It helps to bridge the gap between representation learning and generative modeling. A higher score would require greater performance improvements or greater theoretical insights. A lower score would be appropriate if the method failed to generalize or had significant limitations. The "contrastive loss without positive pairs" is the most novel aspect.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Router-R1: Teaching LLMs Multi-Round Routing and Aggregation via Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09033v1)
- **Authors**: Haozhen Zhang, Tao Feng, Jiaxuan You
- **Abstract**: The rapid emergence of diverse large language models (LLMs) has spurred the development of LLM routers that assign user queries to the most suitable model. However, existing LLM routers typically perform a single-round, one-to-one mapping (\textit{i.e.}, assigning each query to a single model in isolation), which limits their capability to tackle complex tasks that demand the complementary strengths of multiple LLMs. In this paper, we present \textbf{Router-R1}, a reinforcement learning (RL)-based framework that formulates multi-LLM routing and aggregation as a sequential decision process. Router-R1 instantiates the router itself as a capable LLM, leveraging its reasoning ability to interleave "think" actions (internal deliberation) with "route" actions (dynamic model invocation), and integrates each response into its evolving context. To guide learning, we employ a lightweight rule-based reward comprising format rewards, final outcome rewards, and a novel cost reward for performance and cost trade-off optimization, opening a pathway toward optimizing performance-cost tradeoffs via RL. Router-R1 also conditions only on simple model descriptors such as pricing, latency, and example performance, enabling strong generalization to unseen model selection. Experiments on seven general and multi-hop QA benchmarks show that Router-R1 outperforms over several strong baselines, achieving superior performance while maintaining robust generalization and cost management.Code is available at https://github.com/ulab-uiuc/Router-R1.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the Router-R1 paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Router-R1, a reinforcement learning (RL) framework designed for multi-round routing and aggregation of large language models (LLMs).  Instead of the typical one-shot routing, Router-R1 frames LLM coordination as a sequential decision process.  It instantiates the router itself as a capable LLM, allowing it to interleave internal reasoning ("think") actions with external model invocation ("route") actions, integrating responses into the evolving context. Learning is guided by a rule-based reward function that considers format, outcome, and cost. The router conditions its decisions on simple model descriptors, aiming for generalization to unseen models. The authors demonstrate Router-R1's superior performance on several QA benchmarks compared to existing routing methods, showcasing better performance, generalization, and cost management.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several key aspects:

    *   **Multi-Round Routing as Sequential Decision Making:**  While LLM routing has been explored, framing it as a multi-round, sequential decision process with RL is a significant departure from existing approaches. This allows for more complex and adaptive coordination of multiple LLMs.
    *   **LLM as Router:** Using a powerful LLM to *both* reason about *when* and *which* external LLMs to invoke is an elegant way to leverage existing LLM capabilities for routing. The interleaved "think" and "route" actions allows Router-R1 to dynamically adapt its answering strategy based on the question's complexity.
    *   **Rule-based Reward Design for Cost Awareness:** The careful design of the reward function to balance performance and cost, along with the hierarchical structure to prioritize different reward components, is a practical and effective contribution.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work stems from addressing a critical challenge in the age of diverse LLMs: how to effectively coordinate these models to solve complex tasks while managing costs. The ability to adapt to new LLMs without retraining is also practically valuable. The experimental results demonstrate the real-world impact of the framework, outperforming existing methods on multiple benchmarks. This work can significantly impact how LLM-based applications are designed, moving beyond single-model solutions towards more intelligent and adaptive systems.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Formulation:** The paper clearly defines the problem of multi-round LLM routing and motivates its approach effectively.
    *   **Well-Designed Framework:** Router-R1 is thoughtfully designed, with a strong emphasis on adaptability, cost-awareness, and generalization.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experiments are thorough, covering a wide range of datasets and baselines. The analysis of cost rewards and generalization capability is particularly insightful.
    *   **Reproducibility**: the authors provide a link to the source code in the abstract and throughout the paper.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:** While effective, the RL-based approach adds complexity to the system. Setting up and tuning the RL training might require expertise and resources not readily available to all practitioners.
    *   **Latency:** The multi-round nature of Router-R1 introduces latency. The paper mentions this but doesn't fully explore strategies to mitigate it, such as parallelizing model calls or developing more efficient routing heuristics. This is a limitation for time-sensitive applications.
    *   **Limited Task Scope:** The evaluation primarily focuses on question answering. While QA is a relevant benchmark, the paper could benefit from exploring Router-R1's performance on other tasks like summarization or code generation, which may reveal different routing dynamics.
    *   **Model Descriptor reliance**: Model description in text format is not consistent. The performance of Router-R1 relies heavily on the textual descriptor, which can be inconsistently produced, inaccurate or incomplete.

*   **Impact on Field:** This paper is likely to spur further research on adaptive and cost-aware LLM routing.  The framework's modular design may inspire the development of new routing strategies and reward functions.  The emphasis on generalization is also important, as it encourages the creation of systems that can seamlessly integrate new LLMs as they become available.
*   **Score Justification**: The paper is a significant contribution, and while some limitations exist, they do not outweigh the overall impact. The core idea of sequential routing with a LLM-based router is novel and effective. It demonstrates good performance and generalization. However, limitations in latency and the complexity from RL training and the reliance on model descriptions prevent it from a higher score.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### FZOO: Fast Zeroth-Order Optimizer for Fine-Tuning Large Language Models towards Adam-Scale Speed
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09034v1)
- **Authors**: Sizhe Dang, Yangyang Guo, Yanjun Zhao, Haishan Ye, Xiaodong Zheng, Guang Dai, Ivor Tsang
- **Abstract**: Fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) often faces GPU memory bottlenecks: the backward pass of first-order optimizers like Adam increases memory usage to more than 10 times the inference level (e.g., 633 GB for OPT-30B). Zeroth-order (ZO) optimizers avoid this cost by estimating gradients only from forward passes, yet existing methods like MeZO usually require many more steps to converge. Can this trade-off between speed and memory in ZO be fundamentally improved? Normalized-SGD demonstrates strong empirical performance with greater memory efficiency than Adam. In light of this, we introduce FZOO, a Fast Zeroth-Order Optimizer toward Adam-Scale Speed. FZOO reduces the total forward passes needed for convergence by employing batched one-sided estimates that adapt step sizes based on the standard deviation of batch losses. It also accelerates per-batch computation through the use of Rademacher random vector perturbations coupled with CUDA's parallel processing. Extensive experiments on diverse models, including RoBERTa-large, OPT (350M-66B), Phi-2, and Llama3, across 11 tasks validate FZOO's effectiveness. On average, FZOO outperforms MeZO by 3 percent in accuracy while requiring 3 times fewer forward passes. For RoBERTa-large, FZOO achieves average improvements of 5.6 percent in accuracy and an 18 times reduction in forward passes compared to MeZO, achieving convergence speeds comparable to Adam. We also provide theoretical analysis proving FZOO's formal equivalence to a normalized-SGD update rule and its convergence guarantees. FZOO integrates smoothly into PEFT techniques, enabling even larger memory savings. Overall, our results make single-GPU, high-speed, full-parameter fine-tuning practical and point toward future work on memory-efficient pre-training.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the FZOO paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FZOO, a novel zeroth-order (ZO) optimizer designed to fine-tune large language models (LLMs) with Adam-scale convergence speed while maintaining inference-level memory usage.  Unlike first-order optimizers like Adam, ZO methods avoid backpropagation, significantly reducing memory footprint. FZOO achieves its speed-up through two primary strategies: (1) employing batched one-sided gradient estimates with adaptive step sizes informed by the standard deviation of batch losses, and (2) accelerating per-batch computation using efficient Rademacher random vector perturbations and CUDA-optimized parallelism.  The authors theoretically analyze FZOO, proving its equivalence to a normalized-SGD update rule and establishing convergence guarantees.  Extensive experiments on various models (RoBERTa-large, OPT family, Phi-2, Llama3) and downstream tasks demonstrate FZOO's superior efficiency compared to existing ZO methods like MeZO. The paper also showcases FZOO's seamless integration with parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) techniques, further reducing memory consumption.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its combination of several techniques to address the speed limitations of ZO optimizers.  While individual components like Rademacher perturbations and adaptive step sizes have been explored previously, FZOO's unique integration of these elements, specifically tailored for LLM fine-tuning, represents a significant advancement.  The theoretical analysis provides a solid foundation for the method.  The connection to normalized-SGD is interesting and helps explain the method's effectiveness.  A *potential* weakness is that the theoretical novelty, while sound, isn't groundbreaking – it builds on existing ZO and SGD convergence results. The one-sided estimation approach isn't entirely new either, but its implementation and batch processing specifically target LLMs.

* **Significance:**  The paper's significance stems from its potential to make full-parameter fine-tuning of LLMs more accessible to researchers and practitioners with limited computational resources.  The promise of Adam-scale speed with inference-level memory opens up new possibilities for model adaptation and customization. The ability to plug into PEFT techniques further amplifies its practicality. The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating consistent improvements over MeZO across various benchmarks.  However, a *critical* point is that while FZOO reduces memory footprint, the time per iteration is still slower than Adam. The authors show that by combing FZOO with advanced libraries vLLM they can aim to achieve Adam speed but results are still a bit behind. Therefore, FZOO still doesn't surpass Adam in terms of wall-clock time for training.

* **Strengths:**
    * Clear problem definition and motivation.
    * Well-designed FZOO algorithm with a solid theoretical basis.
    * Comprehensive experimental evaluation across diverse models and tasks.
    * Demonstrates practical benefits in terms of memory efficiency and speed improvement over existing ZO methods.
    * Integration with PEFT is a valuable feature.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * The theoretical analysis, while rigorous, isn't fundamentally new.
    * While improvements over MeZO are significant, achieving true Adam-level wall-clock training speed remains a challenge.
    * The paper may be too focused on outperforming MeZO, making it more of an incremental improvement over a previous method than a fundamentally new optimizer.

* **Potential Influence:**
    * FZOO could become a popular choice for fine-tuning LLMs in resource-constrained environments.
    * The work may inspire further research into ZO optimization techniques specifically tailored for LLMs, focusing on achieving even greater speedups and theoretical guarantees.
    * The combination of batched estimation, Rademacher perturbations, and CUDA optimization could serve as a blueprint for future ZO optimizers.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a *significant and valuable contribution* to the field of LLM fine-tuning by addressing the critical memory bottleneck associated with first-order optimizers. While FZOO builds on existing techniques, its *careful combination and optimization* of these elements within the ZO framework, coupled with a *solid theoretical analysis and extensive experimental validation*, warrants a high score. The *impact* on accessibility and resource efficiency is undeniable. However, the score is *not higher* because the theoretical novelty is not groundbreaking, the wall-clock training time isn't yet competitive with Adam, and because the paper primarily shows an incremental, though important, improvement over an existing ZO method (MeZO). However, that incremental improvement greatly advances the applicability and efficiency of ZO methods. Given these facts, a score of 7.5 accurately reflects the paper's strengths and weaknesses within the context of the current LLM optimization landscape.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### AbstentionBench: Reasoning LLMs Fail on Unanswerable Questions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09038v1)
- **Authors**: Polina Kirichenko, Mark Ibrahim, Kamalika Chaudhuri, Samuel J. Bell
- **Abstract**: For Large Language Models (LLMs) to be reliably deployed in both everyday and high-stakes domains, knowing when not to answer is equally critical as answering correctly. Real-world user queries, which can be underspecified, ill-posed, or fundamentally unanswerable, require LLMs to reason about uncertainty and selectively abstain -- i.e., refuse to answer definitively. However, abstention remains understudied, without a systematic evaluation framework for modern LLMs. In this work, we introduce AbstentionBench, a large-scale benchmark for holistically evaluating abstention across 20 diverse datasets, including questions with unknown answers, underspecification, false premises, subjective interpretations, and outdated information. Evaluating 20 frontier LLMs reveals abstention is an unsolved problem, and one where scaling models is of little use. While recent reasoning LLMs have shown impressive results in complex problem solving, surprisingly, we find that reasoning fine-tuning degrades abstention (by $24\%$ on average), even for math and science domains on which reasoning models are explicitly trained. We find that while a carefully crafted system prompt can boost abstention in practice, it does not resolve models' fundamental inability to reason about uncertainty. We release AbstentionBench to foster research into advancing LLM reliability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "AbstentionBench: Reasoning LLMs Fail on Unanswerable Questions."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AbstentionBench, a new benchmark designed to evaluate the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to abstain from answering questions when faced with uncertainty or unanswerable queries. The benchmark comprises 20 diverse datasets spanning various scenarios like unknown answers, underspecification, false premises, subjective interpretations, and outdated information. The authors evaluated 20 frontier LLMs using AbstentionBench, revealing that abstention remains a challenging problem. Surprisingly, reasoning fine-tuning often degrades abstention performance. While carefully crafted prompts can improve abstention in practice, they don't fully address the underlying inability of models to reason about uncertainty.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Strength:** The primary novelty lies in the creation of a comprehensive and diverse benchmark specifically targeting LLM abstention across a range of realistic scenarios. Previous research has often focused on isolated cases or specific types of uncertainty. The systematic curation of datasets is a valuable contribution.
*   **Weakness:** While the individual datasets are, for the most part, pre-existing, the paper's novelty is in the aggregate.

**Significance:**

*   **Strength:** The paper highlights a critical weakness in current LLMs: the inability to reliably abstain from answering unanswerable questions. This has significant implications for the safe and trustworthy deployment of LLMs in real-world applications, especially high-stakes domains. The finding that reasoning fine-tuning *degrades* abstention is a particularly important and unexpected observation, warranting further investigation. The study challenges the conventional wisdom that scaling and reasoning capabilities automatically translate into improved reliability.

*   **Weakness:** Some aspects of the experimental setup, such as relying on LLM judges for evaluation, introduce potential biases and limitations. The reliance on a specific judge LLM can also influence the results. The prompt for the LLM-as-a-judge, while validated, could still impact the final evaluations.
*   **Weakness:** While the system prompt approach is explored, the paper does not propose or investigate any significant algorithmic advancements for improving abstention. It mainly serves to identify a problem and provide a standardized way to evaluate it.

**Potential Influence:**

*   The release of AbstentionBench is likely to stimulate further research into LLM abstention, leading to the development of new techniques and training strategies specifically designed to improve models' ability to recognize and handle uncertainty. The findings could also influence the design of reasoning fine-tuning methods, encouraging the incorporation of uncertainty awareness during training.
*   The benchmark could become a standard evaluation tool for assessing the reliability of LLMs, complementing existing benchmarks focused on accuracy and other capabilities.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by identifying a crucial problem in LLM reliability and providing a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating abstention. The finding that reasoning fine-tuning can hurt abstention is surprising and significant. While the paper doesn't offer immediate solutions, it provides a clear direction for future research.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### Autoregressive Semantic Visual Reconstruction Helps VLMs Understand Better
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09040v1)
- **Authors**: Dianyi Wang, Wei Song, Yikun Wang, Siyuan Wang, Kaicheng Yu, Zhongyu Wei, Jiaqi Wang
- **Abstract**: Typical large vision-language models (LVLMs) apply autoregressive supervision solely to textual sequences, without fully incorporating the visual modality into the learning process. This results in three key limitations: (1) an inability to utilize images without accompanying captions, (2) the risk that captions omit critical visual details, and (3) the challenge that certain vision-centric content cannot be adequately conveyed through text. As a result, current LVLMs often prioritize vision-to-language alignment while potentially overlooking fine-grained visual information. While some prior works have explored autoregressive image generation, effectively leveraging autoregressive visual supervision to enhance image understanding remains an open challenge. In this paper, we introduce Autoregressive Semantic Visual Reconstruction (ASVR), which enables joint learning of visual and textual modalities within a unified autoregressive framework. We show that autoregressively reconstructing the raw visual appearance of images does not enhance and may even impair multimodal understanding. In contrast, autoregressively reconstructing the semantic representation of images consistently improves comprehension. Notably, we find that even when models are given continuous image features as input, they can effectively reconstruct discrete semantic tokens, resulting in stable and consistent improvements across a wide range of multimodal understanding benchmarks. Our approach delivers significant performance gains across varying data scales (556k-2M) and types of LLM bacbones. Specifically, ASVR improves LLaVA-1.5 by 5% in average scores across 14 multimodal benchmarks. The code is available at https://github.com/AlenjandroWang/ASVR.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Autoregressive Semantic Visual Reconstruction (ASVR), a method to enhance Vision-Language Models (VLMs) by incorporating explicit visual supervision.  Instead of solely relying on textual outputs for training, ASVR supervises the model to autoregressively reconstruct semantic representations of input images.  The key finding is that reconstructing *semantic* tokens (derived from a pre-trained visual tokenizer) leads to better performance than reconstructing raw visual appearance (e.g., pixels).  The paper shows that even when using continuous image features as input, reconstructing discrete semantic tokens results in consistent improvements across a range of multimodal benchmarks and varying data scales and LLM backbones. ASVR aims to address the limitations of language-centric training, where crucial visual details might be missed or cannot be adequately described through text alone.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using autoregressive supervision for visual information is not entirely new. Other works have explored autoregressive image generation within VLMs. However, the paper's central claim—that reconstructing *semantic* visual representations is more effective for *improving visual understanding* than reconstructing raw pixels—is a valuable and novel insight. This distinction is crucial because it shifts the focus from generative capabilities to discriminative visual comprehension. The paper also claims that their method is a unified framework allowing for seamless integration of visual and textual supervision, an advantage over methods that rely on external modules.

*   **Significance:** The paper demonstrates a clear performance improvement across a range of multimodal tasks and architectures, suggesting broad applicability and potential impact. The findings that focusing on semantic visual reconstruction can improve aspects like knowledge-based QA, visual-centric tasks, and hallucination robustness highlights the importance of grounding VLMs in meaningful visual representations.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper provides empirical evidence supporting the benefit of semantic visual reconstruction, with consistent gains over baselines.
    *   Ablation studies effectively isolate the impact of different components (semantic vs. appearance reconstruction).
    *   The approach is evaluated on diverse benchmarks, data scales, and LLM backbones, which strengthens the generalizability claim.
    *   The method seems straightforward to implement and doesn't require complex architectural changes.
    *   The qualitative results offer some insights into the improved attention mechanisms resulting from ASVR.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The explanation of *why* semantic reconstruction is better than appearance reconstruction could be more in-depth. The paper alludes to the limitations of text-based supervision but doesn't fully articulate the specific challenges that raw pixel reconstruction might face.
    *   The paper depends on a pre-trained visual tokenizer. While the use of such tokenizers is common, more discussion on the potential limitations introduced by this dependency would be beneficial (e.g., sensitivity to the tokenizer's training data, impact of the tokenizer's architecture, failure modes).
    *   While the benchmarks are diverse, a deeper analysis of the *types* of visual reasoning tasks where ASVR shows the biggest gains would be more useful.
    *   Comparison to other methods that leverage visual supervision for *understanding* (even if not autoregressive) could be more detailed.
    *   The qualitative visualizations of the attention are suggestive but could be augmented with more quantitative analysis to support the claim of more precise focus on relevant regions.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research by encouraging more emphasis on visual representation learning within VLMs. The focus on semantic reconstruction may inspire new methods for grounding models in visual data beyond simple image-caption alignment. The "unified framework" claim, if fully realized, could lead to simpler and more effective training pipelines for multimodal models.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a solid contribution with a clear hypothesis, empirical support, and insightful findings. While some aspects could be further explored (e.g., deeper analysis of why semantic reconstruction works, more detailed discussion of tokenizer limitations), the overall quality and potential impact warrant a high score. The focus on improving visual understanding through a novel autoregressive objective represents a valuable step forward for the field.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### MagCache: Fast Video Generation with Magnitude-Aware Cache
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09045v1)
- **Authors**: Zehong Ma, Longhui Wei, Feng Wang, Shiliang Zhang, Qi Tian
- **Abstract**: Existing acceleration techniques for video diffusion models often rely on uniform heuristics or time-embedding variants to skip timesteps and reuse cached features. These approaches typically require extensive calibration with curated prompts and risk inconsistent outputs due to prompt-specific overfitting. In this paper, we introduce a novel and robust discovery: a unified magnitude law observed across different models and prompts. Specifically, the magnitude ratio of successive residual outputs decreases monotonically and steadily in most timesteps while rapidly in the last several steps. Leveraging this insight, we introduce a Magnitude-aware Cache (MagCache) that adaptively skips unimportant timesteps using an error modeling mechanism and adaptive caching strategy. Unlike existing methods requiring dozens of curated samples for calibration, MagCache only requires a single sample for calibration. Experimental results show that MagCache achieves 2.1x and 2.68x speedups on Open-Sora and Wan 2.1, respectively, while preserving superior visual fidelity. It significantly outperforms existing methods in LPIPS, SSIM, and PSNR, under comparable computational budgets.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "MagCache: Fast Video Generation with Magnitude-Aware Cache":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MagCache, a novel acceleration technique for video diffusion models.  It is based on the observation that the magnitude ratio of successive residual outputs in video diffusion models decreases monotonically for most timesteps and rapidly in the final steps.  MagCache adaptively skips unimportant timesteps using an error modeling mechanism based on this magnitude law and an adaptive caching strategy. Unlike existing caching methods that require extensive calibration, MagCache requires only a single sample for calibration. The experiments demonstrate significant speedups (2.1x to 2.68x on Open-Sora and Wan 2.1) while maintaining or even improving visual quality, outperforming existing caching methods in LPIPS, SSIM, and PSNR.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The core novelty lies in the identification and exploitation of a stable, monotonic magnitude decay law governing the residuals in video diffusion models.  While caching itself is not a new concept, the magnitude-aware strategy and the low calibration overhead distinguish this approach. The observation that the ratio change is consistent across prompts and models is important.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial bottleneck in diffusion models: slow inference speed. MagCache offers a lightweight, training-free method to accelerate video generation without sacrificing visual quality. The results, showing substantial speedups *and* improved visual metrics compared to previous work, are significant. A key strength is that the method requires minimal calibration, making it practical for widespread adoption. The plug-and-play nature, facilitating seamless integration into existing pipelines, is also a noteworthy advantage.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong empirical results:** The paper provides convincing evidence of the effectiveness of MagCache on multiple video generation models and metrics. The visualization results in Figure 3 provide further support for the visual fidelity improvements.
    *   **Low calibration overhead:** Compared to approaches like TeaCache, the single-sample calibration makes MagCache more practical and reduces the risk of overfitting to a specific calibration set.
    *   **Theoretical justification:** The paper offers a theoretical basis (the magnitude decay law) to explain the effectiveness of the caching strategy.
    *   **Detailed Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies on the threshold and skip length provide valuable insights into the interplay between speed and quality, demonstrating the robustness of the approach and parameter selections.
    *   **Good Presentation:** The paper is well-written and organized, with clear explanations of the method and results.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** The paper primarily focuses on video diffusion models. While the authors mention in the conclusion that they will validate the mag cache on image models in future work, the performance in that realm isn't covered in the current manuscript.
    *   **Lack of Statistical Significance:** The absence of error bars in the results, as acknowledged by the authors, is a minor weakness. While the authors claim stability, quantifying that stability with confidence intervals would strengthen the claims.
    *   **Parameter tuning**: While parameter robustess is demonstrated, the exact parameters still needs to be tuned for each video model which may not always be straight forward

*   **Potential Influence:**  MagCache has the potential to significantly impact the video generation field by enabling faster and more efficient inference. Its training-free nature and superior performance compared to existing caching methods could make it a valuable tool for researchers and practitioners alike. If the magnitude law extends to other diffusion models and tasks (as the authors suggest), its impact could be even broader.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**
MagCache presents a novel and effective technique for accelerating video diffusion models. The discovery of the magnitude decay law is significant, and the resulting caching strategy demonstrably improves both speed and visual quality. The limitations are relatively minor and do not detract from the overall contribution. While there are opportunities to extend and further validate the approach, the current results are compelling and indicate a significant advance in the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Agentic Neural Networks: Self-Evolving Multi-Agent Systems via Textual Backpropagation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09046v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaowen Ma, Chenyang Lin, Yao Zhang, Volker Tresp, Yunpu Ma
- **Abstract**: Leveraging multiple Large Language Models(LLMs) has proven effective for addressing complex, high-dimensional tasks, but current approaches often rely on static, manually engineered multi-agent configurations. To overcome these constraints, we present the Agentic Neural Network(ANN), a framework that conceptualizes multi-agent collaboration as a layered neural network architecture. In this design, each agent operates as a node, and each layer forms a cooperative "team" focused on a specific subtask. Agentic Neural Network follows a two-phase optimization strategy: (1) Forward Phase-Drawing inspiration from neural network forward passes, tasks are dynamically decomposed into subtasks, and cooperative agent teams with suitable aggregation methods are constructed layer by layer. (2) Backward Phase-Mirroring backpropagation, we refine both global and local collaboration through iterative feedback, allowing agents to self-evolve their roles, prompts, and coordination. This neuro-symbolic approach enables ANN to create new or specialized agent teams post-training, delivering notable gains in accuracy and adaptability. Across four benchmark datasets, ANN surpasses leading multi-agent baselines under the same configurations, showing consistent performance improvements. Our findings indicate that ANN provides a scalable, data-driven framework for multi-agent systems, combining the collaborative capabilities of LLMs with the efficiency and flexibility of neural network principles. We plan to open-source the entire framework.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the Agentic Neural Network (ANN), a novel framework that adapts principles from classical neural networks to orchestrate multiple Large Language Model (LLM) agents. Unlike existing multi-agent systems (MAS) that often rely on static, manually engineered configurations, ANN conceptualizes multi-agent collaboration as a layered neural network architecture where each agent acts as a node and each layer forms a "team" focused on a specific subtask.  ANN employs a two-phase optimization strategy: a forward phase where tasks are dynamically decomposed and agent teams are constructed layer-by-layer, and a backward phase mirroring backpropagation where global and local collaboration are refined through iterative feedback, allowing agents to self-evolve their roles, prompts, and coordination. The paper evaluates ANN on four challenging datasets (MATH, DABench, Creative Writing, and HumanEval) and demonstrates that it outperforms existing MAS baselines by automating prompt tuning, role assignment, and agent collaboration.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty of Approach:** The core idea of mapping multi-agent collaboration onto a neural network architecture is a significant departure from traditional MAS design and offers a fresh perspective. The neuro-symbolic approach, particularly the use of textual backpropagation, is a novel and promising mechanism for optimizing LLM-based systems.
    *   **Automation of MAS Design:** The paper effectively addresses a major bottleneck in MAS research - the manual effort required for prompt engineering, role assignment, and topology definition. ANN offers a data-driven framework that automates these processes, making MAS more accessible and scalable.
    *   **Empirical Results:** The experimental results across four diverse datasets provide strong evidence of ANN's effectiveness. The fact that ANN consistently surpasses leading MAS baselines under the same configurations is compelling. The investigation into GPT-4o-mini is interesting and useful and adds to the overall contributions of the paper.
    *   **Self-Evolving Capabilities:** The framework's ability to dynamically reconfigure agent teams and coordination strategies based on task demands is a major strength, enabling adaptation to novel tasks.
    *   **Extensive experimental evaluation:** The paper includes extensive experimentation and evaluations of models. The use of ablation studies to investigate the importance of the various components of ANN helps build an evidence-based case. The results are comprehensive, including multiple datasets, and the authors thoughtfully investigate and evaluate their approach and findings.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Initial Structures:** While ANN automates much of MAS design, it still depends on manually defined initial structure candidates and node prompts. This limits its adaptability to truly diverse domains and introduces a potential bias.
    *   **Computational Overhead:** The dynamic selection of agent teams could lead to significant computational overhead, particularly as the number of candidate teams increases. The paper could benefit from a more detailed analysis of the computational complexity of ANN.
    *   **Black Box Nature:** While ANN enhances interpretability compared to purely connectionist systems, it still operates as a "black box" to some extent. Understanding *why* specific agent configurations emerge and how they contribute to performance remains a challenge.
    *   **Prompt engineering:** While the system automates the tuning of agents and their communications with one another, one of the key steps it executes includes writing suitable prompts.
    *   **Lack of comparison:** While the paper has multiple well-known baselines for comparison, the number of these baselines are sparse for several datasets.

*   **Significance and Potential Influence:**

    *   ANN could significantly influence the design and development of future MAS. Its automated optimization approach has the potential to make LLM-based systems more robust, adaptable, and efficient.
    *   The concept of textual backpropagation could be extended to other areas of LLM research, such as fine-tuning and knowledge transfer.
    *   By bridging the gap between symbolic AI (agent coordination) and connectionist AI (neural networks), ANN could foster new synergies between these two paradigms.

*   **Justification of Score:**
    ANN presents a novel approach with significant potential impact, but faces real-world limitations and a need for further refinement in automation and interpretability. It addresses a critical challenge and provides a strong empirical foundation for future research. Therefore, a score of 8 is appropriate.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Seedance 1.0: Exploring the Boundaries of Video Generation Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09113v1)
- **Authors**: Yu Gao, Haoyuan Guo, Tuyen Hoang, Weilin Huang, Lu Jiang, Fangyuan Kong, Huixia Li, Jiashi Li, Liang Li, Xiaojie Li, Xunsong Li, Yifu Li, Shanchuan Lin, Zhijie Lin, Jiawei Liu, Shu Liu, Xiaonan Nie, Zhiwu Qing, Yuxi Ren, Li Sun, Zhi Tian, Rui Wang, Sen Wang, Guoqiang Wei, Guohong Wu, Jie Wu, Ruiqi Xia, Fei Xiao, Xuefeng Xiao, Jiangqiao Yan, Ceyuan Yang, Jianchao Yang, Runkai Yang, Tao Yang, Yihang Yang, Zilyu Ye, Xuejiao Zeng, Yan Zeng, Heng Zhang, Yang Zhao, Xiaozheng Zheng, Peihao Zhu, Jiaxin Zou, Feilong Zuo
- **Abstract**: Notable breakthroughs in diffusion modeling have propelled rapid improvements in video generation, yet current foundational model still face critical challenges in simultaneously balancing prompt following, motion plausibility, and visual quality. In this report, we introduce Seedance 1.0, a high-performance and inference-efficient video foundation generation model that integrates several core technical improvements: (i) multi-source data curation augmented with precision and meaningful video captioning, enabling comprehensive learning across diverse scenarios; (ii) an efficient architecture design with proposed training paradigm, which allows for natively supporting multi-shot generation and jointly learning of both text-to-video and image-to-video tasks. (iii) carefully-optimized post-training approaches leveraging fine-grained supervised fine-tuning, and video-specific RLHF with multi-dimensional reward mechanisms for comprehensive performance improvements; (iv) excellent model acceleration achieving ~10x inference speedup through multi-stage distillation strategies and system-level optimizations. Seedance 1.0 can generate a 5-second video at 1080p resolution only with 41.4 seconds (NVIDIA-L20). Compared to state-of-the-art video generation models, Seedance 1.0 stands out with high-quality and fast video generation having superior spatiotemporal fluidity with structural stability, precise instruction adherence in complex multi-subject contexts, native multi-shot narrative coherence with consistent subject representation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the Seedance 1.0 paper, based on the provided text:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Seedance 1.0, a high-performance video generation model that aims to address key challenges in prompt following, motion plausibility, and visual quality simultaneously. Seedance 1.0 integrates several technical improvements, including multi-source data curation with precision captioning, an efficient architecture design supporting multi-shot generation and joint text-to-video/image-to-video learning, optimized post-training approaches using supervised fine-tuning and video-specific RLHF, and significant inference acceleration via distillation and system optimizations. The model claims superior spatiotemporal fluidity, structural stability, precise instruction adherence, native multi-shot narrative coherence, and consistent subject representation, while achieving ultra-fast generation speeds compared to other commercial counterparts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Approach:** The paper presents a holistic approach, addressing data curation, architecture design, training, and inference optimization, which suggests a well-engineered system.
    *   **Performance Claims:**  The claims of superior performance on key video generation metrics (motion, structure, prompt following) and achieving impressive generation speeds are significant if validated.
    *   **Novel Combinations:** The combination of techniques like decoupled spatial and temporal layers with window attention, unified task formulation, and multi-dimensional RLHF demonstrates an attempt to innovate beyond existing approaches.
    *   **Efficient Architecture:** The emphasis on an efficient architecture and inference optimizations is valuable for practical deployment of video generation models.
    * **Multi-task Versatility:** Supporting both text-to-video and image-to-video within a single model, along with bilingual capabilities, is a useful feature.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Technical Details:**  The paper provides a high-level overview but lacks detailed descriptions of the architecture, training procedures, and distillation strategies. It mentions techniques like "MMDiT" and "TSCD," but it doesn't clearly explain how they have been customized or optimized for video generation in their system, requiring the reader to look elsewhere. A clear explanation of the "multi-dimensional reward mechanisms" in RLHF is also necessary.
    *   **Evaluation Concerns:** While the paper mentions leaderboards and internal evaluations, the specific datasets, metrics, and evaluation protocols are not fully transparent.
    *   **Dependence on Existing Techniques:**  Many of the components (VAE, Diffusion Transformer, RLHF) are based on well-established techniques. The *novelty* lies in the *integration* and specific *optimizations*, but the paper doesn't always provide sufficient detail to assess the originality of these contributions. While the paper talks about innovation, it uses a lot of pre-existing frameworks and techniques, making its originality not as great as the paper indicates.
    *   **Limited Ablation Studies:**  It is difficult to assess the individual contributions of each component without detailed ablation studies.
    *   **Unverifiable Claims:** The paper claims to be the best in various benchmarks, but its analysis is limited. It is tough to accurately verify these claims based on the details the paper provides.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    *   The primary *novelty* appears to be the well-engineered integration and optimization of existing techniques for video generation rather than groundbreaking architectural innovations. This integrated system might achieve significantly improved performance and efficiency.
    *   If the performance claims are validated and the approach proves generalizable, Seedance 1.0 could have a significant impact on the practical deployment of video generation models, enabling faster and higher-quality video creation.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a strong engineering effort, combining several state-of-the-art techniques into a functional system. It seems to improve video generation performance, making it a highly useful tool in content creation. However, the *lack of detailed technical information* significantly hinders its contribution. The reader cannot thoroughly judge the novelty and efficacy of the system.
Although it makes a lot of bold claims about its advantages, its ability to reproduce these results is unverified.

**Score: 6**

The paper is good as an engineering contribution, and it integrates multiple well-established components, though it lacks in-depth analysis and explanations to push it to a high-impact paper.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### LLM-as-a-qualitative-judge: automating error analysis in natural language generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09147v1)
- **Authors**: Nadezhda Chirkova, Tunde Oluwaseyi Ajayi, Seth Aycock, Zain Muhammad Mujahid, Vladana Perlić, Ekaterina Borisova, Markarit Vartampetian
- **Abstract**: Prompting large language models (LLMs) to evaluate generated text, known as LLM-as-a-judge, has become a standard evaluation approach in natural language generation (NLG), but is primarily used as a quantitative tool, i.e. with numerical scores as main outputs. In this work, we propose LLM-as-a-qualitative-judge, an LLM-based evaluation approach with the main output being a structured report of common issue types in the NLG system outputs. Our approach is targeted at providing developers with meaningful insights on what improvements can be done to a given NLG system and consists of two main steps, namely open-ended per-instance issue analysis and clustering of the discovered issues using an intuitive cumulative algorithm. We also introduce a strategy for evaluating the proposed approach, coupled with ~300 annotations of issues in instances from 12 NLG datasets. Our results show that LLM-as-a-qualitative-judge correctly recognizes instance-specific issues in 2/3 cases and is capable of producing error type reports resembling the reports composed by human annotators. Our code and data are publicly available at https://github.com/tunde-ajayi/llm-as-a-qualitative-judge.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LLM-as-a-qualitative-judge: automating error analysis in natural language generation":

**Summary:**

This paper introduces "LLM-as-a-qualitative-judge," a novel approach for automating error analysis in natural language generation (NLG) outputs. Instead of primarily relying on numerical scores from LLM-based evaluators (LLM-as-a-judge), the authors propose using LLMs to generate structured reports of common qualitative error types. The method involves two main steps: (1) open-ended, per-instance issue analysis using an LLM to identify problems in each generated output, and (2) clustering these discovered issues into meaningful categories using a cumulative algorithm that mimics human error grouping.  They also introduce a novel evaluation strategy for their approach, involving manually annotating a diverse set of ~300 error instances from 12 different NLG datasets. The paper demonstrates that LLM-as-a-qualitative-judge can recognize issues with reasonable accuracy and generate error reports that resemble human-generated reports.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using LLMs for *qualitative* error analysis in NLG, rather than just quantitative scoring, is a valuable contribution. The paper moves beyond simply assigning a score and provides actionable insights for developers. This differentiates it from most prior work, which focuses on improving the *accuracy* of LLM-based evaluation scores. While other work has involved using LLMs to generate explanations, this paper provides a robust systematic framework for identifying and grouping these errors. The cumulative clustering algorithm, designed to mimic human reasoning, is also a strong point of innovation. However, clustering via LLMs has been explored, although the paper justifies its new approach well.

*   **Significance:** Automating error analysis is crucial for improving NLG systems efficiently. Manual error analysis is time-consuming and subjective. A reliable automated approach could significantly accelerate the development cycle and reduce the reliance on metrics that sometimes poorly reflect actual output quality. The comprehensive evaluation strategy, including the manual annotation of a diverse set of NLG outputs, adds strength to the argument. The release of both the code and the dataset will be highly valuable for the research community.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper directly addresses a bottleneck in NLG development: error analysis.
    *   The approach is relatively straightforward to implement, given access to an LLM.
    *   The evaluation strategy is well-designed, involving both accuracy assessment of per-instance analysis and agreement measures for clustering.
    *   The use of a diverse set of NLG datasets strengthens the generalizability of the results.
    *   The released code and data are valuable contributions to the research community.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The performance of the LLM-as-a-qualitative-judge heavily relies on the capabilities of the underlying LLM, i.e. GPT-4o for the generation of the reports, and Claude for evaluations. This reliance makes the approach potentially less accessible and also vulnerable to changes in LLM performance or API availability. The experiments also showed a substantial influence on the performance based on the underlying LLM models used.
    *   While the overall accuracy of per-instance analysis is reasonable (~66%), there's still room for improvement. Some error types might be more challenging to identify than others.
    *   The evaluation strategy could be strengthened further by including inter-annotator agreement for the manual error annotation, providing a baseline for comparison.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to shift the focus of NLG evaluation research towards methods that provide more actionable insights for developers. It could also inspire the development of more sophisticated error analysis tools and techniques. By reducing the time and effort involved in error analysis, the proposed method can lead to faster and better NLG system improvements. The dataset and code will likely be used by other researchers for developing better error analysis techniques.

*   **Rigorous Rationale for the Score:** The paper is well-written, clearly presents a novel approach, and provides a thorough evaluation. It addresses a significant problem in the NLG field and has the potential to impact future research. However, some weaknesses remain, such as reliance on LLM capabilities, potential for improved per-instance analysis accuracy, and lack of baseline and human inter-annotator agreement. In addition, the clustering quality reported is mixed and the results on the real data reported rather low Adjusted Rand Index (ARI) values. While it is true that the cluster-agreement itself is highly subjective, overall this is a relevant limitation of the work.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Improving LLM Agent Planning with In-Context Learning via Atomic Fact Augmentation and Lookahead Search
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09171v1)
- **Authors**: Samuel Holt, Max Ruiz Luyten, Thomas Pouplin, Mihaela van der Schaar
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly capable but often require significant guidance or extensive interaction history to perform effectively in complex, interactive environments. Existing methods may struggle with adapting to new information or efficiently utilizing past experiences for multi-step reasoning without fine-tuning. We introduce a novel LLM agent framework that enhances planning capabilities through in-context learning, facilitated by atomic fact augmentation and a recursive lookahead search. Our agent learns to extract task-critical ``atomic facts'' from its interaction trajectories. These facts dynamically augment the prompts provided to LLM-based components responsible for action proposal, latent world model simulation, and state-value estimation. Planning is performed via a depth-limited lookahead search, where the LLM simulates potential trajectories and evaluates their outcomes, guided by the accumulated facts and interaction history. This approach allows the agent to improve its understanding and decision-making online, leveraging its experience to refine its behavior without weight updates. We provide a theoretical motivation linking performance to the quality of fact-based abstraction and LLM simulation accuracy. Empirically, our agent demonstrates improved performance and adaptability on challenging interactive tasks, achieving more optimal behavior as it accumulates experience, showcased in tasks such as TextFrozenLake and ALFWorld.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel Large Language Model (LLM) agent architecture called LWM-Planner, designed to improve planning in complex, interactive environments through in-context learning. The agent learns by extracting "atomic facts" from interaction trajectories, which augment prompts provided to LLM-based components for action proposal, world model simulation, and value estimation. Planning is performed via a depth-limited lookahead search, guided by accumulated facts and interaction history. This allows the agent to refine its behavior online without fine-tuning. The paper presents a theoretical motivation connecting performance to the quality of fact-based abstraction and LLM simulation accuracy, and demonstrates improved performance and adaptability on challenging tasks like TextFrozenLake and ALFWorld.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Novelty:*
The core idea of using LLMs for planning isn't entirely new. LLMs have been explored as simulators and action proposers before. The integration of *online* fact learning *without fine-tuning* and using LLMs both for multi-step simulation and value function *in-context* appears to be the key novelty. The idea of extracting structured, atomic facts rather than relying on raw trajectory retrieval or general textual reflections is a valuable distinction. The fact extraction mechanism is somewhat akin to specialized RAG, actively generating knowledge rather than pulling from a corpus. However, the degree to which this differs from existing works like Reflexion needs to be more clearly delineated.

*Significance:*
If the method holds up to rigorous scrutiny and broader application, the impact could be meaningful. The agent's ability to adapt *online*, purely through in-context learning, is a significant advance. The theoretical motivation helps to frame the approach and connect it to established concepts like fact-based state abstraction and the information bottleneck principle. The results on ALFWorld are encouraging, showing an ability to consistently achieve high reward through learning. The focus on *efficiently* using LLMs without weight updates is particularly relevant given the high computational cost of fine-tuning.

*Strengths:*
*   The paper is clearly written and well-organized.
*   The theoretical framework provides a good basis for understanding the approach.
*   The use of atomic facts is a clever way to ground the LLM's reasoning.
*   The empirical results are promising, demonstrating improved performance and adaptability.

*Weaknesses:*
*   The definition of "atomic facts" remains somewhat vague. It would be valuable to provide a more formal specification of what constitutes an atomic fact and how they are generated algorithmically. The examples are helpful, but more precision would strengthen the approach.
*   There is a reliance on zero-temperature inference that limits diversity and is perhaps not scalable to more stochastic and rich environments.
*   The experiments are limited in scope. While TextFrozenLake and ALFWorld are useful benchmarks, the paper would benefit from results on a broader range of tasks to demonstrate generalizability. The sensitivity to hyperparameters like the fact buffer size and compression strategy isn't adequately addressed.
*   A more extensive comparison to other LLM-based planning agents (beyond ReAct and Reflexion) is needed. For instance, how does it fare against retrieval-augmented approaches or more sophisticated exploration strategies?
*   The paper claims "no LLM fine-tuning" - it would be more specific to say the weights of the LLM are not changed - the architecture heavily relies on prompt engineering and selection to get the right behavior which is a form of learning.

*Potential Influence:*
The paper has the potential to influence the field by showing a way to build more robust and adaptive LLM agents without relying on computationally expensive fine-tuning. The emphasis on structured knowledge representation (atomic facts) could inspire other researchers to explore similar approaches for grounding LLMs in specific task domains. If the method scales well to more complex environments and is adopted by other researchers, it could become a valuable tool for building autonomous agents.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel LLM agent architecture that leverages in-context learning and atomic facts to improve planning in complex environments. The theoretical motivation and empirical results are promising. However, there are weaknesses regarding the scope of the experiments and the level of rigorous comparisons.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### The Curious Language Model: Strategic Test-Time Information Acquisition
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09173v1)
- **Authors**: Michael Cooper, Rohan Wadhawan, John Michael Giorgi, Chenhao Tan, Davis Liang
- **Abstract**: Decision-makers often possess insufficient information to render a confident decision. In these cases, the decision-maker can often undertake actions to acquire the necessary information about the problem at hand, e.g., by consulting knowledgeable authorities or by conducting experiments. Importantly, different levers of information acquisition come with different costs, posing the challenge of selecting the actions that are both informative and cost-effective. In this work, we propose CuriosiTree, a heuristic-based, test-time policy for zero-shot information acquisition in large language models (LLMs). CuriosiTree employs a greedy tree search to estimate the expected information gain of each action and strategically chooses actions based on a balance of anticipated information gain and associated cost. Empirical validation in a clinical diagnosis simulation shows that CuriosiTree enables cost-effective integration of heterogenous sources of information, and outperforms baseline action selection strategies in selecting action sequences that enable accurate diagnosis.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CURIOSITREE, a heuristic-based, test-time policy for zero-shot information acquisition in large language models (LLMs).
