### Multi-Agent Language Models: Advancing Cooperation, Coordination, and Adaptation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09331v1)
- **Authors**: Arjun Vaithilingam Sudhakar
- **Abstract**: Modern Large Language Models (LLMs) exhibit impressive zero-shot and few-shot generalization capabilities across complex natural language tasks, enabling their widespread use as virtual assistants for diverse applications such as translation and summarization. Despite being trained solely on large corpora of text without explicit supervision on author intent, LLMs appear to infer the underlying meaning of textual interactions. This raises a fundamental question: can LLMs model and reason about the intentions of others, i.e., do they possess a form of theory of mind? Understanding other's intentions is crucial for effective collaboration, which underpins human societal success and is essential for cooperative interactions among multiple agents, including humans and autonomous systems. In this work, we investigate the theory of mind in LLMs through the lens of cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL), where agents learn to collaborate via repeated interactions, mirroring human social reasoning. Our approach aims to enhance artificial agent's ability to adapt and cooperate with both artificial and human partners. By leveraging LLM-based agents capable of natural language interaction, we move towards creating hybrid human-AI systems that can foster seamless collaboration, with broad implications for the future of human-artificial interaction.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will summarize the thesis and provide a critical evaluation with a score.

**Thesis Summary:**

The thesis explores the use of Language Models (LLMs) in multi-agent settings, particularly focusing on enhancing cooperation, coordination, and adaptation capabilities. It investigates several key aspects:

*   **LM-in-the-Loop for Text Games:** Chapter 3 proposes a "Language Model in the Loop" approach to improve Reinforcement Learning (RL) agents in text-based games (Jericho). The core idea is to continuously update the LLM (GPT-2) using in-game experiences, reducing reliance on large, human-annotated datasets. Different methods for selecting the in-game transitions used to update the LLM are explored.
*   **LLMs as Multi-Agents in Hanabi:** Chapter 4 introduces a novel text-based version of the Hanabi game, creating a testbed for multi-agent LLMs. The thesis examines how LLMs, when fine-tuned with expert gameplay data, can act as independent Hanabi agents. It also investigates the integration of these LLMs with Q-learning RL agents using distillation loss.
*   **Future Directions:** The thesis concludes by outlining future research directions, including benchmarking the "theory of mind" capabilities of LLMs, exploring knowledge transfer between games with varying numbers of agents, and leveraging LLM few-shot reasoning to adapt to novel partners. It highlights the potential risks like language drift, variance in Hanabi gameplay, and LLM inference speed.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty in Application:** The thesis explores the application of LLMs in interactive environments, particularly text games and Hanabi, which provides a useful testbed for evaluating aspects of LM capabilities.
    *   **LM-in-the-Loop contribution** The work provides empirical demonstration that continuously training the LM using experience gained during gameplay can enable data efficiency in the context of text game exploration.
    *   **Hanabi Text Environment:** The introduction of a text-based Hanabi environment as a multi-agent testbed is a positive contribution. It enables the use of LLMs that operate primarily on text in a cooperative MARL setting.
    *   **Clear Research Questions:** The thesis defines three goals around benchmarking LLM theory of mind, knowledge transfer, and few-shot adaptation, which provides a focus to the work.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Experimental Depth:** While the thesis presents interesting results, some sections, especially the LLaMA finetuning for Hanabi (failed experiment), lack detailed exploration. More rigorous ablation studies to demonstrate the effect of each design choice were warranted.
    *   **Incremental Contribution:** The application of LLMs in RL is a growing area. The text-Hanabi task and the specific integration methods, while novel, lack a disruptive potential to drive the field.
    *   **Benchmarking Depth:** The thesis highlights evaluating theory of mind capabilities, but the performed Hanabi experiments touch this topic superficially.

*   **Significance:** The work contributes to the understanding of LLM capabilities in interactive and multi-agent environments. The limitations section provides honest assessment of the remaining challenge in this space.

    *   The future work points to relevant research directions, but the experiments performed in the thesis itself only provide preliminary investigation for the outlined research goals.

**Justification of Score:**

The thesis presents valuable insights into the potential and limitations of LLMs in interactive and multi-agent systems. However, the experimental depth and novelty of proposed methods are limited. The Hanabi environment helps to make advancements on those key areas. The incremental contribution and high level evaluation strategy results in lower significance.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Know What You Don't Know: Uncertainty Calibration of Process Reward Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09338v1)
- **Authors**: Young-Jin Park, Kristjan Greenewald, Kaveh Alim, Hao Wang, Navid Azizan
- **Abstract**: Process reward models (PRMs) play a central role in guiding inference-time scaling algorithms for large language models (LLMs). However, we observe that even state-of-the-art PRMs can be poorly calibrated and often overestimate success probabilities. To address this, we present a calibration approach, performed via quantile regression, that adjusts PRM outputs to better align with true success probabilities. Leveraging these calibrated success estimates and their associated confidence bounds, we introduce an \emph{instance-adaptive scaling} (IAS) framework that dynamically adjusts the inference budget based on the estimated likelihood that a partial reasoning trajectory will yield a correct final answer. Unlike conventional methods that allocate a fixed number of reasoning trajectories per query, this approach successfully adapts to each instance and reasoning step when using our calibrated PRMs. Experiments on mathematical reasoning benchmarks show that (i) our PRM calibration method successfully achieves small calibration error, outperforming the baseline methods, (ii) calibration is crucial for enabling effective adaptive scaling, and (iii) the proposed IAS strategy reduces inference costs while maintaining final answer accuracy, utilizing less compute on more confident problems as desired.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of miscalibration in Process Reward Models (PRMs), which are crucial for guiding inference-time scaling algorithms in Large Language Models (LLMs). The authors observe that even state-of-the-art PRMs can be poorly calibrated, overestimating success probabilities. To tackle this, they introduce a calibration approach using quantile regression to align PRM outputs with true success probabilities. They then propose an instance-adaptive scaling (IAS) framework that leverages these calibrated success estimates (and associated confidence bounds) to dynamically adjust the inference budget based on the likelihood of a reasoning trajectory yielding a correct final answer. The paper demonstrates that their approach reduces calibration error and inference costs while maintaining accuracy on mathematical reasoning benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in combining PRM calibration (using quantile regression, which is a good approach for this problem) with instance-adaptive scaling for LLM inference. While PRMs and adaptive sampling exist, the specific combination and the quantile regression-based calibration method for PRMs offer a novel contribution. Prior work on uncertainty-based adaptive sampling primarily focused on reinforcement learning or planning algorithms, not LLM inference-time scaling. The instance adaptive scaling is novel in the context of LLMs.

*   **Significance:** The significance is in several aspects:

    *   **Addresses a Practical Problem:** Miscalibration in PRMs is a real issue that limits their utility. Accurate uncertainty estimation is important for applications requiring safety monitoring, efficient resource allocation, and the "I don't know" functionality.
    *   **Improved Efficiency:** The proposed IAS framework has the potential to reduce inference costs (and therefore latency) by allocating compute resources more efficiently. This is particularly relevant as LLMs are increasingly deployed in resource-constrained environments. Quantile regression-based calibration is more robust compared to the alternatives.
    *   **Demonstrated Effectiveness:** The experimental results on mathematical reasoning benchmarks provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed method.
    *   **Contribution to Interpretability and Reliability:** This work highlights that beyond performance improvements, reliability and uncertainty quantification are important in PRM models.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-Motivated:** The paper clearly explains the problem of PRM miscalibration and its implications.
    *   **Sound Methodology:** The quantile regression-based calibration method is a sensible and effective approach.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experiments are well-designed and provide convincing evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed method. The baselines are appropriate, and the authors explore different LLMs, PRMs, and datasets.
    *   **Theoretical Analysis:** The theoretical analysis provides a useful characterization of the minimum number of samples needed for a given success probability.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The experiments are primarily focused on mathematical reasoning tasks. While these tasks are challenging and relevant, it would be beneficial to evaluate the method on other types of tasks, such as code generation or natural language understanding.
    *   **Reliance on PRMs:** The performance of the IAS framework depends on the quality of the underlying PRM. The paper does not address the problem of training PRMs from scratch but focuses on calibrating existing ones.
    *   **Practical Deployment Challenges:** While the method reduces inference costs, there might be practical challenges in deploying it in real-world applications. For example, the overhead of computing the calibrated success estimates and dynamically adjusting the inference budget could be significant. The assumption that trials are independent might not hold in practice.
    *   **Lack of comparison to the state-of-the-art inference-time scaling methods**: The paper does not present a comprehensive comparison with other inference-time scaling methods. While it builds upon BoN and beam search, it would have been helpful to compare its performance to other recent approaches that also aim to optimize the trade-off between accuracy and cost.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in several areas:

    *   **PRM Development:** It highlights the importance of calibration in PRM design.
    *   **Adaptive Inference:** It provides a practical framework for adaptive inference in LLMs.
    *   **Uncertainty Quantification:** It demonstrates the value of uncertainty quantification in LLM applications.
    *   **Efficient LLM Deployment:** The IAS framework could contribute to more efficient and cost-effective LLM deployments.
    *   **Further work**: Further investigation on the role of uncertainty is possible. Future works could explore uncertainty quantification tools.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to address a practical problem in the field of LLMs. The combination of quantile regression-based PRM calibration and instance-adaptive scaling is a significant contribution. While there are some limitations, such as the limited scope of the experiments and reliance on PRMs, the paper has the potential to influence future research in PRM development, adaptive inference, and uncertainty quantification. The experimental results are compelling, and the theoretical analysis provides useful insights. However, a comparison against the state-of-the-art alternative inference time scaling methods, or investigating the challenges in deploying into real-world systems would have elevated the contribution.

The 7.5 score reflects the positive impact that this paper can make by pushing the boundaries of model uncertainty calibration and adaptivity in LLMs. However, with these improvements and further explorations the paper could reach a score near 9 or 10.

- **Classification**: stat.ML
- **Score**: 7/10

### RePO: Replay-Enhanced Policy Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09340v1)
- **Authors**: Siheng Li, Zhanhui Zhou, Wai Lam, Chao Yang, Chaochao Lu
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning (RL) is vital for optimizing large language models (LLMs). Recent Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) estimates advantages using multiple on-policy outputs per prompt, leading to high computational costs and low data efficiency. To address this, we introduce Replay-Enhanced Policy Optimization (RePO), which leverages diverse replay strategies to retrieve off-policy samples from a replay buffer, allowing policy optimization based on a broader and more diverse set of samples for each prompt. Experiments on five LLMs across seven mathematical reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that RePO achieves absolute average performance gains of $18.4$ and $4.1$ points for Qwen2.5-Math-1.5B and Qwen3-1.7B, respectively, compared to GRPO. Further analysis indicates that RePO increases computational cost by $15\%$ while raising the number of effective optimization steps by $48\%$ for Qwen3-1.7B, with both on-policy and off-policy sample numbers set to $8$. The repository can be accessed at https://github.com/SihengLi99/RePO.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Replay-Enhanced Policy Optimization (RePO), an extension of Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) for reinforcement learning of large language models (LLMs). RePO addresses limitations in GRPO, such as high computational costs and low data efficiency, by incorporating off-policy updates using samples from a replay buffer. It combines on-policy and off-policy learning, leveraging diverse replay strategies to retrieve suitable samples from the replay buffer. Experiments on mathematical reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that RePO outperforms GRPO in several LLMs. The paper analyzes the computational overhead and effective optimization steps, showing RePO increases them by relatively low (15%) and relatively high (48%) rates, respectively.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of integrating a replay buffer and off-policy updates into GRPO is a valuable contribution. While reinforcement learning with replay buffers is not entirely novel in the broader RL literature, its specific application within the context of LLM optimization, particularly as an extension to GRPO, adds a new dimension. The exploration of diverse replay strategies tailored to the specific challenges of LLMs adds to the novelty. However, the paper lacks thorough exploration on several of the many possible off-policy replay strategies.
*   **Significance:** Improving the data efficiency and reducing the computational cost of RL fine-tuning for LLMs is highly significant. The paper demonstrates improvements in mathematical reasoning benchmarks and general reasoning tasks, suggesting RePO can be a valuable technique for enhancing LLM capabilities. The gains over GRPO, a reasonably recent and strong baseline, further enhance the significance. The paper's exploration of mixed vs. split methods for on-policy / off-policy updates are great additions, but again only lightly discussed.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Addresses important limitations of GRPO: computational cost and data efficiency.
    *   Combines on-policy and off-policy learning effectively.
    *   Introduces diverse replay strategies tailored to LLM optimization.
    *   Demonstrates empirical improvements over GRPO on various reasoning benchmarks and general tasks.
    * The core concept of REPO is easy to understand and can be implemented relatively easy.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited model size (7B) for exploration due to computational constraints. While understandable, this restricts the generalizability of the results to larger, more performant LLMs.
    *   Hyperparameter sensitivity analysis is limited due to computational cost. A more thorough exploration of hyperparameters, like the weighting of off-policy updates, would strengthen the findings.
    *   The paper could further explore the theoretical properties of RePO and why certain replay strategies perform better than others. It provides insights but lacks formal analysis.
    * The paper claims the experiments cover 6 general reasoning tasks, but only compares against GRPO in MMLU-Pro, ARC-Easy, ARC-Challenge, GPQA-Diamond, BBH, IFEval. This is inaccurate and misleading.
    * Claims such as 'the primary advantage of RePO lies in its ability to leverage outputs from previous steps' is not scientifically stated.

*   **Potential Influence:** RePO has the potential to influence the field of LLM optimization by providing a more efficient and flexible approach to reinforcement learning. The introduced replay strategies can be adopted and further refined by other researchers, and the core concept can be integrated into other RL methods.

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, I assign a score of:

Score: 7
**Rationale:**

The paper presents a valuable and novel extension to GRPO, addressing its limitations with a replay buffer and demonstrating empirical improvements. While the novelty of replay buffers in RL is limited, its application within the GRPO setting and the introduction of task-specific replay strategies make this a more significant work. The limitations around model size and hyperparameter exploration prevent a higher score. Future work should focus on extending RePO to larger models, more in-depth analysis of replay strategy choices, and a more thorough theoretical understanding of its properties.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Ming-Omni: A Unified Multimodal Model for Perception and Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09344v1)
- **Authors**: Inclusion AI, Biao Gong, Cheng Zou, Chuanyang Zheng, Chunluan Zhou, Canxiang Yan, Chunxiang Jin, Chunjie Shen, Dandan Zheng, Fudong Wang, Furong Xu, GuangMing Yao, Jun Zhou, Jingdong Chen, Jianxin Sun, Jiajia Liu, Jianjiang Zhu, Jun Peng, Kaixiang Ji, Kaiyou Song, Kaimeng Ren, Libin Wang, Lixiang Ru, Lele Xie, Longhua Tan, Lyuxin Xue, Lan Wang, Mochen Bai, Ning Gao, Pei Chen, Qingpei Guo, Qinglong Zhang, Qiang Xu, Rui Liu, Ruijie Xiong, Sirui Gao, Tinghao Liu, Taisong Li, Weilong Chai, Xinyu Xiao, Xiaomei Wang, Xiaoxue Chen, Xiao Lu, Xiaoyu Li, Xingning Dong, Xuzheng Yu, Yi Yuan, Yuting Gao, Yunxiao Sun, Yipeng Chen, Yifei Wu, Yongjie Lyu, Ziping Ma, Zipeng Feng, Zhijiang Fang, Zhihao Qiu, Ziyuan Huang, Zhengyu He
- **Abstract**: We propose Ming-Omni, a unified multimodal model capable of processing images, text, audio, and video, while demonstrating strong proficiency in both speech and image generation. Ming-Omni employs dedicated encoders to extract tokens from different modalities, which are then processed by Ling, an MoE architecture equipped with newly proposed modality-specific routers. This design enables a single model to efficiently process and fuse multimodal inputs within a unified framework, thereby facilitating diverse tasks without requiring separate models, task-specific fine-tuning, or structural redesign. Importantly, Ming-Omni extends beyond conventional multimodal models by supporting audio and image generation. This is achieved through the integration of an advanced audio decoder for natural-sounding speech and Ming-Lite-Uni for high-quality image generation, which also allow the model to engage in context-aware chatting, perform text-to-speech conversion, and conduct versatile image editing. Our experimental results showcase Ming-Omni offers a powerful solution for unified perception and generation across all modalities. Notably, our proposed Ming-Omni is the first open-source model we are aware of to match GPT-4o in modality support, and we release all code and model weights to encourage further research and development in the community.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "Ming-Omni: A Unified Multimodal Model for Perception and Generation" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Ming-Omni, a unified multimodal model capable of processing images, text, audio, and video. The model demonstrates proficiency in both perception and generation across these modalities. It uses dedicated encoders for each modality and an MoE architecture called Ling with modality-specific routers for processing the combined input. Ming-Omni extends beyond conventional models by supporting audio and image generation through the integration of an advanced audio decoder and Ming-Lite-Uni. The paper claims Ming-Omni matches GPT-4o in modality support and emphasizes its open-source nature.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Modality Coverage:**  The model addresses a broad range of modalities, including audio and video generation, which are not always present in other multimodal models.
    *   **Unified Architecture:** The use of a unified architecture with MoE routing is a desirable feature, potentially simplifying training and inference compared to models requiring modality-specific components.
    *   **Open-Source:** The open-source availability is a significant strength, promoting reproducibility, further research, and community contributions.
    *   **Performance Claims:** The paper makes strong performance claims, citing comparable results to Qwen2.5-VL and surpassing SDXL in certain aspects. The text also highlights the relatively small size, 2.8B, of the model.
    *   **Details on Modality-Specific Components:** It provides some detail on the audio decoder and image generation modules.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Novelty:** The paper builds upon existing components like Ling (the MoE LLM), Qwen2.5, Whisper and Ming-Lite-Uni. While the combination and specific training strategies might be novel, the core building blocks are not fundamentally new. It appears to be clever component assembly and task-specific tuning.
    *   **Limited Technical Depth:**  While it describes the overall architecture, the paper lacks crucial implementation details. How exactly is the modality-specific routing implemented? What specific loss functions are used for the stepwise balancing strategy?  How does the lightweight bridging framework work for image generation? These are essential for reproducibility and understanding.
    *   **Insufficient Ablation Studies:** There's a lack of ablation studies to demonstrate the effectiveness of individual components and training strategies. It would be beneficial to see how the stepwise balancing strategy contributes to improved performance.
    *   **Limited Analysis of Failure Cases:** The paper focuses primarily on positive results. Analyzing failure cases would provide valuable insight into the model's limitations and areas for improvement.
    *   **Clarity Regarding Authorship:** While stating full authorship contributions at the beginning, such a statement is often associated with less novelty for the paper at large, and often indicates the work would be better conveyed in a technical report.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The model's ability to handle a diverse set of modalities and tasks within a unified framework is a significant step towards more versatile AI systems.
    *   The emphasis on generation in multiple modalities, especially audio, is a valuable contribution.
    *   The open-source nature of the project has the potential to foster research and development in the field.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a relatively solid contribution, but there are some issues with novelty. The model integrates several existing components in a unified architecture. While the training strategies and architecture are potentially novel, the details provided are insufficient to fully assess their significance. The lack of detailed ablation studies also makes it difficult to evaluate the effectiveness of the individual components and strategies. The performance claims are promising, but more rigorous comparisons are needed. However, the breadth of modalities it covers, in particular speech and audio, along with the emphasis of it being open-source, does contribute to its value.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### OmniDRCA: Parallel Speech-Text Foundation Model via Dual-Resolution Speech Representations and Contrastive Alignment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09349v1)
- **Authors**: Chao-Hong Tan, Qian Chen, Wen Wang, Chong Deng, Qinglin Zhang, Luyao Cheng, Hai Yu, Xin Zhang, Xiang Lv, Tianyu Zhao, Chong Zhang, Yukun Ma, Yafeng Chen, Hui Wang, Jiaqing Liu, Jieping Ye
- **Abstract**: Recent studies on end-to-end speech generation with large language models (LLMs) have attracted significant community attention, with multiple works extending text-based LLMs to generate discrete speech tokens. Existing approaches primarily fall into two categories: (1) Methods that generate discrete speech tokens independently without incorporating them into the LLM's autoregressive process, resulting in text generation being unaware of concurrent speech synthesis. (2) Models that generate interleaved or parallel speech-text tokens through joint autoregressive modeling, enabling mutual modality awareness during generation. This paper presents OmniDRCA, a parallel speech-text foundation model based on joint autoregressive modeling, featuring dual-resolution speech representations and contrastive cross-modal alignment. Our approach processes speech and text representations in parallel while enhancing audio comprehension through contrastive alignment. Experimental results on Spoken Question Answering benchmarks demonstrate that OmniDRCA establishes new state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance among parallel joint speech-text modeling based foundation models, and achieves competitive performance compared to interleaved models. Additionally, we explore the potential of extending the framework to full-duplex conversational scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "OmniDRCA: Parallel Speech-Text Foundation Model via Dual-Resolution Speech Representations and Contrastive Alignment":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces OmniDRCA, a novel parallel speech-text foundation model designed for end-to-end speech generation. It addresses the challenge of aligning speech and text modalities by employing two key innovations: 1) Dual-Resolution Speech Representations (DRSR) to mitigate the temporal resolution discrepancy between speech and text tokens, and 2) Contrastive Cross-modal Alignment (CCA) to enhance semantic consistency between modalities before inputting them into the LLM. The model processes speech and text in parallel and the results are shown to achieve state-of-the-art or competitive performance on spoken question answering benchmarks compared to existing parallel and interleaved models.  The paper further explores the model's potential in full-duplex conversational scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the combination of DRSR and CCA within a parallel speech-text foundation model framework. While individual components like contrastive learning and dual-resolution approaches are not entirely novel in themselves, their integration in this specific architecture and for this particular problem (speech-text alignment in parallel generation) seems to provide incremental value.

*   **Significance:** The significance is moderate. The improvements in performance are notable, particularly compared to existing parallel models. The work shows a promising direction for improving end-to-end speech generation, which is a field with a growing interest. The exploration of full-duplex voice conversation is also a valuable addition, although briefly described. The paper is clearly written, and the experiments are well-designed to highlight the contributions of the proposed architecture. The thorough ablation studies lend weight to the findings.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The clear problem definition and targeted solutions are well-articulated.
    *   The DRSR addresses a tangible limitation in speech-text alignment.
    *   The combination of DRSR and CCA is synergistic and leads to performance improvements.
    *   The authors provide good experimental validation and ablation studies.
    * The study includes a thorough comparison with representative models

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The individual components (DRSR, CCA) have antecedents in prior work; the combination is novel but the gains should be more significant given the prior work.
    *   The full-duplex exploration is brief and lacks detailed results, somewhat diminishing its impact.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can serve as a stepping stone for future research in parallel speech-text modeling. The DRSR approach might be adopted or adapted by other researchers in the field. The insights from the ablation studies could inform the design of future models.

**Justification for Score:**

A score of **6** is assigned. This is an above-average score, reflecting a solid contribution to the field. The paper presents a well-engineered system with clear performance gains, especially in comparison to prior parallel modeling approaches. The components, while not revolutionary individually, are thoughtfully combined to address a specific challenge. The weaknesses are that the gains are somewhat incremental over existing approaches and the full-duplex capabilities are briefly presented.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Autoregressive Adversarial Post-Training for Real-Time Interactive Video Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09350v1)
- **Authors**: Shanchuan Lin, Ceyuan Yang, Hao He, Jianwen Jiang, Yuxi Ren, Xin Xia, Yang Zhao, Xuefeng Xiao, Lu Jiang
- **Abstract**: Existing large-scale video generation models are computationally intensive, preventing adoption in real-time and interactive applications. In this work, we propose autoregressive adversarial post-training (AAPT) to transform a pre-trained latent video diffusion model into a real-time, interactive video generator. Our model autoregressively generates a latent frame at a time using a single neural function evaluation (1NFE). The model can stream the result to the user in real time and receive interactive responses as controls to generate the next latent frame. Unlike existing approaches, our method explores adversarial training as an effective paradigm for autoregressive generation. This not only allows us to design an architecture that is more efficient for one-step generation while fully utilizing the KV cache, but also enables training the model in a student-forcing manner that proves to be effective in reducing error accumulation during long video generation. Our experiments demonstrate that our 8B model achieves real-time, 24fps, streaming video generation at 736x416 resolution on a single H100, or 1280x720 on 8xH100 up to a minute long (1440 frames). Visit our research website at https://seaweed-apt.com/2
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Autoregressive Adversarial Post-Training (AAPT), a method to transform pre-trained latent video diffusion models into real-time, interactive video generators. AAPT autoregressively generates latent frames using a single neural function evaluation (1NFE), allowing for real-time streaming of video and interactive control. The approach uses adversarial training, which improves efficiency and enables student-forcing training to reduce error accumulation during long video generation.  Experiments demonstrate real-time, 24fps streaming video generation at 736x416 resolution on a single H100 GPU, and longer videos (up to a minute) at higher resolutions using multiple GPUs. The method is shown to be effective in image-to-video generation, particularly in pose-conditioned virtual human generation and camera-controlled world exploration.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The use of adversarial training for autoregressive video generation, particularly for converting diffusion models to real-time generators, is relatively novel. The student-forcing approach within adversarial training to mitigate error accumulation in long videos is a significant contribution. However, APT (Adversarial Post Training) as a concept already exists, so the novelty is mainly in the adaptation to the autoregressive context, and addressing the temporal coherence/error accumulation problems for streaming video.

*   **Significance:** The main significance lies in achieving real-time interactive video generation with high resolution and long duration using a relatively efficient architecture. This makes it closer to real-world interactive applications, compared to prior work that often sacrificed quality or resolution for speed. The ability to generate minute-long videos consistently is a substantial improvement. The results are promising and show a clear advantage in terms of speed and duration, at least compared to directly extending existing video generation techniques. The ablation studies provide insights into the importance of long-video training and the advantages of student-forcing. The application demonstrations are also useful in visualizing the potential of the method. However, it's worth noting that large-scale video models are evolving rapidly and, given the long lead times for research and publication, parts of the paper are likely to already be outdated. The real-time aspect is interesting, but the reliance on multiple H100 GPUs makes it less practically accessible for many real-world applications.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Addresses a critical problem: the computational expense of video generation.
    *   Demonstrates a concrete solution: AAPT.
    *   Achieves significant performance gains in terms of speed, latency, and duration.
    *   Provides strong empirical results on standard benchmarks.
    *   Includes ablation studies to understand the impact of design choices.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Relies on access to specialized hardware (H100 GPUs), which limits widespread adoption. The comparison suffers from comparing against closed-source/difficult-to-reproduce baseline.
    *   While the adversarial approach is beneficial, the quality may be limited by inherent challenges in using GANs/adversarial training for video (e.g., mode collapse, instability). The experiments show that the quality improvement is only for a short clip and degrades after long generation.
    *   The method is built on existing foundational models, which may limit its long-term impact. The novelty is incremental rather than revolutionary.
    *   Long training times.
    *   Subject and scene consistency still a weakness.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research directions towards more efficient video generation architectures, especially those focusing on real-time interactive applications. The findings regarding adversarial training and student-forcing could be adopted in other generative models. It also adds to the growing body of literature demonstrating the application of LLMs for video generation.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a significant engineering achievement in optimizing video generation for real-time applications. It effectively combines existing techniques (APT, diffusion models) with novel approaches (student-forcing in adversarial training, specific architectural adaptations for autoregressive generation) to achieve compelling results. However, it suffers from heavy hardware requirements and relies on relatively incremental improvements compared to foundational breakthroughs. The consistency problems are also a concern that suggests the method isn't entirely robust. The reliance on existing diffusion models also limits the long-term originality of the approach. Despite these limitations, the paper makes a valuable contribution to the field, demonstrating a clear path toward more practical interactive video generation systems.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### DIVE into MoE: Diversity-Enhanced Reconstruction of Large Language Models from Dense into Mixture-of-Experts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09351v1)
- **Authors**: Yuchen Feng, Bowen Shen, Naibin Gu, Jiaxuan Zhao, Peng Fu, Zheng Lin, Weiping Wang
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) with the Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture achieve high cost-efficiency by selectively activating a subset of the parameters. Despite the inference efficiency of MoE LLMs, the training of extensive experts from scratch incurs substantial overhead, whereas reconstructing a dense LLM into an MoE LLM significantly reduces the training budget. However, existing reconstruction methods often overlook the diversity among experts, leading to potential redundancy. In this paper, we come up with the observation that a specific LLM exhibits notable diversity after being pruned on different calibration datasets, based on which we present a Diversity-Enhanced reconstruction method named DIVE. The recipe of DIVE includes domain affinity mining, pruning-based expert reconstruction, and efficient retraining. Specifically, the reconstruction includes pruning and reassembly of the feed-forward network (FFN) module. After reconstruction, we efficiently retrain the model on routers, experts and normalization modules. We implement DIVE on Llama-style LLMs with open-source training corpora. Experiments show that DIVE achieves training efficiency with minimal accuracy trade-offs, outperforming existing pruning and MoE reconstruction methods with the same number of activated parameters.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "DIVE into MoE: Diversity-Enhanced Reconstruction of Large Language Models from Dense into Mixture-of-Experts" introduces DIVE, a method for converting dense Large Language Models (LLMs) into Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models. DIVE aims to improve upon existing reconstruction techniques by focusing on enhancing the diversity among experts, which the authors argue is often overlooked, leading to redundancy and sub-optimal performance. The method involves three key steps: domain affinity mining to identify suitable training datasets for each expert, pruning-based expert reconstruction to create specialized experts from the dense LLM, and an efficient retraining pipeline that selectively updates only routers, experts, and normalization layers using Parameter Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT). The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of DIVE on Llama-style LLMs, showing that it achieves better training efficiency and minimal accuracy trade-offs compared to existing pruning and MoE reconstruction methods with a similar number of activated parameters.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's main contribution lies in its emphasis on expert diversity during the dense-to-MoE reconstruction process. While the individual components, like pruning and PEFT, are not entirely new, the specific combination and application of these techniques with a focus on domain affinity to foster diversity constitutes a novel contribution. The observation that domain-specific calibration datasets can lead to more diverse experts is a worthwhile insight.
    However, the novelty is somewhat incremental. Other works have also explored MoE reconstruction. The technical novelty is in how they utilize existing techniques to enhance diversity. The paper successfully shows diversity matters; this is a useful observation.
*   **Significance:** If the method holds up with larger LLMs, DIVE offers a practical approach to creating efficient MoE models from pre-trained dense LLMs, which has significant implications for reducing training costs and enabling the deployment of LLMs in resource-constrained environments. Reducing costs with diversity provides both economic and engineering benefit. By leveraging the inherent diversity present in dense LLMs, DIVE can potentially sidestep the instability issues commonly associated with training MoE models from scratch. DIVE provides both speed and diversity; this enhances the overall MoE ecosystem.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed method and its components.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of DIVE in terms of both training efficiency and downstream task performance. The authors provide comparisons against relevant baselines, including LLM-Pruner, FLAP, and LLaMA-MoE.
    *   The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of domain affinity mining and efficient retraining, strengthening the validity of the approach.
    *   The analysis of routing distributions provides evidence that DIVE successfully creates diverse experts that specialize in different domains.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The experiments are limited to relatively smaller LLMs (TinyLlama-1.1B). While the results are promising, it is unclear whether DIVE will scale effectively to larger models, which are currently the focus of much research and development effort.
    *   The paper can benefit from better explanation of how the number of experts is chosen, and the correlation between #experts and #clusters obtained from domain affinity mining. It appears somewhat arbitrarily set to N, but what happens if there are far fewer domain clusters compared to N? How does DIVE handle scenarios where the desired number of experts exceeds the number of well-defined domain clusters?
    *   The reliance on pre-defined tasks for expert creation may limit the potential for emergent expert specialization. The algorithm is constrained to pre-existing task categories.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a useful contribution to the field of MoE model construction, but has limitations. The experimental results on smaller LLMs are promising, demonstrating that DIVE can improve training efficiency and downstream task performance by leveraging expert diversity. The focus on diversity is a valuable direction, and the technical approach of combining domain affinity mining, pruning-based expert reconstruction, and efficient retraining appears to be effective. However, the paper suffers from the limitation of being demonstrated primarily on TinyLlama-1.1B and a lack of thorough examination of the implications and handling of varying expert/cluster counts.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### "Is This Really a Human Peer Supporter?": Misalignments Between Peer Supporters and Experts in LLM-Supported Interactions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09354v1)
- **Authors**: Kellie Yu Hui Sim, Roy Ka-Wei Lee, Kenny Tsu Wei Choo
- **Abstract**: Mental health is a growing global concern, prompting interest in AI-driven solutions to expand access to psychosocial support. Peer support, grounded in lived experience, offers a valuable complement to professional care. However, variability in training, effectiveness, and definitions raises concerns about quality, consistency, and safety. Large Language Models (LLMs) present new opportunities to enhance peer support interactions, particularly in real-time, text-based interactions. We present and evaluate an AI-supported system with an LLM-simulated distressed client, context-sensitive LLM-generated suggestions, and real-time emotion visualisations. 2 mixed-methods studies with 12 peer supporters and 5 mental health professionals (i.e., experts) examined the system's effectiveness and implications for practice. Both groups recognised its potential to enhance training and improve interaction quality. However, we found a key tension emerged: while peer supporters engaged meaningfully, experts consistently flagged critical issues in peer supporter responses, such as missed distress cues and premature advice-giving. This misalignment highlights potential limitations in current peer support training, especially in emotionally charged contexts where safety and fidelity to best practices are essential. Our findings underscore the need for standardised, psychologically grounded training, especially as peer support scales globally. They also demonstrate how LLM-supported systems can scaffold this development--if designed with care and guided by expert oversight. This work contributes to emerging conversations on responsible AI integration in mental health and the evolving role of LLMs in augmenting peer-delivered care.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) to support peer support interactions for mental health. The authors present and evaluate an AI-supported system featuring an LLM-simulated distressed client, context-sensitive LLM-generated suggestions for peer supporters, and real-time emotion visualizations.  Two mixed-methods studies were conducted, involving peer supporters and mental health professionals (experts), to assess the system's effectiveness and implications for practice. The study reveals a tension: while peer supporters found the system useful, experts identified issues in their responses (missed cues, premature advice), suggesting limitations in current peer support training. The authors argue for standardized, psychologically grounded training and demonstrate how LLM-supported systems can scaffold this development with careful design and expert oversight.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by directly comparing the perceptions and practices of trained peer supporters with those of mental health experts when using LLM-supported tools. This is crucial, as previous work often focuses on technical capabilities or professional settings. Identifying the misalignment between peer support practices and professional expectations is a significant finding. Furthermore, embedding the study within a Southeast Asian context adds to its novelty by addressing the Western-centric bias in much of the existing literature on peer support and AI. However, the system itself, comprising an LLM simulator, suggestions, and visualization is not entirely novel on its own, similar concepts are existing before. The key novelty lies in the specific combination and comparison of use.

*   **Significance/Impact:** The paper addresses a timely and important problem: scaling access to mental health support. The use of LLMs in this domain is actively being explored, making this study highly relevant.  The findings regarding training limitations and the need for standardized approaches are particularly important as peer support scales globally. The study's emphasis on culturally sensitive design and expert oversight provides valuable guidance for future system development. By highlighting the potential benefits and pitfalls of integrating AI in peer-led care, the research contributes meaningfully to the ongoing discussion on responsible AI in mental health.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Triangulation:** The use of mixed methods (user studies, expert reviews) strengthens the validity and reliability of the findings.
    *   **Real-world relevance:** The study investigates a real-world problem with direct implications for mental health care.
    *   **Nuanced analysis:** The paper goes beyond simply assessing whether participants *like* the system and delves into how they engage with it, revealing important contextual factors and tensions.
    *   **Clear design recommendations:** The study outlines concrete design considerations for responsible AI integration.
    *   **Emphasis on safety and ethics:** The authors acknowledge the ethical considerations and the need for expert oversight in AI-augmented peer support.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Controlled setting:** The studies were conducted in a lab setting, limiting generalizability to real-world peer support environments.
    *   **Client Simulation:** The LLM-simulated client, while designed to be realistic, may not fully capture the complexities of real distress. This could have influenced the expert ratings as well as the peer supporter's response.
    *   **Limited sample size:** Although sufficient for exploratory study, the sample size of 12 peer supporters and 5 experts is relatively small. Larger studies would strengthen the findings and allow for more robust statistical analysis.
    *   **Subjective Evaluation:** The assessment of LLM-generated responses, both by peer supporters and experts, relies on their judgement of "appropriateness" or "alignment". The assessment might need an objective metrics, such as evaluating the presence of certain phrases (i.e. "I'm here to listen").
    *   **Reverse Turing Phenomenon:** The study mentions the reverse Turing phenomenon as a result, but further in-depth analysis of the text and audio is required.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the design of future AI-supported peer support systems by emphasizing the need for standardized training, expert oversight, and culturally sensitive design. It also contributes to the broader discussion of responsible AI in mental health.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** The paper makes a compelling case for the need to balance structured guidance with conversational flexibility in LLM-supported peer support. By uncovering the misalignment between peer support practices and expert expectations, the research highlights potential limitations in current training models and emphasizes the need for standardized, psychologically grounded training programs, potentially using LLM-supported systems as a scaffold.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:** This paper offers a valuable and timely contribution to the field of AI and mental health, with direct relevance to peer support practices. It is methodologically sound, presents nuanced findings, and provides concrete recommendations for future system development and training. While the study is limited by its controlled setting and sample size, the novelty lies in its direct comparison of peer supporter and expert perspectives, which is an important step towards more responsible and effective integration of AI in mental healthcare. The study is more than incremental; It provides a nuanced and insightful analysis that advances understanding and offers practical guidance, meriting a score above the midpoint.

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Taming SQL Complexity: LLM-Based Equivalence Evaluation for Text-to-SQL
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09359v1)
- **Authors**: Qingyun Zeng, Simin Ma, Arash Niknafs, Ashish Basran, Carol Szabo
- **Abstract**: The rise of Large Language Models (LLMs) has significantly advanced Text-to-SQL (NL2SQL) systems, yet evaluating the semantic equivalence of generated SQL remains a challenge, especially given ambiguous user queries and multiple valid SQL interpretations. This paper explores using LLMs to assess both semantic and a more practical "weak" semantic equivalence. We analyze common patterns of SQL equivalence and inequivalence, discuss challenges in LLM-based evaluation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of evaluating the semantic equivalence of SQL queries generated by Text-to-SQL (NL2SQL) systems, particularly in light of advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs).  It argues that traditional metrics like Execution Accuracy (EX) are flawed and proposes a comprehensive LLM-based framework for SQL equivalence assessment. This framework includes:

1.  **Characterization of SQL Equivalence:** Systematically classifying patterns of equivalent and inequivalent SQL queries, distinguishing between strict semantic equivalence and a more practical "weak" equivalence relevant to business applications.
2.  **LLM-Based Evaluation Pipeline:**  A detailed pipeline incorporating preprocessing, efficient string-based matching for unambiguous cases, and sophisticated LLM reasoning (using GPT-4 variants).  Key features include a multi-run strategy for stability and techniques like query rewriting and the "Miniature & Mull" prompting strategy.
3.  **Experimental Evaluation:**  Thorough evaluation using three datasets: a manually labeled real-world Dataverse dataset, a targeted development set, and a comprehensive synthetic dataset. The results demonstrate the effectiveness of LLMs, quantify the impact of different prompting strategies, and highlight trade-offs.

The paper aims to improve the reliability of SQL equivalence evaluation, leading to more accurate and trustworthy Text-to-SQL systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies in its comprehensive approach to SQL equivalence assessment using LLMs.  While other works have explored LLMs for SQL evaluation, this paper distinguishes itself by:
    *   The detailed characterization of SQL equivalence patterns (both equivalent and inequivalent). This provides a structured foundation for creating robust evaluation methodologies.
    *   The explicit distinction and analysis of "weak" equivalence, which is crucial for practical applications but often overlooked in academic benchmarks.
    *   The combination of preprocessing, string-based matching, and LLM reasoning within a single, well-defined pipeline.
    *   The use of a diverse set of datasets including real-world, development, and synthetic datasets, addressing the limitations of relying solely on academic benchmarks.
    *   The integration and evaluation of advanced prompting techniques like "Miniature & Mull" and query rewriting within the pipeline.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential to improve the reliability of Text-to-SQL systems. Accurate SQL evaluation is a bottleneck in developing better NL2SQL tools.  By providing a more nuanced and practical approach to equivalence assessment, this research could:
    *   Facilitate the development of more accurate and robust NL2SQL systems, especially in real-world business applications where "good enough" solutions are acceptable.
    *   Improve the evaluation of existing systems.
    *   Provide a valuable resource (the SQL pattern characterization) for researchers working on SQL analysis and generation.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Approach:** The paper tackles the problem from multiple angles (pattern characterization, pipeline design, dataset creation, experimental evaluation).
    *   **Practical Focus:** The attention to "weak" equivalence and the use of a real-world Dataverse dataset makes the research highly relevant to practical applications.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper provides a detailed analysis of the strengths and weaknesses of different LLM-based evaluation techniques.
    *   **Well-Defined Methodology:**  The evaluation pipeline is clearly defined, allowing for reproducibility and further refinement.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependence on LLMs:**  The reliance on LLMs introduces challenges like consistency and robustness, which the paper acknowledges but cannot fully eliminate. The paper's reliance on GPT-4 makes the results potentially susceptible to future changes in the model's behavior or availability.
    *   **Generalizability:**  While the Dataverse dataset adds practical relevance, the findings might not be directly generalizable to other SQL dialects or application domains.
    *   **Complexity:**  The pipeline's complexity (multiple stages, prompting techniques) could make it difficult for other researchers to replicate or adapt the entire framework.
    *   **Limited Comparison:** While the paper motivates the need for a better evaluation method and offers its approach, it has limited direct comparison to other similar existing LLM-based evalutors.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in SQL evaluation and NL2SQL systems. The detailed characterization of SQL equivalence patterns and the comprehensive LLM-based pipeline can serve as a valuable starting point for other researchers. The paper's emphasis on practical equivalence could also shift the focus of evaluation metrics from strict accuracy to "usefulness" in real-world applications.

* **Justification of Score:** Considering the identified strengths and weaknesses, the novelty and significance of this work is reasonably good. It provides a robust framework for SQL semantic equivalence with clear practical implementations. However, it is not completely ground-breaking as it builds on the previous existing research and is primarily centered on GPT-4. It has several aspects of concern such as dependency on the model, data generalizability, and complexity.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 4/10

### SAGE: Exploring the Boundaries of Unsafe Concept Domain with Semantic-Augment Erasing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09363v1)
- **Authors**: Hongguang Zhu, Yunchao Wei, Mengyu Wang, Siyu Jiao, Yan Fang, Jiannan Huang, Yao Zhao
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models (DMs) have achieved significant progress in text-to-image generation. However, the inevitable inclusion of sensitive information during pre-training poses safety risks, such as unsafe content generation and copyright infringement. Concept erasing finetunes weights to unlearn undesirable concepts, and has emerged as a promising solution. However, existing methods treat unsafe concept as a fixed word and repeatedly erase it, trapping DMs in ``word concept abyss'', which prevents generalized concept-related erasing. To escape this abyss, we introduce semantic-augment erasing which transforms concept word erasure into concept domain erasure by the cyclic self-check and self-erasure. It efficiently explores and unlearns the boundary representation of concept domain through semantic spatial relationships between original and training DMs, without requiring additional preprocessed data. Meanwhile, to mitigate the retention degradation of irrelevant concepts while erasing unsafe concepts, we further propose the global-local collaborative retention mechanism that combines global semantic relationship alignment with local predicted noise preservation, effectively expanding the retentive receptive field for irrelevant concepts. We name our method SAGE, and extensive experiments demonstrate the comprehensive superiority of SAGE compared with other methods in the safe generation of DMs. The code and weights will be open-sourced at https://github.com/KevinLight831/SAGE.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "SAGE: Exploring the Boundaries of Unsafe Concept Domain with Semantic-Augment Erasing" introduces a method for removing unsafe concepts from text-to-image diffusion models (DMs). The core idea is to move beyond erasing specific words associated with unsafe concepts and instead explore and erase the broader *domain* of these concepts. SAGE employs a cyclic self-check and self-erasure process, where the model generates attack prompts to test its own safety and then fine-tunes itself to address vulnerabilities. Key components include:
*   Semantic-augment erasing:  Transforms word concept erasure into concept domain erasure through iterative self-checking.
*   Global-local collaborative retention: Preserves irrelevant concepts during erasure by aligning global semantic relationships and predicting local noise.
The authors demonstrate that SAGE outperforms existing methods in generating safe images while maintaining the generation quality of unrelated concepts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of exploring the *concept domain* instead of just erasing specific words is a significant step forward. Previous concept erasure methods often get stuck in a "word concept abyss" because they are limited to specific words or phrases. The cyclic self-check and self-erasure mechanism using a model's internal knowledge is innovative.The application of inside-out technique, using semantic embeddings, is another new aspect as opposed to random perturbations in the embedding space.The addition of a global-local strategy for retaining non-target concepts represents a good trade-off by retaining the semantic relationship while still taking local context into account.

*   **Significance:** The ability to remove unsafe content from diffusion models is crucial for responsible AI development and deployment. SAGE offers a more robust and generalizable approach than previous methods, which are often vulnerable to attack prompts or result in unintended erasure of unrelated concepts.The training efficiency aspect, through removing the need for multi-stage unet denoising, is also a beneficial aspect.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong technical contribution:**  The proposed method is well-designed and theoretically sound. The semantic augmentation and global-local retention mechanisms are clearly defined.
    *   **Comprehensive experiments:** The paper includes extensive experiments that demonstrate the effectiveness of SAGE in different scenarios (nudity erasure, style erasure). The comparisons with existing methods are thorough and fair.
    *   **Well-written and clear:** The paper is well-structured and easy to understand, making it accessible to a broad audience.
    *   The zero-shot transfer capability is well demonstrated, allowing models to be trained on one dataset and immediately transferred to another.
    *   Addresses a *real-world* problem in the field of responsible AI.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Complexity:** The method involves several components and hyperparameter settings, which might make it challenging to implement and tune for different applications.  A deeper discussion of the sensitivity of the method to different hyperparameters would be valuable.
    *   **Potential for unintended consequences:**  While the global-local retention mechanism helps, there's always a risk that concept domain erasure might have unintended consequences on the model's ability to generate certain types of images. This aspect could be explored further.
    *   **Reliance on CLIP:** The method relies on the CLIP model for semantic embeddings. While CLIP is a powerful model, it also has its own biases and limitations.
    *   The paper would benefit from analysis on how the method performs with longer chains of words, and in what capacity this method fails to do concept domain erasure.

*   **Potential Influence:** SAGE has the potential to significantly impact the field of safe AI generation. Its novel approach to concept domain erasure could inspire new research directions and lead to the development of more robust and generalizable safety mechanisms for diffusion models. The method's efficiency also makes it a practical solution for real-world applications. The impact is further amplified by the ability of training only on the text-encoder and transferring this to other models that use the same architecture.

*   **Score Justification:**
I am assigning a score of 8.5 to this paper. SAGE offers a significant improvement over existing concept erasure methods, both in terms of robustness and generalizability. The concept domain erasure and the iterative self-check methods are novel contributions. The thorough experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of SAGE, however the aforementioned complexities and reliance on CLIP, and the analysis of performance in different lengths of chains of words prevents the paper from achieving an even higher score. Despite these minor limitations, the paper makes a valuable contribution to the field and has the potential to influence future research in safe AI generation.

**Score: 8.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Anomaly Detection and Generation with Diffusion Models: A Survey
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09368v1)
- **Authors**: Yang Liu, Jing Liu, Chengfang Li, Rui Xi, Wenchao Li, Liang Cao, Jin Wang, Laurence T. Yang, Junsong Yuan, Wei Zhou
- **Abstract**: Anomaly detection (AD) plays a pivotal role across diverse domains, including cybersecurity, finance, healthcare, and industrial manufacturing, by identifying unexpected patterns that deviate from established norms in real-world data. Recent advancements in deep learning, specifically diffusion models (DMs), have sparked significant interest due to their ability to learn complex data distributions and generate high-fidelity samples, offering a robust framework for unsupervised AD. In this survey, we comprehensively review anomaly detection and generation with diffusion models (ADGDM), presenting a tutorial-style analysis of the theoretical foundations and practical implementations and spanning images, videos, time series, tabular, and multimodal data. Crucially, unlike existing surveys that often treat anomaly detection and generation as separate problems, we highlight their inherent synergistic relationship. We reveal how DMs enable a reinforcing cycle where generation techniques directly address the fundamental challenge of anomaly data scarcity, while detection methods provide critical feedback to improve generation fidelity and relevance, advancing both capabilities beyond their individual potential. A detailed taxonomy categorizes ADGDM methods based on anomaly scoring mechanisms, conditioning strategies, and architectural designs, analyzing their strengths and limitations. We final discuss key challenges including scalability and computational efficiency, and outline promising future directions such as efficient architectures, conditioning strategies, and integration with foundation models (e.g., visual-language models and large language models). By synthesizing recent advances and outlining open research questions, this survey aims to guide researchers and practitioners in leveraging DMs for innovative AD solutions across diverse applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper draft:

**Summary:**

The survey paper provides a comprehensive review of anomaly detection and generation using diffusion models (ADGDM). It addresses the crucial role of anomaly detection across various domains and highlights the recent surge of interest in diffusion models due to their ability to learn complex data distributions and generate high-fidelity samples, making them suitable for unsupervised AD. Unlike other surveys, this one emphasizes the synergistic relationship between anomaly *detection* and *generation*, where generation techniques help address the scarcity of anomaly data, and detection methods improve generation fidelity. It categorizes ADGDM methods based on anomaly scoring mechanisms, conditioning strategies, and architectural designs. The paper also discusses challenges like scalability and computational efficiency, and outlines future directions, such as efficient architectures and integration with foundation models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty of this survey lies in its integrated perspective of anomaly detection and generation within the diffusion model framework. Existing surveys often treat these as separate problems. Highlighting their synergistic relationship (generation addressing data scarcity, detection informing better generation) is a significant contribution.  The taxonomy, covering scoring mechanisms and conditioning strategies, is also a valuable addition, providing a structured overview.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a timely and relevant topic. Diffusion models have rapidly gained popularity, and their application to anomaly detection is a promising area of research. By providing a unified view and roadmap, the survey can guide researchers and practitioners in leveraging DMs for innovative AD solutions. Furthermore, highlighting the limitations of existing techniques and suggesting future research directions, such as integration with foundation models (LLMs), has strong potential to influence the research agenda.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Coverage:** The paper covers a wide range of data modalities (images, videos, time series, tabular, multimodal), showcasing the broad applicability of ADGDM.
    *   **Synergistic Perspective:** The emphasis on the interplay between detection and generation is a clear strength, distinguishing it from other surveys.
    *   **Well-Structured Taxonomy:** The categorization of methods provides a clear understanding of the methodological landscape.
    *   **Clear Roadmap for Future Research:** The identification of challenges and future directions is valuable for guiding future work in the field.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Implementation Details:**  While the survey covers various methods, it lacks in-depth implementation details for practical application.  Researchers looking to implement ADGDM may still need to delve into the original papers for specifics.
    *   **Limited Quantitative Comparison:** Performance tables are presented, however, a broader, more comprehensive comparative analysis across different datasets and modalities (with discussion regarding the challenges in doing so) would have strengthened the practical implications.
    *   **Future Directions Could Be More Specific:** Although future directions are outlined, some lack sufficient detail. For instance, "integration with LLMs" could benefit from concrete examples or specific areas where LLMs can improve ADGDM.

*   **Influence on the Field:** The paper has a good chance of becoming a reference point for researchers in the ADGDM field. Its emphasis on the synergistic relationship and its structured taxonomy will likely influence future research directions. Its ability to bridge previously siloed research areas could lead to cross-modal innovations and more generalizable AD solutions.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7.5**.  While the paper is well-written and presents a timely and insightful overview of ADGDM, it has some limitations that prevent it from achieving a higher score. The novelty and significance of the integrated detection/generation perspective and the organized taxonomy are clear strengths. However, the limited implementation details, the scope of quantitative performance comparison, and the relative lack of concrete examples in future direction suggestions detract from its practical value. The survey's potential to shape research within the field is notable, but addressing its weaknesses would significantly improve its impact.

### Revisiting Diffusion Models: From Generative Pre-training to One-Step Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09376v1)
- **Authors**: Bowen Zheng, Tianming Yang
- **Abstract**: Diffusion distillation is a widely used technique to reduce the sampling cost of diffusion models, yet it often requires extensive training, and the student performance tends to be degraded. Recent studies show that incorporating a GAN objective may alleviate these issues, yet the underlying mechanism remains unclear. In this work, we first identify a key limitation of distillation: mismatched step sizes and parameter numbers between the teacher and the student model lead them to converge to different local minima, rendering direct imitation suboptimal. We further demonstrate that a standalone GAN objective, without relying a distillation loss, overcomes this limitation and is sufficient to convert diffusion models into efficient one-step generators. Based on this finding, we propose that diffusion training may be viewed as a form of generative pre-training, equipping models with capabilities that can be unlocked through lightweight GAN fine-tuning. Supporting this view, we create a one-step generation model by fine-tuning a pre-trained model with 85% of parameters frozen, achieving strong performance with only 0.2M images and near-SOTA results with 5M images. We further present a frequency-domain analysis that may explain the one-step generative capability gained in diffusion training. Overall, our work provides a new perspective for diffusion training, highlighting its role as a powerful generative pre-training process, which can be the basis for building efficient one-step generation models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Revisiting Diffusion Models: From Generative Pre-training to One-Step Generation" investigates diffusion model distillation, a technique to reduce the computational cost of these models. The authors identify a limitation in existing distillation methods: a mismatch in the optimization landscape between the multi-step teacher model and the single-step student. They argue that forcing the student to directly mimic the teacher is sub-optimal.  The core contribution is D2O (Diffusion to One-Step), a method that uses only a GAN objective to train the one-step generator, avoiding explicit distillation losses.  Furthermore, they hypothesize that diffusion training serves as a powerful generative pre-training process, equipping the model with capabilities that can be unlocked by lightweight GAN fine-tuning. They support this claim by creating D2O-F, a one-step generator where most parameters of a pre-trained diffusion model are frozen during GAN fine-tuning. Extensive experiments demonstrate that D2O and D2O-F achieve competitive performance with significantly reduced data requirements. Finally, the authors analyze the frequency-domain processing within diffusion models to offer insights into how generative capabilities emerge during training.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel contributions.
    *   The identified limitation regarding mismatched optimization landscapes in diffusion distillation is a valuable insight.
    *   The D2O method, relying solely on a GAN objective for distillation, provides a compelling alternative to existing techniques.
    *   The generative pre-training perspective of diffusion models, supported by the D2O-F experiments, is innovative.

*   **Significance:** The paper has significant potential to impact the field.
    *   The reduction in data requirements for one-step generation makes diffusion models more accessible.
    *   The generative pre-training perspective could lead to new transfer learning strategies for diffusion models.
    *   The frequency-domain analysis offers a promising avenue for understanding and optimizing diffusion model training.
* **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies a weakness in existing distillation methods.
    *   **Well-Supported Claims:** The claims are supported by rigorous experiments and ablation studies. The performance gains of D2O and D2O-F are convincingly demonstrated across several datasets.
    *   **Insightful Analysis:** The frequency-domain analysis provides valuable insights into the internal workings of diffusion models.
    *   **Practical Impact:** The D2O and D2O-F methods offer tangible benefits in terms of data efficiency and reduced computational cost.
    * **Well Written:** The paper is well written, carefully explaining each process.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Architecture Exploration:** The experiments primarily focus on a diffusion U-Net architecture.  Exploring other architectures, particularly transformer-based models like DiT, would strengthen the generality of the findings.
    *   **Scope of Experiments:** While the paper tests on multiple datasets (CIFAR-10, AFHQv2, FFHQ, ImageNet), the resolution is limited to 64x64.  Experiments on higher-resolution images would be more relevant for real-world applications.
    *   **Mechanism Understanding:** The frequency analysis, while insightful, provides only a partial explanation for the success of D2O-F. Further investigation into the underlying mechanisms is warranted.
    * **Long training Time:** Training diffusion models usually require a long time which may limit the efficiency.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence future research in diffusion models. The generative pre-training perspective could inspire new transfer learning methods and accelerate the development of efficient generative models. The insights from the frequency domain analysis could lead to more optimized training strategies.

*Rigorous Rationale:* The paper introduces a valid, potentially transformative perspective on diffusion model training and distillation. The experimental results, while limited in scope, strongly support its claims. The combination of technical innovation, empirical validation, and insightful analysis justifies a high score. The limitations in architecture exploration and scope are valid concerns, but the conceptual contributions outweigh these weaknesses.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Beyond Nash Equilibrium: Bounded Rationality of LLMs and humans in Strategic Decision-making
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09390v1)
- **Authors**: Kehan Zheng, Jinfeng Zhou, Hongning Wang
- **Abstract**: Large language models are increasingly used in strategic decision-making settings, yet evidence shows that, like humans, they often deviate from full rationality. In this study, we compare LLMs and humans using experimental paradigms directly adapted from behavioral game-theory research. We focus on two well-studied strategic games, Rock-Paper-Scissors and the Prisoner's Dilemma, which are well known for revealing systematic departures from rational play in human subjects. By placing LLMs in identical experimental conditions, we evaluate whether their behaviors exhibit the bounded rationality characteristic of humans.
