Our findings show that LLMs reproduce familiar human heuristics, such as outcome-based strategy switching and increased cooperation when future interaction is possible, but they apply these rules more rigidly and demonstrate weaker sensitivity to the dynamic changes in the game environment. Model-level analyses reveal distinctive architectural signatures in strategic behavior, and even reasoning models sometimes struggle to find effective strategies in adaptive situations. These results indicate that current LLMs capture only a partial form of human-like bounded rationality and highlight the need for training methods that encourage flexible opponent modeling and stronger context awareness.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the bounded rationality of Large Language Models (LLMs) in strategic decision-making, comparing them directly to human behavior in classic game theory scenarios: Rock-Paper-Scissors (RPS) and the Prisoner's Dilemma (PD). The study replicates human-subject experimental protocols, placing LLMs in the same decision-making environments as human participants. By analyzing LLM actions and reasoning, the authors examine whether LLMs exhibit human-like deviations from rational Nash equilibrium strategies, and identify patterns of bounded rationality. The results indicate that LLMs adopt simplified decision heuristics, such as outcome-based strategy switching, and are influenced by factors like the anticipation of future interactions. However, LLMs tend to apply these heuristics more rigidly and demonstrate weaker sensitivity to changes in the game environment.  Model-level analyses reveal distinctive "strategic signatures" based on model family and the struggles of reasoning models in adapting to evolving opponent behavior.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the direct, head-to-head comparison of LLM and human behavior within a replicated experimental framework borrowed directly from behavioral game theory. While previous work has explored LLMs in strategic contexts, this paper explicitly attempts to mirror established human subject experiments and analyze their respective *bounded rationality*. This approach offers a more controlled and nuanced understanding of the similarities and differences in how LLMs and humans deviate from theoretically optimal strategies.
* **Significance:** The findings contribute to a growing body of research that challenges the notion of LLMs as purely rational agents. The discovery that LLMs exhibit human-like biases, but in an amplified and less adaptable manner, has significant implications for developing more robust and trustworthy AI systems. If LLMs are to be deployed in real-world strategic interactions (negotiation, resource allocation, multi-agent systems), understanding and mitigating these biases is crucial.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Rigorous methodology:** The use of well-established game-theoretic paradigms and the replication of human-subject experimental designs strengthen the validity and comparability of the findings.
    * **Comprehensive analysis:** The study goes beyond aggregate performance metrics, examining individual LLM behavior and the influence of factors like model family and reasoning mechanisms.
    * **Clear research questions:** The paper clearly articulates its research questions and provides well-reasoned arguments for its experimental design.
    * **Identifies limitations:**  The paper acknowledges the limitations of its approach, including prompt sensitivity and the lack of social context in LLM interactions.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited scope:** Focusing on only two specific games, RPS and PD, limits the generalizability of the findings to more complex strategic scenarios.
    * **Prompt sensitivity:** While the authors attempted to control for prompt sensitivity, it remains a potential confound.  Small variations in prompting could significantly influence LLM behavior. The paper doesn't explore different prompting strategies extensively to probe robustness.
    * **Human representation:** The "human" benchmark relies on existing literature. It doesn't directly compare the *same* humans in the same experimental setup alongside the LLMs, introducing some potential for variability.
    * **"Strategic signature" claims require further validation:** While the paper identifies some architectural tendencies, it's not deeply explored or explained why these patterns emerge.

* **Impact:** The paper's findings could influence the development of LLM training methods by highlighting the need for:
    *  Enhanced opponent modeling capabilities.
    *  Greater context awareness.
    *  Mechanisms to mitigate rigid application of heuristics.

* **Areas for Improvement:**
    * Expanding the range of strategic games to assess generalizability.
    * Implementing a parallel human-LLM experimental design with the same participants to minimize variability.
    *  Exploring different prompting strategies systematically to assess robustness.
    *  Delving deeper into the architectural reasons behind the observed "strategic signatures."

**Justification for Score:**

The paper demonstrates methodological rigor and makes a novel contribution to the understanding of LLM behavior in strategic settings. While the scope is somewhat limited and certain aspects could be explored in more depth, the direct comparison to human behavior and the identification of distinctive biases represent valuable advancements. The findings point towards specific areas for improvement in LLM training, potentially leading to more adaptable and reliable AI systems. It bridges game theory and AI in a compelling way.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Comparing human and LLM politeness strategies in free production
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09391v1)
- **Authors**: Haoran Zhao, Robert D. Hawkins
- **Abstract**: Polite speech poses a fundamental alignment challenge for large language models (LLMs). Humans deploy a rich repertoire of linguistic strategies to balance informational and social goals -- from positive approaches that build rapport (compliments, expressions of interest) to negative strategies that minimize imposition (hedging, indirectness). We investigate whether LLMs employ a similarly context-sensitive repertoire by comparing human and LLM responses in both constrained and open-ended production tasks. We find that larger models ($\ge$70B parameters) successfully replicate key preferences from the computational pragmatics literature, and human evaluators surprisingly prefer LLM-generated responses in open-ended contexts. However, further linguistic analyses reveal that models disproportionately rely on negative politeness strategies even in positive contexts, potentially leading to misinterpretations. While modern LLMs demonstrate an impressive handle on politeness strategies, these subtle differences raise important questions about pragmatic alignment in AI systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Comparing human and LLM politeness strategies in free production":

**Summary:**

This paper investigates how Large Language Models (LLMs) generate polite speech, comparing their strategies to those used by humans. The authors examine LLMs' ability to balance informational accuracy and social goals (politeness) in both constrained (multiple choice) and open-ended (free generation) contexts. Using politeness theory (positive vs. negative politeness) as a framework, they find that while LLMs can replicate some human-like politeness preferences, particularly in larger models, they tend to over-rely on negative politeness strategies (hedging, minimizing imposition), even when human speakers would use more positive, rapport-building strategies. This stylistic difference, although not necessarily leading to negative perceptions in isolation, could potentially result in pragmatic misunderstandings and reduced social presence in human-AI communication.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its direct comparison of human and LLM politeness *strategies* in *open-ended* language generation. Previous research has often focused on either recognition of politeness or generation in constrained settings. Analyzing how models *choose* which strategies to deploy, rather than merely assessing if they can generate polite language, is a valuable contribution. Furthermore, finding systematic biases towards negative politeness even when evaluators still prefer the LLM's output is an important, unexpected finding.

*   **Significance:** The study has significant implications for the field of human-computer interaction and the development of socially intelligent AI. As LLMs are increasingly integrated into sensitive social domains, understanding how they manage politeness is crucial for ensuring effective and positive communication. The paper identifies a potential misalignment that could lead to pragmatic breakdowns, raising concerns about AI systems being perceived as cold, insincere, or lacking genuine engagement. This understanding can inform better training strategies for LLMs and more nuanced theoretical models of politeness in AI. The finding also suggests that while overall preference might indicate general competence, deeper strategic analysis reveals more fundamental differences.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Approach:** The paper combines quantitative analysis (correlation, MSE, JSD) with qualitative analysis (annotation of politeness strategies), providing a holistic view.
    *   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The experiments are clearly described, with a replication of Yoon et al.'s (2020) study adding robustness. The two-alternative forced choice methodology helps extract more subtle preferences than simple rating tasks.
    *   **Strong Empirical Basis:** The study utilizes a large dataset of human and LLM-generated responses, with careful controls and rigorous statistical analysis.
    *   **Theoretical Grounding:**  The use of Brown and Levinson's politeness theory as a theoretical framework provides a solid foundation for the analysis.
    *   **Detailed Error Analysis:**  Going beyond simple correlation to analyze specific instances of misalignment between human and LLM responses makes the paper particularly compelling.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Politeness Strategies:**  While the politeness annotation is comprehensive, the analysis primarily focuses on the positive/negative politeness distinction. Future work could benefit from a more granular analysis of specific subtypes within these categories.
    *   **Contextual Factors:** Although scenarios are carefully crafted, real-world interactions are far more complex. The study doesn't fully explore the influence of factors like relationship dynamics, power imbalances, or cultural differences on politeness strategies. The analysis might gain from a deeper exploration of how these contextual factors influence politeness strategy choices, particularly in open-ended responses where these influences are more subtly expressed.
    *   **Evaluation Metric:** While the two-alternative forced choice is robust, it might not fully capture the nuances of pragmatic understanding.  It reveals relative preference but doesn't necessarily reveal why one response is preferred.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence future research by encouraging:

    *   More detailed analyses of pragmatic alignment beyond simple politeness recognition or generation.
    *   Development of LLM training strategies that explicitly balance positive and negative politeness.
    *   Exploration of the cognitive and emotional impacts of AI-generated language on human users.
    *   Development of computational models that can better capture the nuances of human politeness strategies.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by highlighting the strategic differences in politeness between LLMs and humans in open-ended contexts. The empirical evidence is solid, the methodology is well-defined, and the findings have important implications for human-AI communication. While the scope is somewhat limited (e.g., focusing primarily on positive/negative politeness), the work clearly advances our understanding of LLM pragmatic competence and raises important questions about the alignment of AI systems with human social norms. I consider this a strong, incremental advance in the field.
Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Reasoning as a Resource: Optimizing Fast and Slow Thinking in Code Generation Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09396v1)
- **Authors**: Zongjie Li, Shuai Wang
- **Abstract**: This position paper proposes a fundamental shift in designing code generation models: treating reasoning depth as a controllable resource. Rather than being an incidental byproduct of prompting, we argue that the trade-off between rapid, direct answers ("fast thinking") and elaborate, chain-of-thought deliberation ("slow thinking") must be explicitly managed. We contend that optimizing reasoning budgets across the entire model lifecycle - from synthetic data creation and benchmarking to real-world deploymen - can unlock superior trade-offs among accuracy, latency, and cost. This paper outlines how adaptive control over reasoning can enrich supervision signals, motivate new multi-dimensional benchmarks, and inform cost-aware, security-conscious deployment policies. By viewing fast and slow thinking as complementary modes to be scheduled, we envision coding agents that think deep when necessary and act fast when possible.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the position paper "Reasoning as a Resource: Optimizing Fast and Slow Thinking in Code Generation Models":

**Summary:**

The paper advocates for a fundamental shift in how we design code generation models, proposing that reasoning depth should be treated as a controllable resource rather than a mere byproduct of prompting strategies. It argues that managing the trade-off between fast (direct answers) and slow (chain-of-thought deliberation) thinking is crucial for optimizing accuracy, latency, and cost.  The paper suggests controlling reasoning depth throughout the model lifecycle, including synthetic data generation, benchmarking, and real-world deployment, to achieve better trade-offs.  It outlines specific opportunities for enriching supervision signals, creating multi-dimensional benchmarks, and implementing adaptive, cost-aware deployment policies.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of treating reasoning depth as a explicitly managed resource is valuable. While some prior work has explored adaptive selection of CoT (e.g., AdaCoT, Adapt-Think), this paper expands the scope by advocating for reasoning-depth management across the entire model lifecycle, from training data creation to deployment, and further argues the relevance of security constraints. It introduces the important notion of a "Reasoning Depth Controller" for a more holistic view of the LLM model.
* **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical practical limitation of current code generation models: the often-unbalanced trade-off between accuracy, latency, and cost.  It suggests improvements to data synthesis pipelines and evaluation methodologies, arguing that current benchmarks focus too heavily on end-to-end accuracy, neglecting latency and token usage. The argument for policy-driven reasoning depth control during deployment is also significant, particularly given the growing use of LLMs in latency-sensitive and security-critical applications. Furthermore, it calls out the IP leakage risks associated with unconstrained CoT.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Comprehensive Scope:** The paper considers the entire model lifecycle, making its recommendations applicable at various stages of code LLM development and usage.
    * **Practical Relevance:** The paper highlights practical concerns about cost, latency, and security, making its recommendations highly relevant to real-world deployments.
    * **Concrete Suggestions:** The paper provides specific examples of how to implement reasoning depth control, such as scheduling CoT during data generation and developing multi-dimensional benchmarks.
    * **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper presents a clear argument about the limitations of existing approaches and the need for a new paradigm.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Technical Depth:** As a position paper, it lacks detailed technical specifications on how to implement the proposed framework. The "Reasoning Depth Controller" is presented schematically but lacks concrete implementation details.
    * **Over-Reliance on Existing Methods:** The paper acknowledges prior work like AdaCoT but does not sufficiently detail how the overall framework integrates and expands on these existing methods.
    * **Lack of Empirical Evidence:** The paper would be stronger if it included preliminary empirical evidence, even simulated, to demonstrate the potential benefits of its proposed framework. The arguments are logically sound, but more convincing evidence is required for a transformative paradigm shift.
    * **Limited Discussion of Security Aspects:** Although mentioned as a critical part of the reasoning depth control in LLMs, it only provides a high-level perspective on reasoning depth and IP risks. Details on different attack vectors or the reasoning depth trade-off for security aspects are not well-discussed in the paper.
* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research and development in the area of code generation models by:
    * **Motivating new benchmarks:**  It could lead to the development of benchmarks that explicitly measure accuracy, latency, and token cost, encouraging researchers to optimize for all three.
    * **Inspiring new algorithms:**  It could inspire the development of new algorithms for reasoning depth control that adapt to different tasks, security requirements, and resource constraints.
    * **Informing deployment strategies:**  It could inform the development of deployment strategies that dynamically allocate thinking budgets based on task difficulty and security risks.

**Overall:** This is a well-written and thought-provoking position paper that identifies an important problem and proposes a potentially valuable solution. While lacking detailed technical specifications and empirical evidence, it is logically sound and highly relevant to the current state of code LLMs. It presents a compelling argument that could influence the future direction of research and development in this area.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a valuable perspective on a relevant problem. Its novelty lies in its holistic approach, spanning the entire model lifecycle, and the emphasis on security implications.  The paper is not a breakthrough with novel algorithmic contributions or empirical results but a good articulation of the next direction in the field of code LLMs. Therefore, a score of 7 reflects its strong contribution as a position paper and its potential for shaping future research. The weaknesses mainly are around the lack of implementation details and concrete results to support claims, which would be needed for a truly transformative contribution deserving a higher score. A significant improvement would be to ground the abstract concept with some kind of empirical study to support claims.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### SLED: A Speculative LLM Decoding Framework for Efficient Edge Serving
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09397v1)
- **Authors**: Xiangchen Li, Dimitrios Spatharakis, Saeid Ghafouri, Jiakun Fan, Dimitrios Nikolopoulos
- **Abstract**: Regardless the advancements in device capabilities, efficient inferencing advanced large language models (LLMs) at the edge remains challenging due to limited device memory and power constraints. Existing strategies, such as aggressive quantization, pruning, or remote inference, trade accuracy for efficiency or lead to substantial cost burdens. This position paper introduces a new approach that leverages speculative decoding, previously viewed primarily as a decoding acceleration technique for autoregressive generation of LLMs, as a promising approach specifically adapted for edge computing by orchestrating computation across heterogeneous devices. We propose SLED, a method that allows lightweight edge devices to draft multiple candidate tokens locally using diverse draft models, while a single, shared edge server efficiently batches and verifies the tokens utilizing a more precise target model. This approach supports device heterogeneity and reduces server-side memory footprint by avoiding the need to deploy multiple target models. Our initial experiments with Jetson Orin Nano, Raspberry Pi 5, and an RTX 6000 edge server indicate substantial benefits: significantly reduced latency, improved energy efficiency, and increased concurrent inference sessions, all without sacrificing model accuracy.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SLED (Speculative LLM Decoding), a novel framework for efficient Large Language Model (LLM) inference at the edge. SLED leverages speculative decoding by distributing the task between lightweight edge devices and a more powerful edge server. Edge devices draft candidate tokens using smaller models, while the server verifies these tokens in batches using a more accurate target model. This approach aims to address the limitations of edge computing, such as limited memory and power, while maintaining model accuracy and supporting heterogeneous devices. The authors present empirical evaluations demonstrating reduced latency, improved energy efficiency, and increased concurrent inference sessions with SLED.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonable, but not groundbreaking, level of novelty. Speculative decoding, in general, is already an established technique for LLM acceleration. The main novelty lies in adapting this technique specifically for edge computing and partitioning the task across heterogeneous devices. While the idea of using smaller, less accurate "draft" models on edge devices and a more accurate "target" model on a server is intuitive, the specific architecture and dynamic drafting mechanism contribute a fresh perspective. The disaggregated architecture that partitions the speculative decoding process and focuses the powerful server on verification instead of full generation is key.

*   **Significance:** The paper has potential significance for the field of edge computing and LLM deployment. Efficient LLM inference at the edge is a critical challenge, and SLED offers a viable solution by addressing resource constraints and supporting device heterogeneity. The claimed improvements in latency, energy efficiency, and scalability could make LLMs more accessible for edge-based applications. However, the true significance hinges on how well SLED performs in real-world deployments and whether it can be easily adopted and integrated into existing systems.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Addresses a critical problem: Efficient LLM inference at the edge.
    *   Novel adaptation of speculative decoding for edge computing.
    *   Supports device heterogeneity.
    *   Empirical evaluations demonstrate promising results.
    *   Clearly written and well-organized.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Relies on a well-known technique (speculative decoding); the novelty is in adaptation and partitioning.
    *   Evaluation lacks information about the specifics of the chosen prompts.
    *   Some implementation details (e.g. the exact loss for fine-tuning the local LLMs, the exact hardware/model parameters) are missing.
    *   Evaluation is limited to simulations and measurements; real-world deployments are needed.
    *   Limited details on the model's quantization used for LLM compression

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research in edge-based LLM inference and distributed computing. The SLED framework could be a starting point for developing more sophisticated edge-aware LLM solutions. The authors' insights into resource utilization and device heterogeneity could also inform the design of future edge computing systems.

*   **Missing elements that would significantly strengthen the paper:**

    *   **Detailed cost analysis**: The paper claims cost-effectiveness, but lacks detailed cost analysis. Providing a comparison of the Total Cost of Ownership (TCO) of SLED vs. standard cloud-based inference or other edge deployment strategies would significantly bolster the claims.
    *   **Security Considerations**: Given the distributed nature of SLED, security is a paramount concern. Discussing the potential security vulnerabilities of the framework (e.g., data breaches, adversarial attacks on draft models) and providing mitigation strategies would significantly improve the paper's practical value.
    *   **Qualitative Analysis of Generated Text:** While quantitative metrics are important, a qualitative analysis of the text generated by SLED vs. baseline models would provide more insight into whether the speculative drafting/verification process introduces subtle biases or degradation in generation quality.
    *   **Comparative Study with Other Edge Inference Frameworks**: The paper mainly compares SLED against centralized LLM inference. Comparing SLED with existing edge inference frameworks that utilize techniques like model partitioning or edge-cloud collaboration would provide a better understanding of its relative strengths and weaknesses.
    *   **Adaptive Batching/Queueing Details:** The paper mentions static batching on the edge server. Exploring adaptive batching strategies that dynamically adjust the batch size based on network conditions and device capabilities would further optimize SLED's performance in dynamic edge environments.
    *   **Detailed Hardware and Software Configuration:** Adding more details about the actual hardware used on each edge device, the models they use and the software used would make the paper easier to reproduce.

**Score:** 6/10

**Justification:** The paper presents a novel adaptation of speculative decoding for edge computing, which has the potential to significantly improve the efficiency of LLM inference at the edge. However, the reliance on existing techniques, the limited evaluation scope, and the lack of detail in some areas temper the overall score. The novelty is incremental rather than revolutionary. Additional real-world evaluation is crucial to confirm the claimed benefits and assess its practical viability.

- **Classification**: cs.DC
- **Score**: 6/10

### Token Constraint Decoding Improves Robustness on Question Answering for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09408v1)
- **Authors**: Jui-Ming Yao, Hao-Yuan Chen, Zi-Xian Tang, Bing-Jia Tan, Sheng-Wei Peng, Bing-Cheng Xie, Shun-Feng Su
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated impressive performance on multiple-choice question answering (MCQA) benchmarks, yet they remain highly vulnerable to minor input perturbations. In this paper, we introduce and evaluate Token Constraint Decoding (TCD). This simple yet effective inference-time algorithm enforces alignment between token-level predictions to enhance robustness in noisy settings. Through extensive experiments on CommonsenseQA, MMLU, and MMLU-Pro, we show that TCD, especially when paired with prompt engineering (PE) fixes, significantly restores performance degraded by input noise, yielding up to +39\% absolute gains for weaker models like Gemma3 1B. Penalty sweep analyses further reveal that TCD implicitly regularizes overconfident outputs, with different models requiring distinct penalty schedules to maximize resilience. Our findings establish TCD as a practical, model-agnostic approach for improving reasoning stability under real-world imperfections and pave the way for more reliable deployment of LLMs in safety-critical or user-facing applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Token Constraint Decoding" (TCD), a novel inference-time algorithm aimed at improving the robustness of Large Language Models (LLMs) in question-answering tasks when faced with minor input perturbations (like extra spaces). TCD works by constraining the model's output to a predefined set of allowed tokens, penalizing disallowed tokens, and using temperature scaling.  The authors evaluated TCD on CommonsenseQA, MMLU, and MMLU-Pro benchmarks with Llama3 and Gemma models, showing that TCD, especially when combined with prompt engineering fixes, can significantly recover performance degraded by noisy inputs. The paper includes an analysis of how varying the penalty score in TCD affects the model's performance, revealing that different models require distinct penalty schedules to maximize resilience.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of constraining the output tokens during decoding isn't entirely new.  Methods like constrained beam search have been explored before. However, the paper’s specific implementation of TCD, particularly its lightweight nature and modular way of applying penalties, appears relatively novel. The combination of TCD with prompt engineering and the detailed analysis of the impact of penalty scores contributes incremental novelty.

*   **Significance:** LLM robustness is a very important and timely topic.  The paper addresses a practical vulnerability (sensitivity to minor input variations) that can significantly impact real-world LLM deployments. The fact that TCD is an inference-time method (i.e., doesn't require retraining) makes it practically valuable and easily applicable to existing LLMs. The improvements demonstrated on standard benchmarks, particularly for weaker models, further highlight its significance. The detailed analysis of penalty score optimization across models and datasets is also a significant contribution.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Practicality:** TCD is simple to implement and doesn't require retraining the underlying LLM.
    *   **Effectiveness:** The experiments demonstrate significant improvements in robustness against input noise, especially when combined with prompt engineering.
    *   **Model-Agnostic:** The authors tested TCD on multiple LLMs (Llama and Gemma), suggesting it's a generalizable approach.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The penalty sweep experiments provide valuable insights into how TCD's hyperparameters affect performance and how different models respond to regularization.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** The focus is primarily on multiple-choice question answering.  The paper acknowledges that TCD's applicability to more open-ended or generative tasks is limited.
    *   **Token Dependence:** The method's effectiveness relies on having a well-defined and enumerable set of allowed tokens. It might struggle in scenarios where the correct answer is ambiguous or requires complex reasoning.
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While valuable, TCD is not a paradigm shift. It is an incremental improvement on existing decoding strategies.
    *   **Experimental Focus:** Most of the experiments are confined to established benchmarks. More diverse and realistic scenarios in complex scenarios would add value.

*   **Potential Impact:**
    The simplicity and effectiveness of TCD make it likely to be adopted by practitioners looking to improve LLM robustness in question-answering tasks. The insights from the penalty sweep analysis could inform the development of more adaptive and robust decoding strategies. The paper could also inspire further research into mitigating LLM vulnerabilities to input noise.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

While the paper doesn't introduce a completely groundbreaking concept, it makes several important contributions. TCD is a practical and effective technique for improving LLM robustness in a specific but relevant setting. The thorough experimental evaluation and analysis of penalty score optimization add significant value. Given the importance of robustness in real-world LLM deployment, the paper's findings are likely to be of practical interest to the community. The limitations of the technique are acknowledged, which is positive.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### PGDA-KGQA: A Prompt-Guided Generative Framework with Multiple Data Augmentation Strategies for Knowledge Graph Question Answering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09414v1)
- **Authors**: Xiujun Zhou, Pingjian Zhang, Deyou Tang
- **Abstract**: Knowledge Graph Question Answering (KGQA) is a crucial task in natural language processing that requires reasoning over knowledge graphs (KGs) to answer natural language questions. Recent methods utilizing large language models (LLMs) have shown remarkable semantic parsing capabilities but are limited by the scarcity of diverse annotated data and multi-hop reasoning samples. Traditional data augmentation approaches are focus mainly on single-hop questions and prone to semantic distortion, while LLM-based methods primarily address semantic distortion but usually neglect multi-hop reasoning, thus limiting data diversity. The scarcity of multi-hop samples further weakens models' generalization. To address these issues, we propose PGDA-KGQA, a prompt-guided generative framework with multiple data augmentation strategies for KGQA. At its core, PGDA-KGQA employs a unified prompt-design paradigm: by crafting meticulously engineered prompts that integrate the provided textual content, it leverages LLMs to generate large-scale (question, logical form) pairs for model training. Specifically, PGDA-KGQA enriches its training set by: (1) generating single-hop pseudo questions to improve the alignment of question semantics with KG relations; (2) applying semantic-preserving question rewriting to improve robustness against linguistic variations; (3) employing answer-guided reverse path exploration to create realistic multi-hop questions. By adopting an augment-generate-retrieve semantic parsing pipeline, PGDA-KGQA utilizes the augmented data to enhance the accuracy of logical form generation and thus improve answer retrieval performance. Experiments demonstrate that outperforms state-of-the-art methods on standard KGQA datasets, achieving improvements on WebQSP by 2.8%, 1.2%, and 3.1% and on ComplexWebQuestions by 1.8%, 1.1%, and 2.4% in F1, Hits@1, and Accuracy, respectively.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary**

The paper "PGDA-KGQA: A Prompt-Guided Generative Framework with Multiple Data Augmentation Strategies for Knowledge Graph Question Answering" proposes a novel framework, PGDA-KGQA, to improve knowledge graph question answering (KGQA). The key idea is to augment the training data using large language models (LLMs) through carefully crafted prompts. PGDA-KGQA employs three data augmentation strategies: (1) Single-hop Pseudo Question Generation (SPQG) to align question semantics with KG relations, (2) Semantic Preserving Question Rewriting (SPQR) to improve robustness against linguistic variations, and (3) Answer-guided Reverse Path Exploration (ARPE) to create realistic multi-hop questions. These augmented datasets are used to fine-tune LLMs, which then generate logical forms that are refined through unsupervised retrieval and used to execute SPARQL queries on the KG to obtain answers. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of their approach on WebQSP and ComplexWebQuestions datasets, achieving state-of-the-art results.

**Critical Evaluation**

**Novelty:**

*   **Strength:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the combination of three LLM-based data augmentation techniques, each targeting specific aspects of the KGQA problem (single-hop understanding, paraphrasing, and multi-hop reasoning). The prompt engineering aspect also contributes some novelty. The idea of answer-guided reverse path exploration is a significant improvement over random walks.
*   **Weakness:** While the individual components (LLMs for data augmentation, prompt engineering, fine-tuning) are not entirely novel, the **integration** of these specifically for KGQA, particularly using three distinct and well-defined strategies, is a unique contribution. The techniques, individually, are incremental improvements on existing work.

**Significance:**

*   **Strength:**  The paper addresses a key challenge in KGQA: the scarcity of annotated data, especially for complex, multi-hop questions. By demonstrating a practical and effective approach to data augmentation, the paper enables the training of more accurate and robust KGQA systems.  The performance gains on standard benchmarks are significant and empirically validate the effectiveness of the proposed framework.
*   **Weakness:** The reliance on LLMs can be seen as a weakness. While LLMs have demonstrated impressive capabilities, they also introduce potential biases and inconsistencies. The paper could benefit from a more thorough discussion of these limitations and how they are addressed within the PGDA-KGQA framework. Additionally, the paper could be strengthened by investigating the scalability of the framework to larger and more complex knowledge graphs and a greater variety of questions. Generalization could also be improved, perhaps through meta-learning or similar techniques. Finally, while the improvements are strong, they are not *massive*.

**Clarity and Reproducibility:**

The paper is generally well-written and clearly describes the proposed framework and experimental setup. The inclusion of algorithms and detailed descriptions of the data augmentation strategies enhances reproducibility.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

PGDA-KGQA introduces a strong and well-executed prompting framework to improve LLM data augmentation for KGQA. Its three key augmentation strategies each solve a unique issue (e.g., single-hop alignment, semantic variations, multi-hop reasoning). The results on WebQSP and CWQ are significant and show the benefits of PGDA-KGQA over existing SOTA methods. The critical area for future work is mitigating noisy LLM-augmented data and scaling up the quality and types of KGQA datasets.

**Score: 7**

*   The core innovation of integrating three LLM data augmentation strategies is novel and produces strong experimental results.
*   Each strategy is well defined and specifically targets a key challenge in KGQA.
*   The reliance on LLMs introduces biases. More advanced methods may allow for more generalized results.
*   The quality of LLM generated data can be difficult to guarantee.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Noise Conditional Variational Score Distillation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09416v1)
- **Authors**: Xinyu Peng, Ziyang Zheng, Yaoming Wang, Han Li, Nuowen Kan, Wenrui Dai, Chenglin Li, Junni Zou, Hongkai Xiong
- **Abstract**: We propose Noise Conditional Variational Score Distillation (NCVSD), a novel method for distilling pretrained diffusion models into generative denoisers. We achieve this by revealing that the unconditional score function implicitly characterizes the score function of denoising posterior distributions. By integrating this insight into the Variational Score Distillation (VSD) framework, we enable scalable learning of generative denoisers capable of approximating samples from the denoising posterior distribution across a wide range of noise levels. The proposed generative denoisers exhibit desirable properties that allow fast generation while preserve the benefit of iterative refinement: (1) fast one-step generation through sampling from pure Gaussian noise at high noise levels; (2) improved sample quality by scaling the test-time compute with multi-step sampling; and (3) zero-shot probabilistic inference for flexible and controllable sampling. We evaluate NCVSD through extensive experiments, including class-conditional image generation and inverse problem solving. By scaling the test-time compute, our method outperforms teacher diffusion models and is on par with consistency models of larger sizes. Additionally, with significantly fewer NFEs than diffusion-based methods, we achieve record-breaking LPIPS on inverse problems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes Noise Conditional Variational Score Distillation (NCVSD), a new method for distilling pre-trained diffusion models into generative denoisers. It reveals that the unconditional score function implicitly characterizes the score function of denoising posterior distributions. By integrating this insight into the Variational Score Distillation (VSD) framework, NCVSD enables scalable learning of generative denoisers approximating samples from the denoising posterior distribution across a wide range of noise levels. The resulting denoisers allow for fast one-step generation from Gaussian noise, improved sample quality through multi-step sampling, and zero-shot probabilistic inference. Experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of NCVSD in class-conditional image generation and inverse problem solving, outperforming teacher diffusion models and matching the performance of larger consistency models with fewer function evaluations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the theoretical connection established between the unconditional score function and the score function of the denoising posterior. Leveraging this connection to develop Noise Conditional Variational Score Distillation (NCVSD) for training generative denoisers capable of approximating samples from the denoising posterior distribution across a wide range of noise levels. This allows the model to be scaled efficiently using test-time steps (multi-step sampling) for improved quality. The integration of an auxiliary adversarial loss to refine learning from real data and engineering the denoiser's parameterization using preconditioning also adds to the novelty.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a key limitation of diffusion models: slow inference speed. By distilling diffusion models into generative denoisers, NCVSD significantly reduces the number of function evaluations required for generation, making it more practical for real-time applications. Furthermore, the paper makes the model flexible with three main properties: one-step generation, improved sample quality with test-time scaling, and zero-shot probabilistic inference. The demonstration of superior or comparable performance to consistency models in image generation, coupled with record-breaking LPIPS scores in inverse problem solving using dramatically fewer NFEs (e.g., 50 vs. 1000), indicates a significant practical advancement. The flexibility to work with the split Gibbs sampler is also promising.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper provides a strong theoretical justification for its approach.
    *   The experimental results are compelling, showcasing improvements in both image generation and inverse problem solving.
    *   The discussion of limitations is valuable for future research.
    *   The engineering contributions, such as the auxiliary adversarial loss and careful parameterization, are crucial to the method's success.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Reliance on pre-trained diffusion models limits the possibility of training from scratch.
    *   The need for adversarial training adds complexity and requires careful tuning for stable convergence.
    *   The impact statement acknowledges potential for misuse of synthetic data generation, raising ethical considerations.

    **Further Notes:** The improvement in inference efficiency is substantial, which is crucial for many real-world applications. The versatility to perform a range of tasks with a single model is also a strong selling point.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**
The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of generative modeling. The theoretical insight connecting unconditional and conditional score functions is well-founded, and the NCVSD framework demonstrates compelling practical benefits. The substantial improvement in sampling efficiency, combined with the ability to perform probabilistic inference, makes this a valuable advancement. However, the reliance on pre-trained diffusion models and the need for adversarial training, while not uncommon, slightly reduce the overall score. The paper’s strong theoretical and empirical results justify a score of 8, indicating a significant contribution with the potential for future development and influence in the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### A Call for Collaborative Intelligence: Why Human-Agent Systems Should Precede AI Autonomy
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09420v1)
- **Authors**: Henry Peng Zou, Wei-Chieh Huang, Yaozu Wu, Chunyu Miao, Dongyuan Li, Aiwei Liu, Yue Zhou, Yankai Chen, Weizhi Zhang, Yangning Li, Liancheng Fang, Renhe Jiang, Philip S. Yu
- **Abstract**: Recent improvements in large language models (LLMs) have led many researchers to focus on building fully autonomous AI agents. This position paper questions whether this approach is the right path forward, as these autonomous systems still have problems with reliability, transparency, and understanding the actual requirements of human. We suggest a different approach: LLM-based Human-Agent Systems (LLM-HAS), where AI works with humans rather than replacing them. By keeping human involved to provide guidance, answer questions, and maintain control, these systems can be more trustworthy and adaptable. Looking at examples from healthcare, finance, and software development, we show how human-AI teamwork can handle complex tasks better than AI working alone. We also discuss the challenges of building these collaborative systems and offer practical solutions. This paper argues that progress in AI should not be measured by how independent systems become, but by how well they can work with humans. The most promising future for AI is not in systems that take over human roles, but in those that enhance human capabilities through meaningful partnership.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper argues against the current trend of solely pursuing fully autonomous AI agents, particularly those based on Large Language Models (LLMs).  It advocates for a shift towards LLM-based Human-Agent Systems (LLM-HAS), where AI agents collaborate with humans rather than replacing them. The authors contend that fully autonomous agents suffer from issues related to reliability, handling complex tasks, and accountability. By emphasizing human involvement, LLM-HAS can offer enhanced trust, better management of ambiguity, and clearer lines of responsibility. The paper highlights applications of LLM-HAS across various domains (healthcare, finance, software engineering), outlines challenges in developing such systems (work allocation, adaptivity), and proposes implementation guidelines.  Finally, it addresses common counterarguments for emphasizing full autonomy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The idea of human-in-the-loop systems is not entirely novel.  However, the paper's specific framing of this concept in the context of *current* LLM-based autonomous agents is a valuable contribution. The paper clearly articulates *why* a focus on collaboration is crucial *at this specific point* in AI development.  The emphasis on shortcomings of autonomous agents *given their current state* provides a timely counterpoint to the hype. The guidelines for human-agent system implementation are also potentially novel and beneficial.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its pragmatic and cautionary perspective.  By pointing out limitations of full autonomy (hallucinations, lack of common sense, accountability) and advocating for human-AI collaboration, the paper encourages a more responsible and ethical approach to AI development. Given the recent advancements and investments in autonomous AI agents, the paper's message about the risk of premature deployment and the need for enhanced reliability holds value. The paper is insightful in its examination of evaluation methodologies for human-agent systems, highlighting critical gaps in existing frameworks and advocating for more comprehensive metrics.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-argued position:** The paper provides a clear and coherent argument against prioritizing full autonomy over collaboration.
    *   **Practical focus:** The discussion of applications, challenges, and implementation guidelines provides a practical roadmap for researchers and developers.
    *   **Counterargument addressing:** The paper engages with counterarguments, anticipating and addressing potential criticisms.
    *   **Clear structure:** The paper is well-structured, making it easy to follow the main points.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Empirical Evidence:** While the paper provides examples of LLM-HAS in various domains, more in-depth analysis of the effectiveness and limitations of these systems would strengthen the argument. Quantitative evidence comparing the performance of autonomous agents and collaborative systems in specific tasks would be beneficial.
    *   **Oversimplification**: The framework for "optimal human involvement" might be overly simplistic and lacks nuance. The factors influencing success and the costs are complex and not easily quantifiable.
    *   **Breadth vs. Depth:** The paper covers a wide range of topics (applications, challenges, guidelines). Deep dives on how to address each of the identified technical challenges will add more value.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence the AI research community by encouraging a more critical evaluation of the pursuit of fully autonomous systems. It can spark new research directions focused on human-AI collaboration, more effective evaluation metrics, and ethical considerations. Its impact is amplified by the timeliness of the topic, aligning with growing concerns about AI safety and accountability.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:** The paper's novelty is not revolutionary, as human-in-the-loop AI is not new. However, it offers a timely and significant perspective by reframing the discussion within the context of current LLM-based autonomous agents and addressing concerns about premature deployment. The provided roadmap and implementation guidelines are practical and valuable. The paper effectively balances theoretical arguments with real-world examples. Weaknesses include limited empirical evidence, potential oversimplification of the problem, and the breadth versus depth tradeoff. Despite those limitations, the paper will serve as a strong advocate for collaborative systems and has potential to impact the future of AI research.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Time-Unified Diffusion Policy with Action Discrimination for Robotic Manipulation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09422v1)
- **Authors**: Ye Niu, Sanping Zhou, Yizhe Li, Ye Den, Le Wang
- **Abstract**: In many complex scenarios, robotic manipulation relies on generative models to estimate the distribution of multiple successful actions. As the diffusion model has better training robustness than other generative models, it performs well in imitation learning through successful robot demonstrations. However, the diffusion-based policy methods typically require significant time to iteratively denoise robot actions, which hinders real-time responses in robotic manipulation. Moreover, existing diffusion policies model a time-varying action denoising process, whose temporal complexity increases the difficulty of model training and leads to suboptimal action accuracy. To generate robot actions efficiently and accurately, we present the Time-Unified Diffusion Policy (TUDP), which utilizes action recognition capabilities to build a time-unified denoising process. On the one hand, we build a time-unified velocity field in action space with additional action discrimination information. By unifying all timesteps of action denoising, our velocity field reduces the difficulty of policy learning and speeds up action generation. On the other hand, we propose an action-wise training method, which introduces an action discrimination branch to supply additional action discrimination information. Through action-wise training, the TUDP implicitly learns the ability to discern successful actions to better denoising accuracy. Our method achieves state-of-the-art performance on RLBench with the highest success rate of 82.6% on a multi-view setup and 83.8% on a single-view setup. In particular, when using fewer denoising iterations, TUDP achieves a more significant improvement in success rate. Additionally, TUDP can produce accurate actions for a wide range of real-world tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Time-Unified Diffusion Policy with Action Discrimination for Robotic Manipulation":

**Summary:**

This paper introduces a new diffusion policy method called Time-Unified Diffusion Policy (TUDP) for robotic manipulation. TUDP addresses the limitations of existing diffusion-based policies by aiming to improve both efficiency and accuracy in action generation. The core ideas involve: 1) a time-unified velocity field, which simplifies the denoising process by eliminating the need for time-varying modeling, and 2) an action discrimination branch that provides additional information to guide the denoising process toward specific successful actions. The method is trained using an action-wise training approach and evaluated on the RLBench benchmark and in real-robot experiments. The results show improved performance, especially with fewer denoising iterations, indicating faster action generation and applicability to real-world tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its specific combination of techniques to address the identified limitations of existing diffusion policies.  While individual components (diffusion policies, action recognition) are not novel *per se*, the integration of a *time-unified velocity field* and *action discrimination* through a dedicated network and action-wise training appears to be a unique contribution. The key idea is to avoid explicitly modeling the time dependency for the diffusion process, which makes it easier to learn, while adding an extra "understanding" of which action is correct to better drive the denoising process. The early termination criteria is a smaller contribution and not particularly novel.

*   **Significance:** If the claims hold up, TUDP has the potential to significantly impact robotic manipulation by enabling faster and more accurate action generation using diffusion models. Diffusion models have already shown promise in this area, but their computational cost has been a barrier to real-time application.  Reducing the number of denoising iterations without sacrificing accuracy is a worthwhile goal. The real-robot experiments, even if limited, strengthen the practical significance of the work.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly identifies the challenges of time-varying complexity and action ambiguity in existing diffusion policies for robotic manipulation.
    *   **Well-Motivated Approach:**  The proposed TUDP method is well-motivated by the analysis of these challenges. The time-unified velocity field directly addresses the temporal complexity, and action discrimination helps to resolve ambiguity.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:**  The paper demonstrates state-of-the-art performance on the RLBench benchmark, with improvements in average success rate and average ranking. The ablation studies provide evidence for the effectiveness of the individual components of TUDP. The real-robot experiment also validates the claims.
    *   **Good Presentation:** The paper is well-written and organized. The figures and tables are informative and contribute to understanding the method.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Real-Robot Evaluation:**  While the inclusion of real-robot experiments is a strength, the scale and complexity of these experiments are relatively limited. The specific experimental setup, how often and how were waypoints generated for the BiRRT planner, and the task setup could be better described. More complex manipulation tasks in real-world scenarios would provide stronger evidence for the robustness and generalizability of TUDP.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The reliance on several hyperparameters (neighborhood radius, Gaussian variance, opening weight) raises concerns about the sensitivity of the method and the difficulty of tuning it for new tasks. The ablation studies address this somewhat, but further investigation into the robustness of the method would be beneficial.
    *   **Neighborhood Radius Assumptions**: The core idea behind TUDP is the definition of a neighborhood radius within which actions are correct. This is a strong assumption, and likely limits the applicability of the method in scenarios where the action space is densely populated with correct actions.
    *   **Incremental Improvement**: While TUDP achieves SOTA, the improvement over previous methods like 3D Diffuser Actor and RVT2, especially in many individual tasks in Table 1, may be seen as incremental, given the additional complexity introduced.
    *   **Lack of Thorough Comparison:** Although a range of baseline methods were included, the comparison could have been enhanced by including a more detailed analysis of why TUDP outperformed these methods. Specifically, a more thorough comparison of the computation time of the model vs. other models should be included.
    *   **Limited Novelty in Individual Components:** Many of the individual components in the architecture (e.g., CLIP encoders, multimodal alignment modules) are not novel. The value comes from the specific combinations.

*   **Potential Influence:**  TUDP has the potential to influence future research on diffusion policies for robotic manipulation by demonstrating the benefits of simplifying the temporal modeling and incorporating action discrimination. If the method proves to be robust and generalizable, it could lead to wider adoption of diffusion models in real-world robotics applications.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:** The paper presents a well-motivated and empirically supported method for improving the efficiency and accuracy of diffusion policies for robotic manipulation. The combination of a time-unified velocity field and action discrimination is a novel contribution with potential for significant impact. The paper achieves state-of-the-art performance on a challenging benchmark. However, the real-robot evaluation is limited, the method relies on several hyperparameters that may be difficult to tune, and the improvement over prior work, while impressive, is incremental in some aspects. The strong assumptions on the correct action region within the action space also limits its general applicability. Given these strengths and weaknesses, a score of 7 reflects the solid contribution of the paper while acknowledging the areas for future research.
- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### Hidden in Plain Sight: Evaluation of the Deception Detection Capabilities of LLMs in Multimodal Settings
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09424v1)
- **Authors**: Md Messal Monem Miah, Adrita Anika, Xi Shi, Ruihong Huang
- **Abstract**: Detecting deception in an increasingly digital world is both a critical and challenging task. In this study, we present a comprehensive evaluation of the automated deception detection capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) and Large Multimodal Models (LMMs) across diverse domains. We assess the performance of both open-source and commercial LLMs on three distinct datasets: real life trial interviews (RLTD), instructed deception in interpersonal scenarios (MU3D), and deceptive reviews (OpSpam). We systematically analyze the effectiveness of different experimental setups for deception detection, including zero-shot and few-shot approaches with random or similarity-based in-context example selection. Our results show that fine-tuned LLMs achieve state-of-the-art performance on textual deception detection tasks, while LMMs struggle to fully leverage cross-modal cues. Additionally, we analyze the impact of auxiliary features, such as non-verbal gestures and video summaries, and examine the effectiveness of different prompting strategies, including direct label generation and chain-of-thought reasoning. Our findings provide key insights into how LLMs process and interpret deceptive cues across modalities, highlighting their potential and limitations in real-world deception detection applications.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper and here's a summary and a critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper "Hidden in Plain Sight: Evaluation of the Deception Detection Capabilities of LLMs in Multimodal Settings" explores the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) and Large Multimodal Models (LMMs) to detect deception in various contexts. It evaluates both open-source and proprietary models on three datasets: real-life trial interviews (RLTD), instructed deception in interpersonal scenarios (MU3D), and deceptive reviews (OpSpam). The study investigates various experimental setups, including zero-shot and few-shot learning, with different in-context example selection strategies. They further analyze the impact of auxiliary features (non-verbal cues, video summaries) and different prompting strategies (direct label generation vs. reasoning generation). The results indicate that fine-tuned LLMs can achieve state-of-the-art performance on textual deception detection, but LMMs struggle to effectively leverage multimodal cues, particularly in real-world settings. The paper finds that reasoning-based predictions don't consistently improve performance over direct classification.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper offers a broad evaluation of LLMs and LMMs across multiple deception detection datasets, experimental settings (zero-shot, few-shot, fine-tuning), and modalities. This breadth is valuable for the field.
    *   **Real-World Data:** The use of the RLTD dataset with real-life trial interviews is a strength, providing insights into the challenges of deception detection in unscripted settings.
    *   **Analysis of Auxiliary Features:** The exploration of non-verbal cues and video summaries is relevant, as it tackles the multimodal aspect of deception detection.
    *   **Prompt Engineering Exploration:** The analysis of different prompting strategies, and the somewhat surprising finding that reasoning doesn't always improve performance, offers practical guidance for designing LLM-driven deception detection systems.
    *   **Analysis of LLM Reasoning:** Investigating how LLMs leverage cues like specificity, vagueness, hesitation, and justification provides insights into their decision-making processes. This analysis is potentially valuable for understanding biases and limitations.
    *   **Open-Source Replication:** the use of Hugging Face and OpenAI tools facilitates future replication by the community.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Multimodal Performance:** A key finding is the relative lack of success of LMMs in leveraging multimodal cues. While this is a finding in itself, it limits the practical implications of the work, and also raises questions about if the LMM models were able to operate at higher FPS and properly incorporate the non-text features.
    *   **Data-Origin Confounding:** The acknowledgment of the data-origin confound in the OpSpam dataset highlights a limitation of this dataset, which is extensively used within the community.
    *   **Unremarkable novelty in fine-tuning method:** The paper only mentions that the LLaMA-Factory framework (Zheng et al., 2024) was used, but not how. The framework itself uses SOTA technology that the community already has access to.
    *   **Limited Scope:** While the datasets are diverse, they are still confined to specific domains (legal, interpersonal, online reviews). The generalizability of the findings to other deception scenarios is an open question. The reliance on English-language datasets limits the cultural and linguistic scope of the study.
    *   **Costly experiments:** The experiment's financial cost and computational resourcing may act as a detriment for future experimentation.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The paper *doesn't* break new ground by proposing novel deception detection techniques or architectures.
    *   The novelty lies primarily in its *systematic and comprehensive evaluation* of existing LLMs and LMMs in this context. This is important because it provides a benchmark for future research and a realistic assessment of the current capabilities of these models.
    *   The insights into prompting strategies, feature engineering, and the limitations of LMMs contribute to the field's understanding of how to apply LLMs to deception detection.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper could influence the design of future deception detection systems by highlighting the importance of careful prompt design and feature selection.
    *   The findings on LMMs could stimulate research into more effective methods for integrating multimodal cues.
    *   The analysis of LLM reasoning could contribute to the development of more transparent and explainable deception detection systems.

**Justification for Score:**

I am giving this paper a score of **6**.

*   **Rationale:** While the paper does not present groundbreaking novel techniques, its value lies in its rigorous evaluation of current LLMs and LMMs on a challenging task. It reveals valuable insights about their capabilities and limitations. The study provides a benchmark for future research and suggests directions for improving LLM-driven deception detection systems. The thorough analysis of multimodal cues and the limitations of current models is valuable. However, the LMM limitations do weigh down the rating. The score reflects the paper's contribution as a comprehensive empirical study that informs the field's understanding of LLMs for deception detection, without introducing major theoretical or algorithmic advances.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Improved Supervised Fine-Tuning for Large Language Models to Mitigate Catastrophic Forgetting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09428v1)
- **Authors**: Fei Ding, Baiqiao Wang
- **Abstract**: Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), while enhancing large language models(LLMs)' instruction-following capabilities and domain-specific task adaptability, often diminishes their general capabilities. Moreover, due to the inaccessibility of original pre-training data, catastrophic forgetting tends to be exacerbated when third-party practitioners implement SFT on open-sourced models. To address this challenge, we propose a novel, more cost-effective SFT method which could effectively reduce the risk of catastrophic forgetting without access to original SFT data. Our approach begins by reconstructing the likely SFT instruction distribution of the base model, followed by a multi-model screening process to select optimal data, which is then mixed with new data for SFT. Experimental results demonstrate that our method preserves generalization capabilities in general domains while improving task-specific performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of catastrophic forgetting in large language models (LLMs) during supervised fine-tuning (SFT), a critical issue exacerbated by the inaccessibility of original SFT datasets for many open-source models. The authors propose a novel, cost-effective rehearsal-based method. The core idea involves reconstructing the likely instruction data distribution of the base model by analyzing its generative behavior, generating synthetic SFT data through multi-response generation, and then filtering for high-quality instruction-response pairs.  This reconstructed data is then mixed with domain-specific data for fine-tuning.  Experiments demonstrate that this method effectively reduces the decline in the LLM's general capabilities after fine-tuning compared to simply mixing third-party datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the approach of *reconstructing* the original instruction distribution of a base LLM *without* direct access to its training data. This is significant. Existing rehearsal-based methods usually assume access to training data or generated representations from earlier training stages (as in continual learning).  The multi-response generation and filtering process also adds a layer of innovation, optimizing the instruction-response pairs by mutual scoring across multiple models. This combination addresses a real-world constraint in LLM fine-tuning where original datasets are often proprietary.

*   **Significance:**  Catastrophic forgetting is a major roadblock in adapting LLMs for specific tasks without sacrificing their general knowledge and capabilities. The paper's approach offers a practical solution for mitigating this issue, particularly for practitioners using open-source models. Preserving generalization capabilities is paramount for broader LLM applicability across diverse domains. The experimental results, which show improvements over simply using third-party SFT datasets, support the significance of the proposed method.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practicality:** The method is relatively cost-effective, relying on the LLM's own generative capabilities to reconstruct the instruction distribution.
    *   **Addresses a Real-World Problem:**  The paper tackles the accessibility issue of original SFT datasets, making the solution applicable to a wider range of scenarios.
    *   **Multi-faceted approach:** The combination of distribution reconstruction and multi-response filtering is more effective than a single method.
    *   **Clear Experimental Validation:** The experiments provide evidence of the method's effectiveness in preserving general capabilities while improving task-specific performance.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The experiments primarily focus on a single base model (Llama-3-70B-Instruct) and medical domain fine-tuning.  Generalizability to other models and domains needs further investigation. The reliance on very capable LLMs like GPT-4 for scoring in the filtering stage may limit its accessibility for researchers or practitioners with limited resources.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The paper mentions a "controlled ratio" for mixing reconstructed and domain-specific data, but does not delve into the sensitivity of the method to this ratio or other hyperparameters.
    *   **Limited Ablation:** The ablation study focuses on the multi-response generation aspect, more analysis into the reconstruction aspect and the instruction types could have been done.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper's approach could influence how practitioners fine-tune LLMs, particularly those working with open-source models and limited resources. By addressing the problem of catastrophic forgetting, the work has the potential to increase the reusability and adaptability of LLMs across different applications.

**Overall Score:**

Given the novelty of reconstructing instruction distributions *without* direct data access, the practical relevance of the approach, its contribution to mitigating catastrophic forgetting, and the supporting experimental evidence, but also acknowledging the limitations in scope and hyperparameter analysis, the paper warrants a score of **7.5**.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Mitigating Spurious Correlations in LLMs via Causality-Aware Post-Training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09433v1)
- **Authors**: Shurui Gui, Shuiwang Ji
- **Abstract**: While large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in language modeling, recent studies reveal that they often fail on out-of-distribution (OOD) samples due to spurious correlations acquired during pre-training. Here, we aim to mitigate such spurious correlations through causality-aware post-training (CAPT). By decomposing a biased prediction into two unbiased steps, known as \textit{event estimation} and \textit{event intervention}, we reduce LLMs' pre-training biases without incurring additional fine-tuning biases, thus enhancing the model's generalization ability. Experiments on the formal causal inference benchmark CLadder and the logical reasoning dataset PrOntoQA show that 3B-scale language models fine-tuned with CAPT can outperform both traditional SFT and larger LLMs on in-distribution (ID) and OOD tasks using only 100 ID fine-tuning samples, demonstrating the effectiveness and sample efficiency of CAPT.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Mitigating Spurious Correlations in LLMs via Causality-Aware Post-Training" addresses the issue of Large Language Models (LLMs) struggling with out-of-distribution (OOD) samples due to spurious correlations learned during pre-training. The authors propose a Causality-Aware Post-Training (CAPT) method that decomposes the biased prediction process into two less biased steps: event estimation and event intervention, based on causal analysis.  The core idea is to isolate domain-specific event correlations from reasoning structure. Experiments on CLadder (causal reasoning) and PrOntoQA (logical reasoning) demonstrate that CAPT improves OOD generalization with relatively few fine-tuning samples.  The paper highlights CAPT's ability to mitigate both pre-training and fine-tuning biases.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the *application* of causal reasoning principles to *mitigate* spurious correlations in LLMs *specifically during the post-training phase*. While using causal models to analyze biases in LLMs is not entirely new, CAPT's specific decomposition into event estimation and intervention, coupled with a randomized intervention strategy, is a valuable contribution. The crucial aspect is the focus on event-level biases (beyond just entity biases) and how they are addressed through CAPT. Decomposition into event estimation and intervention, and the design for unbiased fine tuning is a novel angle as well.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from the pervasive problem of spurious correlations hindering LLM generalization. CAPT offers a lightweight and sample-efficient approach to improve OOD performance on challenging reasoning tasks. This has important implications for deploying LLMs in real-world scenarios where robustness to distributional shifts is critical. Showing that smaller LLMs fine-tuned with CAPT can outperform larger LLMs on OOD tasks is a significant result.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of spurious correlations and their impact on LLM reasoning.
    *   **Sound Theoretical Justification:** The proposed CAPT method is grounded in causal reasoning principles (SCM).
    *   **Empirical Validation:** Extensive experiments on two challenging benchmarks, including OOD settings and sample efficiency studies. The results convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of CAPT.
    *   **Lightweight Method:** CAPT is presented as a simple and efficient post-training technique.
    *   **Theoretical contribution:**  The paper provides a rigorous causal framework for understanding and addressing biases in LLMs, going beyond superficial observations.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Event Estimation:** The success of CAPT hinges on the accuracy of event estimation. The method's performance may degrade in domains where event boundaries are ambiguous or context-dependent, requiring more sophisticated event estimation techniques. The quality of event estimation is a potential bottleneck.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The experiments primarily focus on binary classification reasoning tasks. Further research is needed to extend CAPT to more complex tasks like generation or multi-hop reasoning.
    *   **Assumption about Large Pre-training Distribution:** The paper assumes that pre-trained LLMs are trained on sufficiently diverse data to dilute biases in event estimation. While reasonable, this assumption may not hold for all LLMs or specific event types.
    *   **Over-emphasis on simplicity:** While simplicity is a strength, there could be potential in exploring more intricate causal interventions.

*   **Potential Influence:** CAPT's framework could influence future research in several ways:
    *   **Causal Reasoning in LLMs:** Encourage further exploration of causal reasoning techniques for improving LLM robustness and generalization.
    *   **Bias Mitigation:** Provide a practical and effective method for mitigating spurious correlations.
    *   **Sample-Efficient Fine-Tuning:** Inspire new methods for fine-tuning LLMs with limited data.
    *   **Understanding LLM Biases:**  Provide a framework for analyzing and understanding pre-training and fine-tuning biases.

**Score Justification:**

Given the identified strengths and weaknesses, I assign the paper a score of **7**.

*   The problem addressed (spurious correlations) is significant.
*   The CAPT method offers a novel and theoretically sound approach to mitigate these correlations during post-training, a critical aspect often overlooked.
*   The empirical results demonstrate the effectiveness of CAPT, especially in OOD settings and with limited data.
*   The potential influence on future research is considerable.

However, the weaknesses related to the reliance on event estimation accuracy, limited scope, and assumptions about pre-training data prevent a higher score. The paper presents a significant advancement, but further research is necessary to address these limitations and fully realize its potential.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### GigaChat Family: Efficient Russian Language Modeling Through Mixture of Experts Architecture
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09440v1)
