### Understanding the Performance and Power of LLM Inferencing on Edge Accelerators
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09554v1)
- **Authors**: Mayank Arya, Yogesh Simmhan
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated exceptional benefits to a wide range of domains, for tasks as diverse as code generation and robot navigation. While LLMs are usually served from cloud data centers, mission-critical and privacy-sensitive applications may require local hosting of open LLM models. Given the large GPU memory footprint needed for LLMs, edge accelerators such as Nvidia Jetson Orin AGX with 64GB of shared GPU-CPU RAM are a compelling choice. However, the feasibility and performance of LLM inference on edge accelerators is under-explored. This study presents a detailed evaluation of LLM inference on the NVIDIA Jetson Orin AGX, on four SOTA models ranging from 2.7B to 32.8B parameters, such as Meta Llama3.1, Microsoft-Phi2, Deepseek-R1-Qwen.We investigate the impact of varying batch sizes, sequence lengths, and quantization levels on latency, throughput, and perplexity, and also explore various custom power modes on the Orin AGX to perform power and energy consumption analysis. Our findings offer interesting insights on the trade-offs between efficiency, inference speed and resource use, e.g., increasing the sequence length causes a decrease in token throughput and quantization causes smaller LLMs to be slower. These results can help optimize LLM serving on edge accelerators for practical applications.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary, critical evaluation, and novelty score for the paper.

**Summary:**

The paper "Understanding the Performance and Power of LLM Inferencing on Edge Accelerators" investigates the feasibility and performance of running Large Language Model (LLM) inference on edge devices, specifically the NVIDIA Jetson Orin AGX with 64GB RAM. The authors evaluate four state-of-the-art LLMs (Microsoft Phi-2, Meta Llama-3.1, Mistral Small, and DeepSeek-Qwen) with parameters ranging from 2.7B to 32.8B. They systematically analyze the impact of batch size, sequence length, and quantization on latency, throughput, perplexity, power, and energy consumption. They also explore the effect of different power modes on the Orin AGX. The paper presents detailed empirical results highlighting trade-offs between efficiency, inference speed, and resource utilization, offering insights for optimizing LLM serving on edge accelerators for practical applications.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Strengths:*

*   **Relevant and Timely Topic:**  The increasing demand for running LLMs on edge devices, especially for latency-sensitive and privacy-conscious applications, makes this work highly relevant.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper covers a wide range of LLMs and parameters (batch size, sequence length, quantization levels, power modes), making the study quite comprehensive. This allows for a more nuanced understanding of the performance trade-offs.
*   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper presents detailed performance metrics (latency, throughput, perplexity, power, energy) and analyses their interdependencies. The exploration of diverse power modes on the Orin AGX is a particularly valuable contribution, given the importance of energy efficiency on edge devices.
*   **Practical Insights:** The findings provide actionable insights for optimizing LLM serving on edge accelerators. For example, the observation that quantization can negatively impact latency for smaller models is important for practitioners.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and presents the results clearly using tables and figures.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Limited Novelty in Methodology:** The methodologies used in this paper (benchmarking, performance analysis varying different parameters) are fairly standard in computer systems research. The novelty lies more in the specific platform and models used.
*   **Specific to Orin AGX:** The study is heavily focused on the NVIDIA Jetson Orin AGX. While this allows for a detailed investigation, the generalizability of the results to other edge accelerators is not explicitly addressed. The paper does little to compare the AGX with other available platforms.
*   **Accuracy vs. Inference Speed Trade-off:**  While the paper mentions the perplexity, a comprehensive analysis of the accuracy degradation with different quantization levels beyond perplexity (e.g., using task-specific evaluation metrics) is missing.
*   **Limited exploration of System Optimizations beyond power modes**: The paper only analyzes the impact of sequence length, batch size and quantisation levels. It does not perform an exploration into more advanced system level optimizations that could improve LLM performance such as caching and attention mechanism optimization.
*   **Lack of comparative benchmarking**: The paper lacks any comparision to other similar edge devices. This means that is it difficult to assess the performance of the AGX against other similar platforms.

*Novelty and Significance:*

The paper's primary contribution is the comprehensive empirical evaluation of LLM inference on the NVIDIA Jetson Orin AGX. While the individual techniques (quantization, batching, varying sequence length) are well-known, the systematic application and analysis of their combined effects on a specific edge platform with state-of-the-art LLMs is novel. It provides useful data and insights that are not readily available elsewhere. This helps to understand the feasibility of LLM inferencing and the practical problems on edge accelerators.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper provides a valuable empirical study of LLM inference on edge accelerators. However, the methodology is not groundbreaking and the generalizability is limited. While the comprehensive analysis is useful, a deeper dive into the accuracy-performance trade-off and more advanced system optimizations would have significantly increased the impact. The exclusive focus on one specific hardware also hinders broader application. I am assigning a score of 6. The paper is useful and timely, but has some limitations to overall novelty and comparative performance to justify a higher score.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.DC
- **Score**: 6/10

### AD^2-Bench: A Hierarchical CoT Benchmark for MLLM in Autonomous Driving under Adverse Conditions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09557v1)
- **Authors**: Zhaoyang Wei, Chenhui Qiang, Bowen Jiang, Xumeng Han, Xuehui Yu, Zhenjun Han
- **Abstract**: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning has emerged as a powerful approach to enhance the structured, multi-step decision-making capabilities of Multi-Modal Large Models (MLLMs), is particularly crucial for autonomous driving with adverse weather conditions and complex traffic environments. However, existing benchmarks have largely overlooked the need for rigorous evaluation of CoT processes in these specific and challenging scenarios. To address this critical gap, we introduce AD^2-Bench, the first Chain-of-Thought benchmark specifically designed for autonomous driving with adverse weather and complex scenes. AD^2-Bench is meticulously constructed to fulfill three key criteria: comprehensive data coverage across diverse adverse environments, fine-grained annotations that support multi-step reasoning, and a dedicated evaluation framework tailored for assessing CoT performance. The core contribution of AD^2-Bench is its extensive collection of over 5.4k high-quality, manually annotated CoT instances. Each intermediate reasoning step in these annotations is treated as an atomic unit with explicit ground truth, enabling unprecedented fine-grained analysis of MLLMs' inferential processes under text-level, point-level, and region-level visual prompts. Our comprehensive evaluation of state-of-the-art MLLMs on AD^2-Bench reveals accuracy below 60%, highlighting the benchmark's difficulty and the need to advance robust, interpretable end-to-end autonomous driving systems. AD^2-Bench thus provides a standardized evaluation platform, driving research forward by improving MLLMs' reasoning in autonomous driving, making it an invaluable resource.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AD2-Bench, a novel benchmark designed to evaluate the Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning capabilities of Multi-Modal Large Language Models (MLLMs) specifically in the context of autonomous driving under adverse conditions and complex scenarios.  Existing benchmarks are criticized for lacking comprehensive data coverage of challenging environments, insufficient annotation quality for multi-step reasoning, and inadequate evaluation paradigms that fully assess CoT performance. AD2-Bench addresses these shortcomings by providing a large-scale dataset (~10k real-world images), detailed manual annotations (including over 5.4k CoT instances with atomic-level ground truth), and a tailored evaluation framework. A key contribution is the novel CoT pipeline and accompanying multi-dimensional metrics that assess the perception-understanding-reasoning hierarchy. The paper evaluates several state-of-the-art MLLMs on AD2-Bench, revealing accuracy limitations and highlighting the need for improved reasoning capabilities in these models for robust autonomous driving. The benchmark features region, text and point level visual prompts which the models can leverage to improve their reasoning performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates considerable novelty. The creation of a large-scale, manually annotated CoT benchmark specifically for autonomous driving in adverse conditions is a significant contribution. The atomic annotation strategy and the associated multi-dimensional evaluation metrics also represent a novel approach to assessing MLLM reasoning fidelity. The inclusion of diverse visual prompts is also a novel inclusion in driving datasets.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial gap in the evaluation of MLLMs for autonomous driving.  The ability to reason effectively in complex and adverse conditions is paramount for safety-critical applications like self-driving cars.  By exposing the limitations of current MLLMs on AD2-Bench (accuracy below 60%), the paper underscores the urgent need for further research in this area and provides a standardized platform for future advancements.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Dataset Scale and Quality:** The size and quality of the AD2-Bench dataset, especially the manually annotated CoT instances, are major strengths.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation Framework:** The proposed evaluation metrics offer a more nuanced and interpretable assessment of MLLM reasoning than traditional accuracy measures. The proposed multi-dimensional metric captures semantic similarity, reasoning coherence, and decision consistency.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The benchmark directly addresses the challenges of autonomous driving in real-world scenarios.
    * Visual prompts assist LLMs in improving their reasoning accuracy.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Compute limitations and error bars:** The paper reports results only as a single value for accuracy without any measure of certainty, therefore making it hard to claim statistical significance of the results.
    *   **Broader Impact Discussion:** While the paper focuses on the technical aspects of the benchmark, a more in-depth discussion of the broader societal implications of MLLMs in autonomous driving (both positive and negative) would have strengthened the paper.
    *   **Limited Diversity and Generalisability:** There is recognition that the endless combinations of edge-cases in the environment will make it almost impossible for all the combinations to be present in the dataset. It is not clear that the dataset would generalize to similar adverse conditions such as fog conditions in snow or extreme dark-lighting.
    *   **No comparisons with humans:** While many LLMs, particularly the larger ones, are purported to perform close to human levels, there is no comparison made with human drivers and their performance in the situations provided in the dataset.
*   **Potential Influence:** AD2-Bench has the potential to become a widely used benchmark in the autonomous driving and MLLM research communities, driving progress towards more robust and interpretable end-to-end systems. It can facilitate the development of MLLMs that can effectively handle adverse conditions and complex scenarios, improving the safety and reliability of autonomous vehicles.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and significant contribution in the form of AD2-Bench. The detailed annotation pipeline and tailored evaluation framework are noteworthy. The benchmark directly addresses a crucial challenge in autonomous driving. The limitations are relatively minor given the scope of the work. The paper would be further strengthened by a discussion of societal impacts and comparisons with human benchmarks, and claims of statistical significance in their numerical results. Nevertheless, its impact on the field is likely to be considerable.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Towards Open Foundation Language Model and Corpus for Macedonian: A Low-Resource Language
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09560v1)
- **Authors**: Stefan Krsteski, Matea Tashkovska, Borjan Sazdov, Hristijan Gjoreski, Branislav Gerazov
- **Abstract**: The increase in technological adoption worldwide comes with demands for novel tools to be used by the general population. Large Language Models (LLMs) provide a great opportunity in this respect, but their capabilities remain limited for low-resource languages, restricting applications in countries where such languages are spoken. We create several resources to facilitate the adoption of LLMs and to support research advancements for Macedonian. We collect the largest Macedonian corpus to date, consisting of 40GB of textual data and totaling 3.5B words. To support conversational applications, we collect a 106k-instance instruction dataset, carefully built to be culturally grounded. For evaluation, we construct a Macedonian evaluation suite covering seven benchmarks. Finally, we train domestic-yak, a state-of-the-art 8B-parameter model, on our curated datasets and evaluate it against eight baseline models using the newly constructed benchmark suite. Our model outperforms all existing models in the 8B parameter range across all benchmarks, and achieves performance comparable to models up to 10x larger. Furthermore, a qualitative analysis with native speakers reveals that our model is preferred over larger counterparts, receiving higher ratings for grammatical correctness and cultural appropriateness. All datasets, code, and model weights are openly released, setting a foundation for advancing LLMs in similarly underrepresented languages. These resources are publicly available at github.com/LVSTCK for source code, and at huggingface.co/LVSTCK for pretrained model weights and data.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the underrepresentation of low-resource languages, specifically Macedonian, in large language models (LLMs). It contributes a new Macedonian corpus (3.5B words), a culturally-grounded instruction tuning dataset (106k instances), a state-of-the-art 8B-parameter foundation language model called "domestic-yak" (pretrained and instruction-tuned variants), and a Macedonian evaluation suite. The authors demonstrate that their model outperforms existing models of comparable size and achieves performance comparable to models 10x larger on several benchmarks. Qualitative analysis using native speakers shows preference for "domestic-yak" over larger models due to better grammatical correctness and cultural appropriateness. All resources are openly released.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has good novelty. Constructing a substantial corpus, instruction dataset, and evaluation benchmark tailored to Macedonian is a significant contribution. While multilingual models exist, few efforts focus so comprehensively on a specific low-resource language. The approach of using the best available multilingual model (LLaMA3) as a starting point, then adapting it to Macedonian is appropriate. Combining web scraped data, converted documents, and synthetic data for instruction tuning is also a worthwhile approach.
*   **Significance:** The work has considerable significance for the Macedonian NLP community and provides a blueprint for other low-resource languages. Addressing the lack of high-quality data and evaluation benchmarks is crucial for advancing NLP capabilities. The open release of resources is commendable and will facilitate future research and development. The qualitative analysis adds valuable insights beyond standard benchmark scores.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Comprehensive resource creation: The authors didn't just train a model; they addressed the entire ecosystem needed for LLM development in a low-resource setting.
    *   Culturally-aware approach: Creating a culturally relevant instruction dataset is very important and distinguishes this from simple translation-based approaches.
    *   Demonstrated impact: The model's performance and the preference expressed by native speakers clearly show the effectiveness of their approach.
    *   Open access: The open release of data, code, and models promotes reproducibility and encourages further research.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Reliance on Serbian adaptation: While justified, the adaptation of a Serbian evaluation benchmark still introduces some bias and may not perfectly capture Macedonian nuances. The authors mitigate this with template based translations.
    *   Limited qualitative scope: While informative, the qualitative analysis with 35 participants is a small sample size. While qualitative feedback is expensive to acquire, some additional details on the participant selection process may be useful.
    *   Context length limitation: The paper acknowledges that the context length could be a limiting factor, but this isn't rigorously explored. Demonstrating performance on context-aware tasks would have been very valuable.

*   **Justification for score:** The paper presents a complete pipeline for language model creation for Macedonian, setting a clear path for the development of LLMs in other low-resource settings. While there is nothing paradigm-shifting regarding the architecture of the approach, the data collection and adaptation process is both necessary and thoughtful. As such, the score should be a 8/10, with emphasis on the careful construction of the corpus and demonstration that a targeted process can yield quality.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### From Symbolic to Neural and Back: Exploring Knowledge Graph-Large Language Model Synergies
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09566v1)
- **Authors**: Blaž Škrlj, Boshko Koloski, Senja Pollak, Nada Lavrač
- **Abstract**: Integrating structured knowledge from Knowledge Graphs (KGs) into Large Language Models (LLMs) enhances factual grounding and reasoning capabilities. This survey paper systematically examines the synergy between KGs and LLMs, categorizing existing approaches into two main groups: KG-enhanced LLMs, which improve reasoning, reduce hallucinations, and enable complex question answering; and LLM-augmented KGs, which facilitate KG construction, completion, and querying. Through comprehensive analysis, we identify critical gaps and highlight the mutual benefits of structured knowledge integration. Compared to existing surveys, our study uniquely emphasizes scalability, computational efficiency, and data quality. Finally, we propose future research directions, including neuro-symbolic integration, dynamic KG updating, data reliability, and ethical considerations, paving the way for intelligent systems capable of managing more complex real-world knowledge tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This survey paper explores the synergy between Knowledge Graphs (KGs) and Large Language Models (LLMs). It categorizes existing approaches into two main groups: KG-enhanced LLMs (where KGs improve LLM reasoning, reduce hallucinations, etc.) and LLM-augmented KGs (where LLMs aid in KG construction, completion, and querying). The paper analyzes these approaches, identifies critical gaps, and highlights the mutual benefits of integration.  It emphasizes scalability, computational efficiency, and data quality, and proposes future research directions, including neuro-symbolic integration, dynamic KG updating, data reliability, and ethical considerations. The paper provides a good overview of the field and directions for future research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Coverage:** The paper offers a relatively comprehensive overview of the KG-LLM integration landscape, covering various techniques and applications. It organizes the field into understandable categories.
    *   **Focus on Practical Considerations:** The emphasis on scalability, computational efficiency, and data quality differentiates it from some other surveys that may focus more on theoretical aspects. This makes the paper more valuable for practitioners.
    *   **Clear Structure and Organization:** The paper is well-structured and easy to follow, making it accessible to researchers and practitioners entering the field.
    *   **Future Directions:** The identified future research directions are insightful and relevant, highlighting key challenges and opportunities.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Novelty:** While the survey is comprehensive, it doesn't present fundamentally new insights or propose radically different perspectives on the KG-LLM relationship. It synthesizes existing knowledge rather than generating new knowledge.
    *   **Depth of Analysis:** While broad, the depth of analysis in specific areas can be limited.  The paper would be strengthened by more in-depth comparisons of different methods within each category, highlighting their specific trade-offs.
    *   **Lack of Quantitative Analysis:** The survey primarily relies on qualitative analysis.  Including some quantitative performance comparisons (e.g., based on benchmark datasets) would provide a more objective assessment of the different approaches.
    *   **Over-reliance on recent works**: Survey references a high number of works from 2023/2024.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies primarily in its ability to provide a structured overview of a rapidly evolving field. It helps researchers and practitioners understand the current state-of-the-art and identify promising directions for future work. However, its limited novelty and lack of quantitative analysis somewhat diminish its overall impact.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is a valuable survey that provides a solid introduction to the intersection of KGs and LLMs, especially because it's a quickly growing field. It identifies important gaps and suggests future research directions. However, it lacks significant novelty and could benefit from more in-depth analysis and quantitative comparisons. Therefore, a score of 7 is appropriate.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Integrating Quantized LLMs into Robotics Systems as Edge AI to Leverage their Natural Language Processing Capabilities
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09581v1)
- **Authors**: Miguel Á. González-Santamarta, Francisco J. Rodríguez-Lera, David Sobrín-Hidalgo, Ángel Manuel Guerrero-Higueras, Vicente MatellÁn-Olivera
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have experienced great advancements in the last year resulting in an increase of these models in several fields to face natural language tasks. The integration of these models in robotics can also help to improve several aspects such as human-robot interaction, navigation, planning and decision-making. Therefore, this paper introduces llama\_ros, a tool designed to integrate quantized Large Language Models (LLMs) into robotic systems using ROS 2. Leveraging llama.cpp, a highly optimized runtime engine, llama\_ros enables the efficient execution of quantized LLMs as edge artificial intelligence (AI) in robotics systems with resource-constrained environments, addressing the challenges of computational efficiency and memory limitations. By deploying quantized LLMs, llama\_ros empowers robots to leverage the natural language understanding and generation for enhanced decision-making and interaction which can be paired with prompt engineering, knowledge graphs, ontologies or other tools to improve the capabilities of autonomous robots. Additionally, this paper provides insights into some use cases of using llama\_ros for planning and explainability in robotics.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces *llama_ros*, a ROS 2-based tool designed to integrate quantized Large Language Models (LLMs) into robotics systems, enabling edge AI processing of natural language. By leveraging the *llama.cpp* runtime engine, the tool aims to facilitate efficient execution of LLMs in resource-constrained robotic platforms. The paper outlines the architecture of *llama_ros*, describing its components (Llama class, LlamaNode, LlamaClientNode) and the ROS 2 interfaces it exposes (response generation, tokenization, embeddings).  It also showcases potential applications of *llama_ros* in robotic scenarios, including planning within a cognitive architecture and enhancing explainability in social robots using Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG). Finally, it highlights the integration with LangChain for advanced prompting techniques and potential future developments, such as VLM integration and ontology use.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  While the individual components (LLMs, ROS 2, quantization) are not novel, the integration of *quantized* LLMs into ROS 2 for *edge AI* in robotics is a practical and potentially significant contribution. Prior works integrated LLMs into ROS, but often relied on remote access to LLMs. This paper directly tackles the resource constraint challenge. The use of *llama.cpp* is a key aspect, as it allows running LLMs locally. However, the level of *customization* beyond just wrapping *llama.cpp* and exposing it as ROS2 interfaces appears limited based on the paper's description. The integration with LangChain is a plus, but is an incremental novelty.

* **Significance:** The significance lies in enabling robots to perform more sophisticated natural language understanding and generation *locally*, without heavy reliance on cloud resources.  This opens the door to applications in environments with limited or no internet connectivity.  The use cases presented (planning and explainability) are relevant and demonstrate the potential benefits. The planning use-case, specifically, integrates a knowledge graph and uses RAG.  The explainability use-case highlights how to apply RAG to the robot's log data to generate explanations, which is a relevant contribution towards the development of XAR. However, the paper lacks extensive experimental results to *quantify* the benefits in terms of performance, resource usage, or accuracy.  It does not discuss the size (in terms of number of parameters) and quantization level of the LLMs that can be run. Therefore, it's difficult to assess the practical limitations (e.g., which robots can benefit?). The paper also fails to compare to existing tools and frameworks (e.g. CPU vs GPU performance with *llama.cpp*)

* **Strengths:**
    * Clearly articulates the problem of deploying LLMs in resource-constrained robotic systems.
    * Proposes a practical solution using *llama_ros* and *llama.cpp*.
    * Demonstrates the use cases and potential for planning and explainability.
    * Highlights the integration with existing libraries and frameworks (LangChain).
* **Weaknesses:**
    * Lack of quantitative experimental results demonstrating the performance and resource usage of *llama_ros*.
    * Limited discussion of the limitations and trade-offs associated with quantization.
    * Limited comparison to existing LLM+ROS integration frameworks.
    * Does not quantify the impact of the proposed pipeline for the planning example.
    * The novelty relies mostly on "putting the pieces together," without substantial architectural innovation of *llama_ros* itself.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence researchers and practitioners interested in integrating LLMs into robotics.  *llama_ros* could serve as a valuable starting point for developing more sophisticated AI-powered robotic systems, especially if more performance results are presented. The source code being publicly available is an advantage.

**Score:** 6/10

**Justification:**

The paper presents a useful and practical tool, *llama_ros*, for integrating quantized LLMs into robotics. The novelty is limited by the incremental nature of the contribution, which largely consists of combining existing technologies. The absence of thorough quantitative evaluation significantly weakens the paper and makes it difficult to assess the true impact of *llama_ros*.  The significance is potentially high, given the increasing interest in edge AI and natural language interfaces for robots. However, the paper falls short of truly demonstrating this significance with real data. Therefore, the score reflects its usefulness and potential, balanced against its lack of rigorous evaluation and architectural originality. The paper proposes a promising architecture but a rigorous evaluation with an experimental section is needed to justify a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 6/10

### ASTAGEN: Empirical Evaluation of Automated SATD Taxonomy Generation with LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09601v1)
- **Authors**: Sota Nakashima, Yuta Ishimoto, Masanari Kondo, Tao Xiao, Yasutaka Kamei
- **Abstract**: Technical debt refers to suboptimal code that degrades software quality. When developers intentionally introduce such debt, it is called self-admitted technical debt (SATD). Since SATD hinders maintenance, identifying its categories is key to uncovering quality issues. Traditionally, constructing such taxonomies requires manually inspecting SATD comments and surrounding code, which is time-consuming, labor-intensive, and often inconsistent due to annotator subjectivity. This study presents ASTAGEN, an initial step toward automating SATD taxonomy generation using large language models (LLMs). Given a comment and its surrounding code, ASTAGEN first generates a concise explanation for each SATD comment, then incrementally generates and updates categories to construct a taxonomy. We evaluate ASTAGEN on SATD datasets from three domains: quantum software, smart contracts, and machine learning. It successfully recovers domain-specific categories reported in prior work, such as Layer Configuration in machine learning. Compared to a naive use of an LLM, ASTAGEN produces more consistent category assignments due to its explanation-driven, iterative design. It also completes taxonomy generation in under two hours and for less than one USD, even on the largest dataset. These results suggest that while full automation remains challenging, ASTAGEN is able to support semi-automated taxonomy construction. Furthermore, our work opens up avenues for future work, such as automatic taxonomy generation in other areas.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ASTAGEN, a novel approach to automating the generation of Self-Admitted Technical Debt (SATD) taxonomies using Large Language Models (LLMs).  ASTAGEN works in two phases: first, it generates concise explanations for each SATD comment along with its surrounding code; second, it iteratively generates and updates categories based on these explanations. The approach is evaluated on three different domains: quantum software, smart contracts, and machine learning software, comparing its performance to both human-defined taxonomies and a naive LLM implementation. Results indicate that ASTAGEN can successfully generate domain-specific categories with greater consistency and efficiency (in terms of both time and cost) than manual or naive LLM-based methods. The paper concludes by suggesting practical use cases for ASTAGEN in semi-automated taxonomy construction.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of software engineering, specifically addressing the challenging and labor-intensive task of creating SATD taxonomies.

**Novelty:**

*   **Automating Taxonomy Generation:** Automating SATD taxonomy generation is a significant step forward. Prior research has focused on SATD detection and classification *within* existing taxonomies, but ASTAGEN tackles the more complex task of *creating* the taxonomies themselves. This is genuinely novel.
*   **Explanation-Driven Approach:** The two-phase design, where LLMs first generate explanations and then categorize based on those explanations, is a clever approach. It helps to address the limitations of LLMs related to context length. This method seems to increase consistency and accuracy, especially when compared to direct, naive application of LLMs.
*   **Iterative Refinement:** The iterative refinement process, inspired by human collaborative construction, is a thoughtful design choice. This approach allows for the integration of feedback and ensures that the taxonomy evolves to reflect the entire dataset.

**Significance:**

*   **Reduced Manual Effort:** The potential for ASTAGEN to reduce the manual effort required for taxonomy construction is considerable. The paper demonstrates significant reductions in both time and cost, making taxonomy generation accessible to a broader audience.
*   **Domain-Specific Taxonomies:** The ability of ASTAGEN to generate domain-specific taxonomies is particularly valuable. This allows for a more nuanced understanding of technical debt in different contexts.
*   **Improved Consistency and Reproducibility:** By automating the taxonomy generation process, ASTAGEN can potentially improve consistency and reproducibility. Manual taxonomy generation is often subjective and can vary depending on the annotators involved.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Absolute Performance Metrics:** The paper notes the lack of clear guidelines for interpreting the absolute values of precision and recall and recognizes that those values remain fairly low. While comparison to a naive baseline is useful, a greater understanding of what these values *mean* in the context of a “good” taxonomy would be useful. The reliance on relative improvement, while justifying ASTAGEN's design, can mask underlying limitations.
*   **Reliance on LLM Quality:** The performance of ASTAGEN is inherently dependent on the quality of the underlying LLM. Advances in LLMs will undoubtedly improve ASTAGEN's performance, but this dependence is a limitation. This also implies that the results might not be readily reproducible if a significantly different LLM becomes dominant.
*   **Evaluation Metrics Could be More Rigorous:** While the paper uses best-match precision and recall to address the challenges of comment assignment accuracy, these metrics may not fully capture semantic similarity or the utility of the generated categories. The paper admits that alignment metrics are generally a challenge for LLMs.
*   **Threats to Validity:** The authors acknowledge several threats to validity, which adds to the rigor of the paper. The construct validity concern related to the subjectivity of manually defined taxonomies is also important and should be mentioned.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field. The idea of automating SATD taxonomy generation is both innovative and practical. While the absolute performance metrics indicate areas for improvement, the comparison against the baseline and the reduction in manual effort demonstrate the potential value of the approach. The paper is well-written, presents a clear methodology, and acknowledges its limitations, and the authors have provided enough rationale for their arguments. The work has the potential to influence future research in automated software engineering analysis and has practical implications for improving software quality. The weaknesses noted are important for guiding future work, but do not detract significantly from the overall value of the paper.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 8/10

### Consistent Story Generation with Asymmetry Zigzag Sampling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09612v1)
- **Authors**: Mingxiao LI, mang ning, Marie-Francine Moens
- **Abstract**: Text-to-image generation models have made significant progress in producing high-quality images from textual descriptions, yet they continue to struggle with maintaining subject consistency across multiple images, a fundamental requirement for visual storytelling. Existing methods attempt to address this by either fine-tuning models on large-scale story visualization datasets, which is resource-intensive, or by using training-free techniques that share information across generations, which still yield limited success. In this paper, we introduce a novel training-free sampling strategy called Zigzag Sampling with Asymmetric Prompts and Visual Sharing to enhance subject consistency in visual story generation. Our approach proposes a zigzag sampling mechanism that alternates between asymmetric prompting to retain subject characteristics, while a visual sharing module transfers visual cues across generated images to %further enforce consistency. Experimental results, based on both quantitative metrics and qualitative evaluations, demonstrate that our method significantly outperforms previous approaches in generating coherent and consistent visual stories. The code is available at https://github.com/Mingxiao-Li/Asymmetry-Zigzag-StoryDiffusion.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Asymmetry Zigzag Sampling (AZS), a novel training-free sampling strategy to improve subject consistency in visual story generation using text-to-image diffusion models. AZS combines Zig Visual Sharing (ZVS), which injects subject-specific visual information, with Asymmetric Prompt Zigzag Inference (APZI), an asymmetric prompt schedule that uses complete prompts in some steps and null prompts in others.  The method strategically leverages visual sharing at the latent level, preserving prompt alignment.  Experiments on SDXL and FLUX models demonstrate that AZS outperforms existing methods in generating coherent and consistent visual stories, balancing subject consistency and textual fidelity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *asymmetric* zigzag sampling approach.  While zigzag sampling exists, the asymmetric application to prompts and the integration with a visual sharing module are novel. The specific way in which visual semantics are injected during the "zig" phase, and then the latent space is refined with a null prompt in the "zag" phase, is a key contribution. This avoids over-conditioning and maintains textual prompt alignment.

*   **Significance:**  The problem of subject consistency in visual story generation is significant. The method provides a training-free approach, which is practically important as it avoids the resource-intensive fine-tuning required by many competing methods. The results demonstrate improvements in subject consistency without sacrificing prompt alignment which is a significant step forward. The demonstration on two different model architectures, SDXL and FLUX, strengthens the claim of generalizability. However, the experiments are conducted on a limited set of prompts and subjects, which could affect the robustness of the results.
    The user study, while limited in size, strengthens the validity of the quantitative results and shows that AZS is favored by human evaluators in terms of combined image quality, consistency, and adherence to the prompt.
    While the method introduces a new approach, the improvements are incremental and the gains could be a result of several engineering choices combined, rather than the overall AZS framework. It would have been interesting to also explore combining visual sharing with other existing techniques to see if the visual sharing portion is responsible for the gains.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Training-free: Increases practicality and scalability.
    *   Strong experimental results: Outperforms baselines in key metrics.
    *   Generalizable: Works with different diffusion architectures.
    *   Addresses a significant problem: Subject consistency in visual storytelling.
    *   The method is well described and easy to implement.
    *   Open-source implementation: Increases reproducibility and future adoption.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Increased computational overhead compared to standard sampling.
    *   The prompts and subjects used for evaluation are somewhat limited.
    *   The ablation studies explore fewer combinations that would have given more insights into the different design choices.
    *   Improvements compared to baselines are good, but not revolutionary.
    *   The reliance on a single (strong) identity prompt could limit expressiveness and creativity beyond basic consistency.

*   **Potential Influence:** The method is likely to influence future research on controllable visual generation. The idea of strategically modulating semantic information (through visual sharing and prompt control) during the sampling process is valuable. The training-free aspect makes the method easily adoptable and combinable with other techniques. It serves as a new baseline for comparison and paves the way for more sophisticated techniques leveraging the zigzag sampling framework.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field. The Asymmetry Zigzag Sampling method is novel and addresses a key problem in visual storytelling. The experimental results are convincing, and the approach is well-motivated. However, the improvements, while notable, are not groundbreaking. A score of 7 reflects that the paper represents a significant advance with practical benefits but falls short of revolutionizing the field due to its incremental improvements and some identified limitations. The lack of deeper ablation studies and limited experimentation (prompts, subjects), prevents this from being rated higher.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Benchmarking Debiasing Methods for LLM-based Parameter Estimates
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09627v1)
- **Authors**: Nicolas Audinet de Pieuchon, Adel Daoud, Connor T. Jerzak, Moa Johansson, Richard Johansson
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) offer an inexpensive yet powerful way to annotate text, but are often inconsistent when compared with experts. These errors can bias downstream estimates of population parameters such as regression coefficients and causal effects. To mitigate this bias, researchers have developed debiasing methods such as Design-based Supervised Learning (DSL) and Prediction-Powered Inference (PPI), which promise valid estimation by combining LLM annotations with a limited number of expensive expert annotations. Although these methods produce consistent estimates under theoretical assumptions, it is unknown how they compare in finite samples of sizes encountered in applied research. We make two contributions: First, we study how each method's performance scales with the number of expert annotations, highlighting regimes where LLM bias or limited expert labels significantly affect results. Second, we compare DSL and PPI across a range of tasks, finding that although both achieve low bias with large datasets, DSL often outperforms PPI on bias reduction and empirical efficiency, but its performance is less consistent across datasets. Our findings indicate that there is a bias-variance tradeoff at the level of debiasing methods, calling for more research on developing metrics for quantifying their efficiency in finite samples.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper benchmarks two debiasing methods, Prediction-Powered Inference (PPI) and Design-based Supervised Learning (DSL), aimed at improving the accuracy of parameter estimates derived from Large Language Model (LLM) annotations.  These methods combine LLM annotations with a limited number of expert annotations to reduce bias.  The authors conduct experiments across four datasets and four LLM-model classes, varying the number of expert annotations and total sample sizes. Their findings suggest that while both PPI and DSL outperform simply using limited expert annotations, DSL often achieves better bias reduction and empirical efficiency, although its performance can be less consistent across different datasets. The paper also highlights a bias-variance tradeoff in the choice of debiasing methods and calls for further research into metrics that can quantify their efficiency in finite samples.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Direct Comparison:** The paper directly compares two prominent debiasing techniques (PPI and DSL) in a systematic way, filling a gap in the existing literature where these methods are often evaluated independently.
    * **Empirical Evaluation:**  The experimental design, using multiple datasets and varying sample sizes, provides valuable empirical insights into the performance of these methods under different conditions. This is particularly important since theoretical guarantees may not always translate perfectly to real-world scenarios with finite samples.
    * **Focus on Practical Considerations:** The research questions are relevant to practitioners who need to decide how to allocate resources between LLM-based annotation and expert annotation.
    * **Identification of a Bias-Variance Tradeoff:** The paper correctly highlights a bias-variance trade-off at the method level, implying that a single "best" method may not exist and that choosing the right method is situation-dependent.
    * **Thorough Methodology:** The paper meticulously outlines the datasets, evaluation metrics, model details, and prompt templates. The code availability further enhances reproducibility.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope of Estimators and Models:** The analysis is restricted to binary logistic regression.  While useful as a starting point, the findings might not generalize to other statistical estimators (e.g., survival models, causal inference models) or model types.
    * **Dataset Characteristics:**  The study focuses on datasets with relatively short texts.  The performance of debiasing methods may vary with longer documents or different text styles.
    * **Simplified Expert Annotations:** The paper assumes that expert annotations are ground truth. In reality, human annotations can also be noisy, unreliable, or subject to bias. This simplification limits the practical implications of the study.
    * **Inconsistent DSL Performance:** The paper reports that DSL performance is inconsistent across different datasets. The underlying reason for this is not clearly explained, which limits the generalizability and applicability of the results.
    * **Lack of Novel Methodological Contribution:** While the paper provides a valuable comparison of existing methods, it does not introduce new debiasing techniques. The contribution is primarily empirical.

* **Novelty and Significance:**

The paper has moderate novelty. While PPI and DSL are established techniques, the systematic comparison across different datasets and annotation procedures, and the focus on finite-sample behavior, constitute a worthwhile contribution. The study provides practical guidance for researchers and practitioners and identifies areas where further methodological improvements are needed.  The observations concerning dataset-dependent behavior of DSL are valuable and warrant future investigation.

**Justification:**

The paper is well-written, clearly organized, and addresses an important practical problem. The experimental setup is thorough, and the results are presented clearly. While the scope of the study is somewhat limited, it provides valuable insights into the comparative performance of PPI and DSL in a real-world setting.  The call for more research into quantifying the efficiency of debiasing methods is also a relevant and important direction for future work. The identified inconsistencies in DSL's performance underscore the need for caution and further investigation. However, the lack of novel methodological contribution or significant theoretical advancement prevents it from earning a higher score.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### In-Context Bias Propagation in LLM-Based Tabular Data Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09630v1)
- **Authors**: Pol G. Recasens, Alberto Gutierrez, Jordi Torres, Josep. Ll Berral, Anisa Halimi, Kieran Fraser
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used for synthetic tabular data generation through in-context learning (ICL), offering a practical solution for data augmentation in data scarce scenarios. While prior work has shown the potential of LLMs to improve downstream task performance through augmenting underrepresented groups, these benefits often assume access to a subset of unbiased in-context examples, representative of the real dataset. In real-world settings, however, data is frequently noisy and demographically skewed. In this paper, we systematically study how statistical biases within in-context examples propagate to the distribution of synthetic tabular data, showing that even mild in-context biases lead to global statistical distortions. We further introduce an adversarial scenario where a malicious contributor can inject bias into the synthetic dataset via a subset of in-context examples, ultimately compromising the fairness of downstream classifiers for a targeted and protected subgroup. Our findings demonstrate a new vulnerability associated with LLM-based data generation pipelines that rely on in-context prompts with in sensitive domains.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper investigates how statistical biases present in the in-context examples used for LLM-based tabular data generation propagate into the resulting synthetic data distribution. The authors demonstrate empirically that even small biases in the in-context examples can lead to significant distortions in the synthetic data. They also introduce a novel adversarial scenario where a malicious actor injects biased examples to influence the outcome for a targeted subgroup, potentially compromising fairness in downstream models. The paper highlights a previously under-explored vulnerability in LLM-based data generation pipelines that rely on in-context learning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper addresses a significant and timely gap in the literature. While previous works have explored LLMs for tabular data generation, they have largely overlooked the potential for in-context biases to propagate and distort the synthetic data. The introduction of an adversarial attack scenario is also a novel contribution, demonstrating a practical threat model. It advances beyond general fairness concerns with LLMs to a specific, actionable vulnerability in tabular data generation.

*   **Significance:** The findings have significant implications for the responsible development and deployment of LLM-based data augmentation techniques, especially in sensitive domains like healthcare and finance. The paper shows that the common assumption of unbiased in-context examples is often violated, leading to potentially unfair or inaccurate synthetic data. This has the potential to affect downstream model training with far-reaching consequences. The adversarial attack analysis provides a concrete example of how data generation systems may be vulnerable to malicious manipulation.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of in-context bias propagation and its impact on fairness.
    *   **Systematic Empirical Evaluation:**  The experiments are well-designed and executed, using multiple LLMs and datasets to demonstrate the robustness of the findings. Varying bias parameters helps establish causality.
    *   **Novel Adversarial Threat Model:** The formalization of a feature-aligned attack allows for exploring the conditions under which downstream bias is induced.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The paper highlights the importance of careful prompt engineering and monitoring in LLM-based data generation pipelines.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of Adversarial Attacks:** The paper focuses primarily on feature-aligned examples as an attack vector.  Exploring other types of adversarial manipulations, e.g., label swapping, might provide a more complete picture of the attack surface.
    *   **Reliance on Balanced Data Generation:** The forced balanced generation across subgroups limits generalizability. How do these biases manifest when combined with other natural imbalances?
    *   **Limited Consideration of Defenses:** While the paper identifies the problem, it offers minimal discussion of potential mitigation strategies or defenses against in-context bias propagation. Further research must be conducted into defenses such as prompt sanitization, debiasing techniques, and model retraining or fine-tuning.
    *   **Simplified Downstream Evaluation:** Only using Random Forest classifiers might not capture the nuances of the bias impact on different models.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in several areas:
    *   **Fairness in AI:** It raises awareness of a new source of bias in LLM-based systems.
    *   **Data Augmentation:** It calls for more careful consideration of the assumptions underlying data augmentation techniques.
    *   **Security of AI Systems:** It highlights a vulnerability that could be exploited by malicious actors.
    *   **Prompt Engineering:** It underscores the importance of crafting prompts that are representative and unbiased.

**Rigorous Rationale:**
The paper tackles an important problem related to fairness and security in AI, specifically in the context of tabular data generation using LLMs. It demonstrates a previously underexplored source of bias and introduces a relevant adversarial attack. The experiments are systematic and use a range of LLMs and datasets, enhancing credibility. Although the scope is limited in some aspects and mitigation strategies aren't detailed, the contributions are significant. It offers a valuable early analysis of a real-world problem, leading to concrete actionable recommendations.

- **Score**: 7/10

### Ties of Trust: a bowtie model to uncover trustor-trustee relationships in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09632v1)
- **Authors**: Eva Paraschou, Maria Michali, Sofia Yfantidou, Stelios Karamanidis, Stefanos Rafail Kalogeros, Athena Vakali
- **Abstract**: The rapid and unprecedented dominance of Artificial Intelligence (AI), particularly through Large Language Models (LLMs), has raised critical trust challenges in high-stakes domains like politics. Biased LLMs' decisions and misinformation undermine democratic processes, and existing trust models fail to address the intricacies of trust in LLMs. Currently, oversimplified, one-directional approaches have largely overlooked the many relationships between trustor (user) contextual factors (e.g. ideology, perceptions) and trustee (LLMs) systemic elements (e.g. scientists, tool's features). In this work, we introduce a bowtie model for holistically conceptualizing and formulating trust in LLMs, with a core component comprehensively exploring trust by tying its two sides, namely the trustor and the trustee, as well as their intricate relationships. We uncover these relationships within the proposed bowtie model and beyond to its sociotechnical ecosystem, through a mixed-methods explanatory study, that exploits a political discourse analysis tool (integrating ChatGPT), by exploring and responding to the next critical questions: 1) How do trustor's contextual factors influence trust-related actions? 2) How do these factors influence and interact with trustee systemic elements? 3) How does trust itself vary across trustee systemic elements? Our bowtie-based explanatory analysis reveals that past experiences and familiarity significantly shape trustor's trust-related actions; not all trustor contextual factors equally influence trustee systemic elements; and trustee's human-in-the-loop features enhance trust, while lack of transparency decreases it. Finally, this solid evidence is exploited to deliver recommendations, insights and pathways towards building robust trusting ecosystems in LLM-based solutions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the research paper:

**Summary**

The paper addresses the critical challenge of understanding and fostering trust in Large Language Models (LLMs), particularly in high-stakes domains like politics. It argues that existing trust models are oversimplified, often one-directional, and fail to capture the intricate relationships between trustors (users with their contextual factors) and trustees (LLMs with their systemic elements).  The authors propose a novel "bowtie model" as a framework for holistically conceptualizing and investigating trust in LLMs. This model considers trustor-side contextual factors (e.g., demographics, ideology, past experiences), trustee-side systemic elements (e.g., scientific discipline, scientists involved, product features), and a "core" component linking the two. The paper presents a mixed-methods explanatory study, including an interactive tool using ChatGPT, to explore trustor attitudes and uncover relationships within the bowtie model, addressing how contextual factors influence trust-related actions, how these factors interact with systemic elements, and how trust varies across these elements. Findings reveal the importance of past experiences, familiarity, human-in-the-loop features in shaping trust, and lack of transparency in diminishing it.  The paper concludes with recommendations for building robust, trusting ecosystems in LLM-based solutions.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its conceptual framework – the bowtie model – for understanding trust in LLMs.  While individual trust models exist, the comprehensive integration of trustor and trustee sides, and the emphasis on inter-relationships, is a significant contribution. The application of the bowtie framework within the context of LLMs and political discourse is also relatively novel. The identification of specific contextual factors and systemic elements relevant to LLM trust contributes to the field.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a relevant and urgent problem: the lack of public trust in AI systems, especially LLMs, and its potential impact on democratic processes. By offering a more nuanced model of trust, the study aims to improve the design and deployment of LLM-based solutions. The recommendations for building trustworthy LLM ecosystems are also significant, providing practical guidance to researchers, policymakers, and practitioners. The systematic approach and the provision of evidence are strengths.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Framework:** The bowtie model offers a structured and holistic approach to understanding trust.
    *   **Mixed-Methods Approach:** Combining quantitative and qualitative data provides a deeper understanding of the research questions. The use of a political discourse analysis tool provides a contextualised real-world setting for participants.
    *   **Practical Recommendations:** The paper offers concrete guidance for building more trustworthy LLM systems.
    *   **Clear Research Questions:** The RQs are well-defined and effectively addressed by the study.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Sample Size:** The sample size of 29 participants for the interactive activities and 10 for interviews is relatively small, potentially limiting the generalizability of the findings and statistical power. While the authors justify their sample sizes, increasing it would provide stronger evidence.
    *   **Specificity of Political Discourse:** The study's focus on political discourse in Greece limits the broad applicability of some findings. Cultural and political contexts are known to influence trust.
    *   **ChatGPT as a black box:** The study treats ChatGPT largely as a given or black box in its implementation.  A deeper investigation of the inner workings of ChatGPT (e.g., how its biases may influence topic identification in political speeches) would strengthen the trustee (LLM) side.
    *   **Correlation vs. Causation:**  While the study identifies correlations, establishing causation definitively is challenging with this methodology. Longitudinal studies or experiments could provide more conclusive evidence.
    *   **Overreliance on Self-Reporting:** The study relies heavily on participants' self-reported attitudes and perceptions, which are subject to biases.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research on trust in LLMs, encouraging more holistic models and empirical investigations. The bowtie model can serve as a valuable framework for analyzing trust in other AI applications. The recommendations can inform the development of more trustworthy and human-centered LLM systems.
    **Rigorous Rationale for the Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by proposing a new way to approach trust in LLMs, especially considering the complexity of the system. The chosen method of study and collection and analysis of data, both quantitative and qualitative, are logical and relevant. While the study has limitations and would benefit from a larger scale, this research effectively contributes to the emerging body of research on the topic and provides a useful framework that others can build upon. While the paper addresses a highly relevant and important issue with a sound framework, the limited sample size and the relatively constrained application domain detract from its overall significance. This does not undermine the intrinsic value of the work, and the insights derived, but limits the weightage given to the paper's findings and subsequent derived conclusions.

### HSENet: Hybrid Spatial Encoding Network for 3D Medical Vision-Language Understanding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09634v1)
- **Authors**: Yanzhao Shi, Xiaodan Zhang, Junzhong Ji, Haoning Jiang, Chengxin Zheng, Yinong Wang, Liangqiong Qu
- **Abstract**: Automated 3D CT diagnosis empowers clinicians to make timely, evidence-based decisions by enhancing diagnostic accuracy and workflow efficiency. While multimodal large language models (MLLMs) exhibit promising performance in visual-language understanding, existing methods mainly focus on 2D medical images, which fundamentally limits their ability to capture complex 3D anatomical structures. This limitation often leads to misinterpretation of subtle pathologies and causes diagnostic hallucinations. In this paper, we present Hybrid Spatial Encoding Network (HSENet), a framework that exploits enriched 3D medical visual cues by effective visual perception and projection for accurate and robust vision-language understanding. Specifically, HSENet employs dual-3D vision encoders to perceive both global volumetric contexts and fine-grained anatomical details, which are pre-trained by dual-stage alignment with diagnostic reports. Furthermore, we propose Spatial Packer, an efficient multimodal projector that condenses high-resolution 3D spatial regions into a compact set of informative visual tokens via centroid-based compression. By assigning spatial packers with dual-3D vision encoders, HSENet can seamlessly perceive and transfer hybrid visual representations to LLM's semantic space, facilitating accurate diagnostic text generation. Experimental results demonstrate that our method achieves state-of-the-art performance in 3D language-visual retrieval (39.85% of R@100, +5.96% gain), 3D medical report generation (24.01% of BLEU-4, +8.01% gain), and 3D visual question answering (73.60% of Major Class Accuracy, +1.99% gain), confirming its effectiveness. Our code is available at https://github.com/YanzhaoShi/HSENet.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "HSENet: Hybrid Spatial Encoding Network for 3D Medical Vision-Language Understanding":

**Summary:**

This paper introduces HSENet, a novel hybrid spatial encoding network designed to improve vision-language understanding in 3D medical imaging, specifically computed tomography (CT) scans. The key idea is to address the limitations of existing methods that primarily focus on 2D medical images, which cannot fully capture the complex 3D anatomical structures. HSENet employs a dual-3D vision encoder architecture to perceive both global volumetric contexts and fine-grained anatomical details, pre-trained using a dual-stage alignment with diagnostic reports. It also includes a "Spatial Packer," a multimodal projector that condenses high-resolution 3D spatial regions into a compact set of visual tokens using centroid-based compression. The authors argue that this approach effectively transfers hybrid visual representations to a language model (LLM) for accurate diagnostic text generation. They present experimental results across 3D language-visual retrieval, medical report generation, and visual question answering, demonstrating state-of-the-art performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel components, the dual-3D vision encoder architecture and the Spatial Packer with Voxel2Point Cross-Attention. This hybrid approach is a clear attempt to overcome the limitations of prior methods by explicitly encoding both global and local spatial information in 3D medical volumes. Pre-training of the model in a dual-stage process to further align the feature representations with diagnostic language is also a nice contribution. The approach of compressing 3D information into a smaller token size to work with LLMs while preserving spatial information is valuable.
*   **Significance:** The potential impact of this work is significant. Improved automated 3D CT diagnosis can enhance clinical decision-making by improving diagnostic accuracy and workflow efficiency. The state-of-the-art results on the tasks considered suggest a real advancement in the field. Successfully bridging the gap between 3D visual information and language models opens the door for more sophisticated diagnostic tools. The rigorous evaluation of the model over medical imaging tasks is a positive aspect.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The dual-encoder architecture effectively captures both global and local spatial information.
    *   The Spatial Packer provides an efficient way to project 3D visual representations into the LLM's semantic space.
    *   The pretraining strategy is well-motivated and enhances the model's ability to understand medical reports.
    *   Experimental results are comprehensive and demonstrate state-of-the-art performance.
    *   The paper addresses an important limitation of existing methods and offers a practical solution.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper is technically sound and its methods are novel; however, the performance improvement, while being statistically significant, may not be high enough in a clinical setting for a true change of practice.
    *   The reliance on expert-written reports for pre-training may introduce biases present in those reports. While the dataset size is reasonable, it's still relatively limited in the medical domain, and the model's generalizability to unseen pathologies or variations may be a concern.
    *   While the paper qualitatively analyses the reports, a deep analysis on model failure and cases of diagnostic hallucinations in a more complex setting is missing.
    *   The clinical utility must be demonstrated through a simulated reader study or similar before this is ready for prime time.

*   **Potential Influence:** The HSENet framework offers a valuable contribution that pushes the field forward. Other researchers can build upon the ideas presented in this paper, potentially developing even more sophisticated methods for 3D medical vision-language understanding. The methods described in this paper may prove to be adaptable to other 3D imaging modalities beyond CT, and that might find use outside the medical imaging community.

- **Score**: 8/10

### DGAE: Diffusion-Guided Autoencoder for Efficient Latent Representation Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09644v1)
- **Authors**: Dongxu Liu, Yuang Peng, Haomiao Tang, Yuwei Chen, Chunrui Han, Zheng Ge, Daxin Jiang, Mingxue Liao
- **Abstract**: Autoencoders empower state-of-the-art image and video generative models by compressing pixels into a latent space through visual tokenization. Although recent advances have alleviated the performance degradation of autoencoders under high compression ratios, addressing the training instability caused by GAN remains an open challenge. While improving spatial compression, we also aim to minimize the latent space dimensionality, enabling more efficient and compact representations. To tackle these challenges, we focus on improving the decoder's expressiveness. Concretely, we propose DGAE, which employs a diffusion model to guide the decoder in recovering informative signals that are not fully decoded from the latent representation. With this design, DGAE effectively mitigates the performance degradation under high spatial compression rates. At the same time, DGAE achieves state-of-the-art performance with a 2x smaller latent space. When integrated with Diffusion Models, DGAE demonstrates competitive performance on image generation for ImageNet-1K and shows that this compact latent representation facilitates faster convergence of the diffusion model.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the DGAE paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DGAE (Diffusion-Guided Autoencoder), a novel autoencoder architecture designed for efficient latent representation learning, primarily targeting the needs of latent diffusion models (LDMs). DGAE addresses two key challenges: performance degradation under high compression ratios and training instability associated with GAN-based autoencoders. DGAE utilizes a diffusion model to guide the decoder in recovering informative signals that are not fully decoded from the latent representation.  The core idea is to leverage the data modeling power of diffusion models within the decoder to improve reconstruction, even with a smaller latent space. Experiments demonstrate that DGAE achieves state-of-the-art performance with a smaller latent space than existing methods (like SD-VAE), facilitates faster convergence of diffusion models when trained on the DGAE latent space, and exhibits more stable training dynamics compared to GAN-guided VAEs. The paper provides empirical evidence that the decoder plays a more critical role than the encoder in maintaining reconstruction quality under high compression.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in using a diffusion model *specifically* to guide the *decoder* of an autoencoder.  While diffusion models have become ubiquitous, their incorporation directly into the autoencoder decoding process, especially with the explicit goal of improving reconstruction quality and reducing latent space dimensionality, is a significant contribution. The empirical observation that scaling decoder capacity has a more significant impact than scaling encoder capacity under high compression is also valuable.

*   **Significance:** The paper has strong potential significance within the field of generative modeling and LDMs. A smaller, more expressive latent space translates directly into reduced computational costs for both training and inference of LDMs. The more stable training dynamics are also valuable, addressing a common pain point with GAN-based VAEs. Furthermore, faster convergence when training diffusion models on the DGAE latent space is a major benefit. The demonstrated competitive performance on ImageNet-1K further strengthens the significance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the challenges of maintaining reconstruction quality and training stability in autoencoders, especially under high compression.
    *   **Well-Motivated Approach:** The choice of using diffusion models to guide the decoder is well-motivated by their strong generative capabilities and stable training dynamics.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides thorough experimental results, including quantitative metrics (PSNR, SSIM, rFID, gFID, Precision, Recall) and qualitative visualizations, to support its claims. The ablation studies (scaling decoder capacity) are particularly insightful.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** DGAE consistently outperforms SD-VAE across various spatial compression ratios and latent sizes.
    *   **Practical Benefits:** The paper demonstrates real-world benefits, such as faster convergence of diffusion models trained on the DGAE latent space.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental improvement**: While the technique improves upon existing techniques in terms of the metrics provided, the idea is built upon other previous successful techniques.
    *   **Limited Ablation Studies:** While the paper includes a section on scaling decoder capacity, further ablation studies on different aspects of the diffusion guidance (e.g., noise schedules, guidance strengths) would be beneficial.
    *   **Reliance on Existing Architectures:** The paper leverages existing U-Net and DiT architectures. While this is understandable, a deeper exploration of DGAE-specific architectural innovations could further enhance its value.
    *   **Computational Cost:** While DGAE leads to a smaller latent space and potentially faster LDM convergence, the computational overhead of incorporating a diffusion model into the *decoder* needs to be carefully considered, especially for very large-scale models. The paper could benefit from a more detailed discussion of the computational trade-offs.

*   **Potential Impact:** DGAE has the potential to become a widely adopted autoencoder architecture for LDMs, enabling more efficient and stable training of high-resolution generative models. The insights about the importance of decoder capacity could also influence future autoencoder designs.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, a score of 8 is warranted. DGAE represents a substantial improvement over existing autoencoders for LDMs, offering a well-motivated and empirically validated approach to improve reconstruction quality, reduce latent space dimensionality, and enhance training stability. The paper addresses a relevant and important problem, provides strong evidence for its claims, and presents potentially high practical impact. Although the approach is built upon previous successful techniques and some aspects such as architectural innovations have room for improvement. Also, a lack of details on computational cost and diffusion guidance can also be highlighted.
